# NMR relaxation and pore size

> 214 SCA symposium papers in this topic, most central in the citation graph (PageRank) first. Topic keywords: nmr, relaxation, t2, magnetic resonance, pore, fluid, distributions, water, surface, field.

TL;DRs, summaries, keywords and method labels were written by Claude (Sonnet 5.5) from each paper's text and can contain errors; titles, authors and years come from the papers themselves. Check the PDF before relying on a detail. Index of all topics: https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/llms.txt

## Method for Correlating NMR Relaxometry and Mercury Injection Data

- ID: SCA1995-11, year: 1995
- Authors: D. Marschall, J. S. Gardner, D. Mardon, G. R. Coates
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, mercury injection capillary pressure, surface relaxivity, Swanson parameter, fractional flow, T2 cutoff
- Cited by 12 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1995/SCA1995-11.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1995-11)

NMR T2 distributions are matched to MICP data to get effective relaxivity, a fractional-flow immobile-water cutoff, and a relaxivity-scaled Swanson-type permeability predictor.

The paper correlates NMR T2 distributions with mercury injection capillary pressure data on 17 plugs (3 clean sandstones, 5 shaly sandstones, 7 limestones, 2 dolostones) covering 6.4–27 p.u. porosity and 0.078–4450 md permeability. Matching the distributions by cross-correlation gives effective relaxivities: limestones 1.0–3.2 µm/s, dolomites 4.6–5.4, sandstones 6.4–25, with no clear clean versus shaly sand difference. A new immobile-fluid definition uses fractional flow (Fw = 0.01) computed from the T2 distribution via Standing's relative permeability equations. It gives cutoffs from a few ms to several hundred ms, independent of relaxivity, and generally higher immobile saturation than a fixed 33 ms or 50 psi cutoff. An NMR analog of the Swanson parameter, max(BVNW·T2), shows three rock-type trends against permeability; multiplying by effective relaxivity collapses them to one trend as good as the MICP Swanson parameter, while porosity does not correlate with permeability.

## Core Analysis by Low Field NMR

- ID: SCA1994-04, year: 1994
- Authors: Christian Straley, Dan Rossini, Harold Vinegar, Pierre Tutunjian
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: low field NMR, T2 distribution, free fluid porosity, T2 cutoff, NMR permeability, D2O oil saturation
- Cited by 6 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1994/SCA1994-04.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1994-04)

Low field (about 2 MHz) NMR T2 measurements on cores give accurate porosity, pore-size distributions, free fluid, clay bound water, permeability, and oil saturation and viscosity.

Core NMR is traditionally done at high field, where internal gradients from paramagnetic minerals degrade T2 signals; this paper evaluates low field (1–2 MHz) T2 measurements to support NMR logging tools. At 85 MHz porosity was underestimated in shaly sands, correlating with Fe+Mn and CEC, while 1 and 2 MHz matched gravimetric porosity. 2 MHz porosity agreed with buoyancy porosity for 210 sandstones/diatomites (slope 0.98, SD 0.8 p.u.) and 71 carbonates (slope 0.97). T2 distributions resemble mercury injection pore-size distributions. T2 cutoffs of 33 ms (sandstone) and 92 ms (carbonate) predict free fluid from 100 psi centrifuge, and a 3 ms cutoff estimates clay bound water. Permeability k = 4.6 T2ML^2 φ^4 fits 110 sandstones, truncating vugs at 750 ms improves Clearfork dolomite estimates (error factor 4.8 to 2.6), and T2ML correlates inversely with oil viscosity (0.7–1000 cp). D2O soaking of native-state Belridge diatomite isolates oil, gives gas and oil saturation matching Dean-Stark, and allows in situ viscosity.

## A Practical Approach to Obtain 1st Drainage Capillary Pressure Curves from NMR Core and Log Data

- ID: SCA1999-24, year: 1999
- Authors: Y. Volokitin, W.J. Looyestijn, W.F.J. Slijkerman, J.P. Hofman
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, drainage capillary pressure, Hg-air injection, sandstone, entry pressure, hydrocarbon correction
- Cited by 5 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-24)

Converts NMR T2 distributions to Hg-air drainage capillary pressure curves in sandstones using a scaling constant, with a hydrocarbon correction; saturation errors are about 9-12 saturation units.

The study develops a method to construct 1st drainage capillary pressure curves from NMR T2 distributions for sandstones, for both core and log use. It uses 186 samples from the NMR sandstone catalogue with NMR and Hg-air data. The optimal scaling constant Kappa=Pc/T2^-1 is defined by minimizing RMS saturation error over 0-500 psi Hg. A universal Kappa of 3 psi·s gives about 12 saturation units average error, falling to 9 with local core calibration. A variable scaling improves the fit at high Pc, and entry pressure is predicted within a factor of 1.85. For partially saturated rocks, the Sw=1 spectrum is reconstructed from bound-water signal plus a model moveable-water peak positioned via a <T2>–Swi correlation, giving errors of 10.5 (8.5 with core calibration) saturation units.

## Static and Dynamic Measurements of Reservoir Heterogeneities in Carbonate Reservoirs

- ID: SCA2000-06, year: 2000
- Authors: Shameem Siddiqui, James Funk, Aon Khamees
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: CT scanning, NMR T2 distribution, Lorenz coefficient, Dykstra-Parsons coefficient, coefficient of variation, carbonate heterogeneity
- Cited by 6 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2000/SCA2000-06.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2000-06)

CT porosity distributions, NMR T2 and CT-monitored coreflooding of carbonate plugs are used to compute Lorenz and variation coefficients; porosity-based indicators miss saturation heterogeneity.

The study characterizes static and dynamic heterogeneity in Jurassic carbonate plugs from two wells using CT, NMR T2, profile permeametry, SEM and conventional core data. A three-plug composite from Well B underwent ambient-condition oilflood and waterflood at 5 cc/min with CT scanning and image subtraction at several pore volumes injected. Lorenz coefficients for Well A varied from 0.74 for horizontal plugs to 0.40 for whole core, with a Dykstra-Parsons coefficient of 0.91. At plug scale, CT+NMR and CT+profile permeability Lorenz coefficients agreed (0.49 vs 0.50 for plug #248), while the FZI-based value was lowest (0.21). Oil flooding revealed an unswept region in the first plug that was not predictable from CT porosity alone. The coefficient of variation of CT saturation was lowest at highest water saturation and highest at lowest water saturation.

## 1D and 2D NMR Core-Log Integration in Organic Shale

- ID: SCA2013-018, year: 2013
- Authors: Philip M. Singer, Erik Rylander, Tianmin Jiang, Ryan McLin, Richard E. Lewis, Steven M. Sinclair, Matador Resources
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 mapping, Eagle Ford shale, T2 cutoff calibration, tight rock analysis, MICP, pore-body size
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-018.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-018)

Log and core NMR in lower Eagle Ford shale are calibrated against TRA, MICP and SEM to derive T2 cutoffs, fluid typing, temperature response and pore sizes.

The paper calibrates 1D log-NMR and 2D core-NMR interpretation in oil-bearing lower Eagle Ford shale using ten core plugs, with TRA retort porosities, MICP and SEM as references. Centrifuging to air produced no fluid, so movable porosity was equated with gas-filled porosity, giving a T2 cutoff for movable fluid of 8.3 ms. The effective-porosity T2 cutoff was 2.9 ms from TRA and 2.4 ms from MICP, corresponding to pore-body diameters of 219, 76 and 63 nm. T1/T2 maps were partitioned into bound hydrocarbon, effective oil, clay-bound water and total water using calibrated cutoffs (core-NMR total porosity vs TRA R²=0.77). Heating from ambient to 100 °C raised T2 of effective oil about 3.2 times and revealed more bitumen signal. Matching NMR to SEM gave surface relaxivity of 4.4–6.6 µm/s, and an MICP-based permeability model gave ~160 nD against measured ~140 nD.

## NMR Chemical Shift Selective Imaging of Individual Fluids in Sandstone and Dolomite Cores

- ID: SCA1989-03, year: 1989
- Authors: James J. Dechter
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: chemical shift selective imaging, NMR pre-saturation, deuterated isopropanol, fluid-selective 3D reconstruction, Bentheim sandstone, Bakers dolomite
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1989/SCA1989-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1989-03)

A pre-saturation chemical shift NMR imaging method separately images oil and brine in dolomite and sandstone cores, and with deuterated isopropanol, three fluids.

Imaging individual fluids in cores by NMR is hampered by rock-induced line broadening and short T2. The authors applied a pre-saturation pulse sequence, a minor modification of multi-slice spin warp imaging, on a 1.5 T clinical scanner. They imaged Bakers dolomite and Bentheim sandstone (23% porosity, 1400 md) saturated with Soltrol-130 oil and brine, using 19 slices of 5 mm and about 0.5 mm in-plane resolution. Oil and water linewidths were about 180-200 Hz, so the signals remained resolvable and either one could be suppressed, giving water-selective and oil-selective images that were complementary. In sandstone, water lay at the core faces and in a central channel, with oil in a peripheral sheath. Injecting about 0.38 PV of deuterated isopropanol, imaged as the low-intensity region (about 40% of core volume), enabled three-fluid imaging and showed oil and water displaced from the inlet end. The method needs a homogeneous B1 field, and its quantitative use was still being evaluated.

## Magnetic Resonance Core Analysis at 0.3 T

- ID: SCA2014-010, year: 2014
- Authors: Jonathan Mitchell
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: low-field NMR, T1 relaxation, internal gradients, RARE imaging, 23Na NMR, saturation imaging
- Cited by 5 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-010.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-010)

A 0.3 T benchtop NMR system gives quantitative porosity via T1 (even in sandstones), millimetre-resolution saturation imaging, and sodium and fluorine detection for core analysis.

Standard 2 MHz NMR core analysis has poor SNR and cannot support multidimensional imaging, so the paper demonstrates a commercial 0.3 T (12.9 MHz 1H) benchtop spectrometer. Eight sandstone, limestone and chalk plugs were brine saturated and measured with CPMG T2 and saturation-decay T1. T1 porosity matched gravimetric brine mass in all rocks (error ±0.1 g), whereas T2 failed in sandstones (±1 g) because of internal gradients, though it worked in carbonates. 2D RARE images of Bentheimer and Portland gave mean porosities of 0.241 and 0.214, close to plug values, and revealed vugs and fossils in the limestone. 3D imaging showed residual oil of 0.23 after brine flooding Bentheimer, and 23Na profiles and 19F images demonstrated salinity monitoring and three-phase discrimination.

## Diffusion Effects on NMR Response of Oil & Water in Rock: Impact of Internal Gradients

- ID: SCA1999-16, year: 1999
- Authors: John L. Shafer, Duncan Mardon, John Gardner
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, internal magnetic field gradient, echo spacing diffusion, surface relaxivity, Vicksburg sandstone, core-to-log calibration
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-16.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-16)

Multi-echo-spacing NMR T2 on Vicksburg sandstones shows internal gradients (25–100 G/cm) are as large with base oil at Swi as with brine, and poorly predicted by bulk properties.

Internal magnetic field gradients affect NMR T2 response and core-to-log calibration, but their dependence on saturating fluid is unclear. The authors measured T2 at echo spacings of 0.32, 1.2 and 3.6 ms on 13 iron-rich Vicksburg sandstone plugs from two wells, at 100% brine, 50 psi porous-plate Swi, and Escaid base oil resaturated at Swi. Gradients were computed from the free-fluid T2 shift, using the slope between 0.32 and 1.2 ms, giving apparent gradients of about 25 to 100 Gauss/cm. Gradients for oil-saturated Swi samples were equal to or greater than for 100% brine, contrary to the expected small oil–water susceptibility contrast. Gradients did not correlate with median pore-throat diameter, only weakly with surface relaxivity, and iron content and magnetic susceptibility were poor predictors. In low-permeability samples, undersampling of fast decay at long echo spacing produced negative apparent gradients, and calibration at reservoir fluid saturation is recommended.

## Pore-Scale Simulation of NMR Response in Carbonates

- ID: SCA2008-30, year: 2008
- Authors: Olumide Talabi, Saif Alsayari, Martin A. Fernø, Haldis Riskedal, Arne Graue, Martin J Blunt
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: random walk NMR simulation, T2 distribution, pore network extraction, maximal ball algorithm, surface relaxivity, mercury injection capillary pressure
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-30.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-30)

Random-walk NMR simulations on micro-CT images and pore networks of sand packs, Bentheimer and carbonates show tuned networks reproduce sandstone T2 but only approximately match carbonates.

The paper simulates NMR magnetization decay with a random walk method on micro-CT images and pore networks, then inverts the decays to T2 distributions. Sand packs (LV60B, F42B) were imaged and networks extracted with a maximal ball algorithm; the simulations matched experiments with a surface relaxivity of 41 µm/s, although network T2 distributions were narrower than the image-based ones. A Berea network tuned to mercury injection capillary pressure for Bentheimer sandstone (relaxivity 9.3 µm/s) reproduced the measured decay and T2, validating the algorithm. For carbonate C1, the extracted network (coordination number 2.3) decayed faster than the micro-CT image. For Edwards limestone and Middle East carbonates (relaxivities 2.1–4.5 µm/s), tuned networks gave decays and T2 distributions that differed from experiment, attributed to geometry (pore sizes and surface areas) because relaxation is in the fast diffusion limit. The authors suggest adding independent data such as synchrotron micro-CT to improve carbonate networks.

## Absolute Pore Size Distributions from NMR

- ID: SCA2006-46, year: 2006
- Authors: G.H. Sørland, K. Djurhuus, J.R. Lien, A. Skauge
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR pore size distribution, surface-to-volume ratio, pulsed field gradient diffusion, surface relaxivity, CPMG T2, mercury intrusion
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-46.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-46)

Combining short-observation-time PFG diffusion (S/V) with CPMG T2 yields surface relaxivity and absolute NMR pore size distributions, compared against mercury intrusion on two sandstone plugs.

The paper addresses converting NMR T2 distributions into absolute pore size distributions without assuming surface relaxivity. Using a 12 MHz Maran DRX spectrometer with strong gradients and short eddy-current dead times, the authors measured time-dependent diffusion of brine at observation times down to about 1.5 ms in two sandstone plugs. Fitting the short-time expansion of Mitra et al. gives the average S/V, which combined with the average 1/T2 from CPMG gives the surface relaxivity. Extrapolation to zero observation time returned the bulk brine diffusion coefficient of (2.9 ± 0.1)×10^-9 m²/s, validating the short-time regime. For plug A the mean pore radius was 6.0 µm from NMR versus 2.0 µm from mercury intrusion, with a broader NMR distribution.

## Relationships Between Non-Wetting Phase Invasion and Magnetization Evolution in Connected Pore Systems as Revealed by Network Simulation

- ID: SCA2000-05, year: 2000
- Authors: D. Chang, M.A. Ioannidis, I. Chatzis, Porous Media
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, NMR T2 relaxation, diffusive pore coupling, primary drainage capillary pressure, pore-to-throat aspect ratio, percolation
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2000/SCA2000-05.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2000-05)

Pore network simulations of primary drainage and magnetization decay show when diffusive pore coupling matters and that the T2-to-Pc scaling factor gives an apparent pore-to-throat aspect ratio.

The paper uses cubic pore networks (15^3 pores, square throats, Weibull size distributions, 10 realizations) to link pore and throat size distributions to water distribution and NMR magnetization decay during primary drainage. Drainage is simulated by bond-correlated site percolation, with 6% of each invaded pore volume left as residual water in corners. Two models were studied: Model 1 with a narrow throat distribution overlapping the pores (459 mD) and Model 2 with a skewed throat distribution typical of sandstones (32.5 mD). Diffusive coupling is insignificant in Model 2 but clear in Model 1, where it narrows the decay spectrum, skews it to faster rates, and strengthens at low Sw. Scaling T2 distributions to drainage Pc curves gave a nearly constant k of about 4.7 for Model 1 (aspect ratio 2.2 vs actual 1.5) and a variable k for Model 2 (apparent aspect ratio 4.3 vs actual 2.7 at breakthrough), reflecting limited pore accessibility near the percolation threshold.

## An Integrated Pore-Scale Approach for the Simulation of Grain Morphology, Wettability, and Saturation-History Effects on Electrical Resistivity and NMR Measurements of Saturated Rocks

- ID: SCA2006-20, year: 2006
- Authors: Emmanuel Toumelin, Carlos Torres-Verdín, Sarath Devarajan, Boqin Sun
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: random-walk simulation, Archie saturation exponent, resistivity index hysteresis, D/T2 NMR maps, mixed-wet pendular rings, grain pack Delaunay tessellation
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-20)

Random-walk simulations on grain packs with explicit fluid geometries reproduce resistivity index hysteresis and D/T2 NMR maps, showing saturation history and wettability bias NMR interpretation.

The paper presents a pore-scale method using diffusion random walks in Delaunay-tessellated grain packs (Finney pack, solid and microporous grains) with fluid blobs, wetting films and pendular rings. Six drainage and imbibition cycles, in water-wet and altered oil-wet states, are simulated to compute formation factor, resistivity index and NMR magnetization decays, which are inverted into D/T2 maps. Formation factor and primary drainage resistivity index match published sandstone data and pore-network results. In water-wet rock, film-thickening imbibition gives Archie n from above 2 at low Sw down to 1 at high Sw, while pore-to-pore imbibition keeps n at 2.1. In oil-wet rock, n rises sharply below a critical Sw (40% for the 20-p.u. pack, 60% at 7 p.u., 72% for the microporous 22-p.u. pack), matching measurements. D/T2 maps with 1-cp oil show overlapping oil and water peaks that underestimate Sw by at least 10 saturation units; pore-size errors reach 300% and permeability errors tenfold in oil-wet cases.

## A Hydraulic (Flow) Unit Based Model for the Determination of Petrophysical Properties from NMR Relaxation Measurements

- ID: SCA1995-14, year: 1995
- Authors: Henry A. Ohen, Austin Ajufo, Federica Manni Curby
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T1 relaxation, hydraulic flow units, flow zone indicator, surface relaxivity, T1 cutoff, permeability prediction
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1995/SCA1995-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1995-14)

Links hydraulic flow units to NMR T1 relaxation to derive surface relaxivity, T1 cutoff, pore throat size and permeability, validated on 17 core plugs.

The paper links the hydraulic unit (flow zone indicator) concept to NMR relaxation so that wireline NMR can be calibrated to core data. Equations relating FZI, specific surface area, tortuosity, surface relaxivity and T1 are derived, along with a permeability model based on FZI and NMR bound-water saturation. Seventeen brine-saturated 1-inch plugs were measured by T1 inversion recovery at 1 MHz and compared with CMS-300 porosity/permeability, mercury injection, centrifuge capillary pressure and thin sections. NMR porosity matched core porosity closely, and NMR-based zonation reproduced the four hydraulic units from core data for all samples but one. Surface relaxivity varied from 0.005 to 0.054 µm/ms between the worst and best units, and a T1 cutoff of about 10 ms matched 400 psi centrifuge irreducible water, while 20 ms matched 50 psi. The regression-based permeability model matched measured permeability within the calibration database.

## Laboratory Techniques to Characterize NMR Diffusion in Carbonates

- ID: SCA2000-03, year: 2000
- Authors: James Funk, Shameem Siddiqui, Mohammed BaTaweel, Sultan Al-Faqeer
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR diffusion, T2 distribution, APEX mercury injection, CT pore volume distribution, saturation exponent, surface relaxivity
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2000/SCA2000-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2000-03)

Combines NMR T1/T2, CT, APEX mercury injection and resistivity tests on Cretaceous and Jurassic carbonates to characterize diffusion effects on T2 distributions.

NMR T2 responses in carbonates are hard to correlate with pore size because diffusion, saturation and wettability affect relaxation. About sixty Cretaceous samples and a set of Jurassic samples were measured with 2 MHz CPMG at varying echo spacings, alongside CT pore-volume distributions, APEX mercury injection and resistivity index tests. In Cretaceous lagoonal samples, the T2 peak at 126 ms matched the APEX pore body peak at 125 microns, while the T1/T2 ratio was about 1.2 in homogeneous samples but rose to 2.66 for long-time peaks in the heterogeneous sample tied to the CT high-porosity tail. Desaturated/saturated T2 peak ratios (1.7 to 9.3) were inversely related to saturation exponent, supporting surface-film desaturation. Whether the sample is in the fast diffusion regime depends on the choice of pore size and surface relaxivity (ratio 0.0005 versus 0.062). Jurassic two-phase tests showed qualitative wettability and echo-spacing effects that remain unresolved.

## An Investigation into the Effect of Clay Type, Volume and Distribution on NMR Measurements in Sandstones

- ID: SCA2001-29, year: 2001
- Authors: A.K. Moss, Applied Reservoir
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, clay-bound water, montmorillonite and illite, Coates and SDR permeability, synthetic sandstone, residual water saturation
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-29.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-29)

Synthetic sand packs with 0-15% montmorillonite or illite show how clay type and content shift NMR T2 spectra, residual water and permeability model fits.

The study examines how clay type and volume affect NMR T2 distributions in sandstones, using five synthetic sand packs: clean sand, 5, 10 and 15% montmorillonite, and 10% illite. Samples were consolidated under stress, measured for porosity and brine permeability at 2000 psi, then desaturated by continuous oil injection over about 30 days before T2 measurement at 2.2 MHz. Permeability fell from 289 mD (clean) to 1 mD (15% montmorillonite) and residual brine saturation rose from 0.20 to 0.64. Increasing montmorillonite shifted T2 to shorter times and added clay-bound water signal below 4 ms; the illite sample had a near-unimodal spectrum, no signal below 1 ms and a low residual saturation of 0.2. Tuned Coates and SDR equations matched the clean and montmorillonite samples but not the illite sample, and the synthetic trends were compared with reservoir sandstone analogues.

## NMR and Ultrasonic Studies in Core Plug Samples from Carbonates and Sandstones' Tight Formations

- ID: SCA2001-61, year: 2001
- Authors: Romero P
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 cut-off, ultrasonic P wave FFT, tight formations, microfractures, vuggy carbonates, irreducible water saturation
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-61.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-61)

Ultrasonic P-wave FFT spectra and low-field NMR T2 distributions on tight Venezuelan sandstone and carbonate plugs reveal fractures, grain size and vugs, and give T2 cut-offs of 6 and 66 ms.

Tight sandstone plugs (Orocual field, San Juan Medio) and complex-lithology carbonate plugs (Barinas field, Escandalosa O) were studied with 1 MHz ultrasonic P waves analysed by FFT and 2 MHz NMR T2 measurements at 100% and irreducible water saturation. P-wave spectra differ between fractured and non-fractured samples in frequency domain but not in time domain. In sandstones, T2 distributions follow grain size, and the best T2 cut-off from 23 samples was 6 ms rather than the usual 33 ms, which would underestimate free fluid volume. In carbonates, T2 maxima vary between depositional cycles (about 90 ms at 26% porosity with vugs versus about 12 ms at 11% porosity), and the average cut-off was about 66 ms versus 90-110 ms normally used. NMR porosity correlates with buoyancy porosity at 0.99 for sandstones and 0.98 for carbonates.

## Irreducible Fluid Saturation Determined by Pulsed Field Gradient NMR

- ID: SCA1996-20, year: 1996
- Authors: M. Appel
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: pulsed field gradient NMR, bound volume index, restricted diffusion, CPMG T2 cutoff, mercury porosimetry, surface relaxivity
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-20)

PFG NMR with a bi-exponential diffusion model gives bound volume index in sandstone cores without fluid displacement, broadly matching mercury porosimetry, centrifuge and CPMG NMR.

Bound and free fluid volumes in cores are usually found by centrifuge desaturation, mercury porosimetry or CPMG NMR with a T2 cutoff. This paper proposes pulsed field gradient (PFG) NMR, which measures molecular displacement and is insensitive to surface relaxivity, as a fast method needing no fluid exchange. Water-saturated sandstone plugs (about 2 cm by 1 cm) were measured at 7.05 T and on a low-field bench-top instrument. Echo attenuation over diffusion times of 25 to 600 ms was fitted with a two-component model, and the low-mobility fraction was taken as BVI. PFG BVI generally agreed with CPMG, mercury and air-brine centrifuge values, except for the Table #2 sample (PFG 20% versus CPMG 1.3% and centrifuge 8%). For Marsing sandstone the restricted fraction implied a maximum pore radius of about 7.9 µm, equivalent to a capillary pressure of 50 to 100 psi.

## Estimating Saturations in Organic Shales Using 2D NMR

- ID: SCA2015-024, year: 2015
- Authors: Benjamin NICOT, Nopparat VORAPALAWUT, Berengere ROUSSEAU, Luis F. MADARIAGA, Gerald HAMON
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: 2D T1-T2 NMR, organic shale, oil-water saturation, NMR dispersion (NMRD), thermogravimetric analysis, spontaneous imbibition
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-024.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-024)

2D T1-T2 NMR separates oil from water in Vaca Muerta shales via T1/T2 contrast, and fluid volumes agree with gravimetric and TGA measurements.

Saturation estimation in tight organic shales is biased by destructive thermal methods that prevent direct pore volume measurement. The authors used 2D T1-T2 NMR at 2.5 and 23 MHz on Vaca Muerta samples (oil-window shale A, wet-gas shale B) with spontaneous imbibition of brine, D2O and isopar L, plus fast-field-cycling NMRD. Water appears at T1/T2 ~1-2, while oil shows high T1/T2 (4-5 at 2.5 MHz, ~10 at 23 MHz), which is due to light oil confinement in organic pores rather than bitumen. Because oil T1/T2 grows with frequency while water stays constant, 23 MHz is recommended. NMR fluid volumes matched mass balance for imbibition and TGA for 15 shale B samples, with saturations computed using porosity from water-saturated twin samples.

## Alternative Methods for Determining Water Saturations in Core Plugs

- ID: SCA1990-20EURO, year: 1990
- Authors: Solfrid L. Tonstad, Helga Baardsen, Jon K. Ringen
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: water saturation determination, Karl Fischer titration, chloride titration, material balance, CT scanning, Dean-Stark extraction
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-20EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-20EURO)

Presents Karl Fischer, chloride titration, tracer, density, CT and GOR methods to verify core plug water saturation, finding Karl Fischer, chloride titration and CT accurate to ±0.02-0.03 Sw.

Material balance errors can corrupt all saturation values in a waterflood series on a core plug, so independent checks of Sw are needed. The authors describe six alternatives: Karl Fischer titration of solvent, chloride titration after miscible displacement by nitrate brine, oil volume by nonane tracer or by density difference, CT scanning, and a gas-oil-ratio method at reservoir conditions. Tests used Bentheim and Berea sandstone, sintered Al2O3 and reservoir sandstones (porosity 20-35%, permeability 30-8000 mD, near-neutral to strongly water-wet) and compared the methods with material balance, weighing and CT. Karl Fischer accuracy was about ±0.02 Sw and chloride titration about ±0.03 Sw. Material balance was often inaccurate because of hold-up volumes and emulsions, and CT deviated on heterogeneous plugs. Karl Fischer, chloride titration and CT are recommended, while the tracer and density methods need further work.

## Low-Field NMR Profiles for Verification of Oil and Water Saturations in Cores

- ID: SCA2006-31, year: 2006
- Authors: Christian Straley, Gabriela Leu
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR saturation profiling, centrifuge desaturation, Hassler-Brunner model, D2O oil saturation, 19F fluorinert, internal gradients
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-31)

Low-field 2 MHz NMR profiling, using D2O or fluorinated oil, gives quantitative saturation profiles of cores and shows centrifuging gives uniform saturation only at low water saturation.

The paper presents a low-field (2 MHz) NMR profiling method using a pulsed field gradient Hahn echo to map porosity and saturation along cores, with echo-time and probe-bandwidth corrections. For Berea, profile porosity agreed with buoyancy porosity within 0.3 porosity units after correcting for 6.4–12% echo losses mainly from internal gradients. Centrifuged Berea 500 plugs, unsleeved on glass beads, were compared with Hassler-Brunner and Korringa predictions: at 2.21 psi the profiles roughly matched HB, while at 4.77 psi saturation was below 25% and fairly uniform. Reversing core orientation gave uniform saturation only below about 50% water saturation. D2O replacement isolates oil, and FC-77 fluorinert with 19F detection gives complementary oil and water profiles, with 19F signal 53.4% of 1H, against a predicted 52.8%.

## Time-Domain Curve Fitting for Automated Oil Core NMR Spectral Analysis

- ID: SCA1990-25, year: 1990
- Authors: WA Edelstein, HJ Vinegar, PN Tutunjian, PB Roemer
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR free induction decay, time-domain fitting, 13C NMR, oil saturation, powder pattern, missing points problem
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-25.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-25)

Fitting a few Lorentzian, Gaussian or powder-pattern functions to the NMR free induction decay in the time domain automates oil/water quantification in cores despite missing initial data points.

Fourier transforms of NMR free induction decays with missing initial points (RF breakthrough, ring-down) give distorted baselines that need manual correction. The authors fit sums of Lorentzian, Gaussian and 13C carbonate powder-pattern functions to the time-domain data by minimizing chi-square with the IMSL global minimizer ZXMWD. For 1H oil/water in Indiana limestone, Bentheim sandstone and Bakers dolomite plugs, time-domain oil fractions were similar to Fourier-domain and mass-balance values, with fits averaging 78 s. For 13C of Wasson crude in whole cores, about 150 µs of initial data was discarded; Berea oil weight was estimated at 17.6 g (aliphatic) or 16.7 g (aliphatic+aromatic), versus 20.8 g by mass balance and 13.8 g by hand-processed Fourier spectra. Carbonate powder-pattern fits took about 5 minutes. Time-domain fitting extrapolates to time zero, but some short-T2 oil may still be missed.

## Use of NMMR Imaging For Determining Fluid Saturation Distributions During Multiphase Displacement in Porous Media

- ID: SCA1992-19, year: 1992
- Authors: Kyung-Hoe Kim, Songhua Chen, Fangfang Qin, A. Ted Watson
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, saturation profiles, transverse relaxation, bi-exponential model, porosity, limestone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-19.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-19)

NMR profile imaging with multi-echo extrapolation to zero echo time and statistically selected bi-exponential T2 models gives accurate porosity and saturation distributions in limestones.

Quantifying saturation and porosity from NMR profile images is hampered by fast transverse relaxation that varies with position and saturation. The authors acquire profiles at 12-15 echo times on a 2-T imager and extrapolate pixel-by-pixel to TE=0, using single-, multi-, or stretched-exponential models with an F-test choosing the number of components. Experiments used epoxy-mounted limestone cores (Indiana and two building limestones) with n-octadecene and D2O, plus an agarose/CuSO4 reference. In a building limestone, average T2 fell from 13.9 ms at full saturation to 9 ms after 21 PV of D2O, so a fixed T2 gives sizeable saturation errors. A bi-exponential model was selected in all cases; for Indiana limestone it gave 17.7% porosity versus 16.9% gravimetric, while single- and stretched-exponential models erred by 25% and 26%. Pixel-wise bi-exponential porosity profiles averaged within 3% of gravimetric values.

## Studies of Rock Structures Using NMR Restricted Diffusion Measurements and Analyses

- ID: SCA1993-13, year: 1993
- Authors: Songhua Chen, Jinli Qiao, Hsie-Keng Liaw, A. Ted Watson
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: pulsed field gradient NMR, restricted diffusion, bipolar gradient stimulated echo, permeability estimation, pore size distribution, internal field gradients
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-13)

PFG NMR restricted-diffusion measurements with bipolar-gradient stimulated echoes on sandstones and limestones detect permeability differences and track wetting-phase pore-size distributions during drainage.

The paper uses pulsed field gradient NMR to probe pore structure, permeability and fluid distribution in ten sandstone and carbonate samples (Bentheimer, Brown, Berea, Texas Cream limestone, dolomite). Four-bipolar-gradient stimulated echo sequences suppress internal field gradients, and differences between the two sequence variants were within noise even for heterogeneous Brown sandstone. A new expression for echo attenuation with time-dependent diffusivity is derived. A characteristic diffusion time Δk, combined with porosity, gives permeability ratios: Bentheimer/Berea was 2.0 versus 2.4 from reported values, and the five Berea samples agreed reasonably with provider permeabilities. Long-time diffusion data fitted with a Gaussian pore-size distribution gave a mean pore size of about 60 µm for Texas Cream limestone, consistent with thin sections (60–100 µm), and the water-phase distribution shifted to smaller sizes as saturation fell during drainage.

## Improving the Accuracy of NMR Relaxation Distribution Analysis in Clay-Rich Reservoirs and Core Samples

- ID: SCA1997-02, year: 1997
- Authors: Songhua Chen, Daniel T. Georgi
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, clay bound water, effective porosity, echo spacing, CPMG inversion, signal-to-noise ratio
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-02.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-02)

Synthetic CPMG data show clay-bound water leaks into NMR effective porosity depending on noise and TE; a time-domain subtraction using 0.6 ms echoes corrects it.

NMR effective porosity from CPMG data can be overestimated in clay-rich rocks because clay-bound water (CBW) signal leaks into effective porosity bins. Using synthetic echo trains with Gaussian noise (1 p.u.) and SVD inversion, the authors examine the effects of T2 bin range, noise and echo spacing TE (0.6, 0.9, 1.2 ms). With 12 p.u. CBW and 12 p.u. effective porosity, a 9-bin fit gave PHIE of 16.27 p.u., and 11 bins reduced but did not eliminate the leakage. Without noise, components down to about TE/7 can be resolved if the bin range is broad enough, but noise degrades short T2 components, so effective porosity depends on TE. A correction subtracts the CBW decay, reconstructed from a high-SNR 0.6 ms echo train, from the first 12 echoes of the 1.2 ms train before inversion. In simulations (500 sets, SNR=60), CBW was estimated within ±10% at 80% confidence.

## NMR Studies of an Iron-Rich Sandstone Oil Reservoir

- ID: SCA1998-21, year: 1998
- Authors: Håkon Rueslåtten, Terje Eidesmo, Carsten Slot-Petersen, Jim White
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1/T2 ratio, chlorite, glauconite, BVI T2 cut-off, CMR log calibration, internal magnetic gradients
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-21)

Lab NMR on twelve plugs from a chlorite- and glauconite-rich North Sea sandstone shows chlorite, not glauconite, shortens T2, raises T1/T2 and biases CMR porosity and BVI cut-offs.

CMR logs and core calibration were used in a North Sea sandstone with abundant glauconite (18-38 vol%) and chlorite (4-26 wt%), where porosity is 25-35 PU but permeability is poor. Twelve twin seal-peel plugs were measured with T1 and T2 NMR at 2.04 MHz when water saturated and at Swirr with decane, along with Karl Fischer titration, petrography and standard petrophysics. T1/T2 ratios for decane-saturated plugs at Swirr ranged from 2.0 to 10.9 and correlated with chlorite content, which the authors attribute to chlorite-induced internal magnetic gradients that shorten T2 but not T1. Porosity underestimation by CMR (2-15 PU) and the lab NMR-helium porosity difference (up to about 7.5 PU) increased with chlorite but not glauconite. A 33 ms T2 cut-off mismatched titrated Swirr in chlorite-rich plugs (effective cut-offs 3.3-10.5 ms), while a 150 ms T1 cut-off matched. Even so, 33 ms gave the best Timur/Coates permeability estimates.

## NMR Surface Relaxivity and Diffusion Effects in Grain Packs

- ID: SCA1999-20, year: 1999
- Authors: S. Godefroy, J.-P. Korb, D. Petit, M. Fleury
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: surface relaxivity, NMR T2 relaxation, silicon carbide grain packs, paramagnetic impurities, temperature dependence, surface-limited regime
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-20)

Low-field NMR on acid-cleaned and uncleaned SiC grain packs shows surface-limited relaxation with rock-like relaxivity when clean, and T2 decreasing with temperature, attributed to surface proton diffusion.

Surface relaxivity controls T2 interpretation but varies with mineralogy, impurities and temperature. The authors measured T1 and T2 at 2.2 MHz on water-saturated SiC grain packs (8-150 µm grains, about 44% porosity) from 34 to 85°C, before and after HCl cleaning to remove paramagnetic Fe and Mn. Uncleaned packs showed quadratic pore-size dependence (diffusion-limited, ρ2 ≥ 37 µm/s), while cleaned packs showed linear dependence (surface-limited), with ρ2 about 1.7 µm/s from BET (3.4 µm/s with a spherical shape factor), within the range of natural rocks. Internal gradients were small (3.2-19.6 G/cm) and negligible. After bulk subtraction, relaxation times decreased with increasing temperature, with activation energies of -2 to -2.8 kcal/mol, opposite to bulk water, interpreted as surface diffusion of proton species near paramagnetic sites as in silica glasses.

## In-Situ Viscosity Using Low Field NMR: A Field Case Study

- ID: SCA2005-31, year: 2005
- Authors: F. Hum, A. Kantzas, R. MacPherson, T. Hancsicsak
- Method: field or reservoir case study
- Keywords: low field NMR, heavy oil viscosity, T2 geometric mean, relative hydrogen index, oil sands, spectrum de-convolution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-31)

Low field NMR with a tuned bulk-oil model and spectral de-convolution predicts in-situ heavy oil viscosity in an Alberta oil sands field, with 33% average error.

Oil viscosity is key to heavy oil recovery, but extracting oil from core is costly and wellhead samples can be contaminated. Nineteen unconsolidated oil sand cores from three wells in an Athabasca field were measured by low field NMR (Corespec 1000) at 17°C. Oil was centrifuged out and its viscosity measured by cone-and-plate rheometer, then extrapolated to 17°C; viscosities ranged from 10,200 to 276,000 cP over 45 m and increased with depth. The bulk NMR model relating viscosity to T2gm and RHI was tuned (alpha 4073, beta 5.03), giving 12% average error. For in-situ prediction, the oil peak was de-convoluted from clay-bound water in the oil sand spectrum, and RHI was predicted from T2gm; this gave R² of 0.96 but 33% average error, adequate for viscosity ranges with depth.

## The Effects of Salt Type and Salinity on Formation Water Viscosity and NMR Responses

- ID: SCA2005-51, year: 2005
- Authors: Hyung Tae Kwak, Gigi Zhang, Songhua Chen
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: brine viscosity, structural temperature effect, Jones-Dole B coefficient, NMR T2 relaxation, brine diffusivity, kosmotrope chaotrope
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-51.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-51)

Measurements of viscosity, NMR T2 and diffusivity for LiCl, NaCl, KCl and CsCl brines show salt type matters; treating brines as NaCl can err by up to 100%.

Formation water viscosity is usually estimated as that of NaCl brine, which ignores the structural temperature effect of different ions. The authors measured kinematic viscosity (Cannon-Fenske viscometer), T2,LM and diffusivity (2 MHz NMR) of LiCl, NaCl, KCl and CsCl solutions up to about 335 kppm, at 30-70°C. LiCl and NaCl (kosmotropes) raise viscosity relative to water, while KCl and CsCl (chaotropes) lower it. At 30°C, treating 335 kppm KCl and CsCl as NaCl overestimates viscosity by about 70% and 100%. KCl switches role near 50°C, becoming more viscous than water above it, and T2,LM×η is not constant with salinity. Existing T2-viscosity and diffusivity-viscosity correlations can therefore err by up to 100% for non-NaCl brines.

## NMR Surface Relaxivity Determination Using NMR Apparent Diffusion Curves and Bet Measurements

- ID: SCA2007_35, year: 2007
- Authors: Marc Fleury
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: surface relaxivity, NMR diffusion, apparent diffusion curve, BET specific surface, V/S ratio, MICP throat size
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_35.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_35)

NMR apparent diffusion curves give V/S and surface relaxivity for grain packs and sandstones; BET substitutes for high-surface carbonates, and calibrated NMR sizes fall below MICP throat sizes.

Surface relaxivity requires an independent surface-to-volume (S/V) determination. The author fits the full NMR apparent diffusion curve (about 10 ms to 1 s, bipolar gradient sequence) with an interpolation function to extract V/S, then derives relaxivity from the surface-weighted T2. Validation on a BET reference alumina pack (0.1 m²/g) gave V/S of 2.2 µm versus 2.0 µm from BET, and ρ2 of 5.7 µm/s. SiC grain packs (8–110 µm) gave V/S = 0.053 dg and ρ2 of 1.2–3 µm/s, and Fontainebleau sandstones gave ρ2 of 3.5–11.7 µm/s, with V/S varying only about twofold while permeability varied four orders of magnitude. For carbonates, S/V is too high for the diffusion method (practical limit about 1 µm⁻¹), so BET was used; the calibrated V/S was at least a factor of 2–3 smaller than MICP throat size.

## NMR Monitoring of Spontaneous Brine Imbibition in Carbonates

- ID: SCA2008-13, year: 2008
- Authors: Haldis Riskedal, Lejla Tipura, James Howard, Arne Graue
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, D2O brine, spontaneous imbibition, Edwards Limestone, Amott-Harvey index, MRI saturation profiles
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-13)

NMR T2 monitoring of spontaneous imbibition in Edwards Limestone using H2O/D2O brines, MRI and slice gradients shows poor reproducibility attributed to rock heterogeneity.

The study monitors water saturation changes during spontaneous brine imbibition in Edwards Limestone plugs, whose complex pore geometry makes oil and water T2 signals overlap. Replacing H2O with D2O isolated the decane T2 contribution in repeated imbibition cycles on plugs HR28 and HR29. Oil T2 at residual water saturation decreased linearly with decreasing Amott-Harvey index across more than 20 plugs, similar to Portland chalk. Imbibition curves and endpoints were not reproducible between cycles, and oil T2 shifted in opposite directions during spontaneous versus forced imbibition in four of ten cycles; MRI showed a dispersed waterfront and uneven porosity. Gradient-based slice T2 measurements were affected by diffusion, giving faster relaxation for thinner slices, so they were not directly comparable with uniform-field distributions.

## X-Ray Microtomography and NMR: complimentary tools for evaluation of pore structure within a core

- ID: SCA2014-038, year: 2014
- Authors: Aleksandr Denisenko, Ivan Yakimchuk
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2, X-ray microtomography, surface relaxivity, pore size distribution, capillary lattice model, microporosity
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-038.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-038)

Pore size distributions from micro-CT are fitted to NMR T1 and T2 spectra to calibrate surface relaxivity and estimate sub-resolution microporosity, tested on glass beads, sandstone and dolomite.

NMR relaxation times give only indirect pore sizes, so the authors calibrate relaxivity by fitting micro-CT pore size distributions (inscribed-sphere local thickness, ~2.2 µm pixels) to NMR T1 and T2 spectra measured at 20.6 MHz. A capillary-lattice model using Klinkenberg gas permeability gives an independent relaxivity estimate. Tests were on 8 mm by 10 mm plugs of sintered glass beads, quartz sandstone and dolomite. For glass beads, ρT2 was 53 µm/s from CT and 73 µm/s from the lattice model. For sandstone, ρT2 was 24 versus 30.2 µm/s, and microporosity below CT resolution was 14.6 p.u. (45.6% of porosity) from T2. For the dolomite, the two methods disagreed strongly (ρT2 of 75 versus 2.1 µm/s; microporosity 7.9 p.u.), which is attributed to the poor link between permeability and pore-body structure in complex carbonates.

## Understanding Fractures and Pore Compressibility of Shales using NMR

- ID: SCA2016-070, year: 2016
- Authors: M. Dick, D. Green, E.M. Braun, D. Veselinovic
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, shale, confining pressure, pore compressibility, fracture porosity, pore networks
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-070.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-070)

NMR T2 measurements at confining pressures up to 5000 psi on four brine-saturated shales separate micro, meso and macro pore networks and show macro pores (fractures) close at low stress.

Understanding natural and coring-induced fractures in shales requires quantifying fracture porosity, which NMR can separate from small matrix pores by its longer T2. Four shale cores (Gates, Doig, Muskwa, Montney; He porosity 5-8.9 p.u.) were saturated with 2% KCl brine at 10,000 psi and measured by NMR T2 at 0 to 5000 psi hydrostatic confining pressure. Total porosity fell about 5% over this range in all samples. Shales 2 and 4 had three pore networks (e.g., shale 2: micro T2<1.7 ms, meso 1.7-55 ms, macro >55 ms), while shales 1 and 3 had a single pore size. The micro pores showed no compression, meso pores compressed roughly linearly, and macro pores (~0.5 p.u.) lost porosity sharply between 0 and 1000 psi then little, interpreted as fracture closure.

## A First Step in Evaluating the Role of Diffusion in EOR in Tight Shale Formations

- ID: SCA2018-024, year: 2018
- Authors: Son T. Dang, Carl H. Sondergeld, Chandra S. Rai, Ali O. Tinni, Nicholas Drenzek
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: diffusion coefficient, infrared spectroscopy, shale tortuosity, miscible gas EOR, Wicke-Kallenbach method, huff-n-puff
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-024.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-024)

A high-pressure infrared spectroscopy setup measures methane-nitrogen diffusion through a shale plug, giving a diffusion factor of 0.125 and tortuosity, implying shallow gas penetration during huff-n-puff soaking.

Direct diffusion coefficients in shales are scarce because pore volume is small and conventional fluid sampling and pressure-decay methods are unsuitable. The authors modified the Wicke-Kallenbach method, placing a high-pressure IR cell in line to continuously monitor methane concentration at the outlet of a nitrogen-saturated 1-inch shale plug (3% porosity) at 100-500 psi. Methane breakthrough took about 450 min through open space versus about 3600 min through the shale. Fitting with Fick's second law gave effective diffusion coefficients of 3.25e-8 m2/s (open space) and 0.4e-8 m2/s (shale), a diffusion factor of about 0.125 that was constant with pressure. Tortuosity (the inverse of diffusion factor) fits a literature trend with porosity, and the authors estimate injected gas penetrates only 1-2 ft during 1-2 month soaks.

## Two-Phase Fluid Flow Experiments Monitored by NMR

- ID: SCA2019-036, year: 2019
- Authors: Thomas Hiller, Gabriel Hoder, Alexandra Amann-Hildenbrand, Norbert Klitzsch, Norbert Schleifer
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR flow cell, two-phase flow, effective gas permeability, tight sandstone, drainage, Klinkenberg slip flow
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-036.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-036)

A new high-pressure NMR flow cell monitors water saturation, gas permeability and T2 distributions during stepwise nitrogen drainage of a tight Rotliegend sandstone.

Conventional effective-permeability measurements on tight rocks require repeated disassembly and resaturation, risking sample damage and giving no direct saturation measurement during flow. The authors present a new NMR flow cell, usable to 30 MPa confining pressure, that measures water saturation, effective gas permeability and NMR relaxation time distribution simultaneously. A fully water-saturated tight Rotliegend sandstone was drained by stepwise increases of nitrogen inlet pressure while low-field NMR monitored the process. After temperature-fluctuation correction, saturation changes were found to be very accurate, and the method gives total saturation, its distribution in the pore space, and the relationship between saturation and relative apparent permeability. The sample had a geometric mean T2 of 4.8 ms, helium porosity of 6.7%, and a Klinkenberg-type permeability of 4e-17 m² with b = 0.20 MPa.

## An integrated petrophysical analysis based on NMR, organic geochemistry and mineralogy. The Vaca Muerta source rock-unconventional play at different thermal maturities

- ID: SCA2022-40, year: 2022
- Authors: Diana Masiero, Marcos Comerio, Esteban Domené, Gabriela Vila, Bernarda Epele, Mariano Cipollone, Mariela Silka, Carlos Camacho, Lourdes Vera López, Silvina Chiappero, YPF-Tecnología S.A, Buenos Aires, YPF S.A
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: Vaca Muerta, NMR T1-T2 maps, gas filled porosity, Rock-Eval TOC, microCT, shale mineralogy
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-40.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-40)

An integrated workflow combining 2 MHz NMR T1-T2 maps, gas-filled porosity, XRD, Rock-Eval and microCT characterizes Vaca Muerta shales; NMR zones correlate with clay and TOC, more weakly in the gas window.

Petrophysical characterization of unconventional shales is difficult because of fine grains, organic matter and heterogeneity. The authors studied sidewall and core samples from two Vaca Muerta wells, 13 from Well A (late oil window) and 15 from Well B (gas window), using 2 MHz NMR T2 and SR-FID-ECHO-CPMG T1-T2 maps, helium GFP with Hg pycnometry, XRD, Rock-Eval and microCT. Total porosity from GFP plus NMR T2 was 10–19 pu, highest in siliciclastic shales. MicroCT identified open fractures (>30 µm) in Well A, used to correct GFP, and calcite-filled microfractures in Well B. NMR zone 3 (clays) correlated strongly with XRD clay content in both wells, while zone 2 (solid organic matter) correlated well with TOC in Well A but with more scatter in Well B, possibly due to maturity, interval differences, heterogeneity and sample mass differences.

## Use of a High Magnetic Field to Visualize Fluids in Porous Media by MRI

- ID: SCA1991-12, year: 1991
- Authors: Catherine Chardaire-Rivière, Jean-Claude Roussel
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: MRI, high magnetic field, chemical shift imaging, saturation profiles, drainage, 3-D reconstruction
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-12.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-12)

9.4 T MRI images oil and water in small cores during drainage, validates saturation profiles against a 1-D simulator, and reconstructs 3-D gas-oil displacement showing preferential gas paths.

The paper applies high-field (9.4 T) MRI to visualize fluids in small cores (1 cm diameter, 2.5 cm long) of Fontainebleau sandstone and bioclastic limestone. Chemical shift sequences separated oil and water images in the limestone, with slices 50 µm thick and about 100 µm in-plane resolution. For the sandstone, D2O replaced water because signals could not be separated, and oil saturation profiles were obtained during constant-pressure drainage (0.045 bar). Profiles compared with a 1-D two-phase Darcy simulator matched well, especially near residual water saturation (0.3), with deviations attributed to core heterogeneity; relative permeability and capillary pressure curves were estimated from the profiles. A 13-slice multislice experiment reconstructed in 3-D showed gas flowing along a preferential path that a 1-D model cannot represent.

## Pore Level Fluid Imaging Using High Resolution Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Imaging and Thin Slab Micromodels

- ID: SCA1990-24, year: 1990
- Authors: S. Mahmood, D. Doughty, L. Tomutsa, M. M. Honarpour
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: MRI microscopy, rock-slab micromodel, projection reconstruction, residual oil trapping, laminated sandstone, deuterium imaging
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-24)

A modified NMR spectrometer provides 3-D projection-reconstruction MRI of oil and water in small sandstone plugs and bead packs, complemented by optical rock-slab micromodels.

Pore-scale fluid imaging in rock is limited by clinical MRI resolution and by the simplified geometry of conventional micromodels. The authors modified a 270 MHz NMR spectrometer with a gradient coil system (350 mT/m) and 3-D projection reconstruction, imaging deuterium-oxide brine and Soltrol oil in layered Berea and Shannon sandstone plugs (120 micron pixels) and polymer bead packs (50 micron). Rock line widths (700 Hz deuterium, 2,000 Hz proton), broadened by paramagnetic clays, limited useful rock resolution to about 200-250 microns. Rock-slab micromodels (2-3 mm slabs) allowed optical observation up to 1200X of trapped oil blobs, oil ganglia from viscous bypassing, three-phase gas displacement, and fingering in Berea but not in layered Shannon. Laminations strongly affected fluid distribution and trapping, and MRI (static, 3-D, single phase selective) and optical micromodels (dynamic, high resolution) proved complementary.

## NMR Magic Angle Sample Spinning for Measuring Water and Heavy Oil Saturation and High Resolution Relaxometry

- ID: SCA1999-23, year: 1999
- Authors: D. M. Wilson, G. A. LaTorraca
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: magic angle spinning, heavy oil saturation, NMR relaxometry, proton chemical shift, diatomite, CPMG T2
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-23)

Magic angle spinning NMR resolves oil and water proton chemical shifts in heavy-oil diatomite, giving saturations and separate T1 and T2 relaxation of each phase.

Conventional low-field NMR cannot separate oil and water in cores with heavy oil because relaxation rates and diffusivities are similar. The authors applied 1H magic angle spinning at 500 and 100 MHz (with 20 MHz non-MAS comparisons) to crushed Lost Hills diatomite samples (oil API 19 and 27), using about 150 mg and minutes of acquisition. Quantitative high-resolution CPMG gave oil yields of 8.9% (Diatomite B) and 10.1% (Diatomite A) by weight, versus 8.9% and 8.2% from Soxhlet extraction. Separate T1, CPMG T2 and spin-echo T2 were obtained for oil and water. Oil T1 depends strongly on field, indicating dipolar relaxation, while water T1 changes little, indicating surface relaxation. Rock-entrained oil T2 is about an order of magnitude shorter than bulk oil T2, suggesting surface relaxation also matters for oil in pores.

## Preliminary Transformation of an NMR Spectrometer into an NMR Image for Evaluating Fluid Content and Rock Properties of Core Samples

- ID: SCA1987-13, year: 1987
- Authors: Daryl A. Doughty, Nicida L. Maerefat
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, SPINWARP pulse sequence, T2 relaxation, gradient coils, sandstone cores, high-frequency NMR
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1987/SCA1987-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1987-13)

A 270 MHz NMR spectrometer was converted to an imager; it imaged phantoms and sandpacks but not sandstone plugs because of very short T2, motivating a move to 60 MHz.

The authors converted a JEOL GX-270 (6.34 T, 270 MHz proton) FT-NMR spectrometer into an NMR imager for core analysis by adding X-Y-Z gradient coils, a gradient control board, shaped RF pulse capability, and a probe for samples up to 32 mm diameter. A SPINWARP sequence gave 128 x 128 pixel images of water phantoms and a brine-saturated sandpack (25-30 mesh sand, ~3 mm slice). Berea and Cottage Grove sandstone plugs (1 in. diameter) gave no detectable imaging signal, with a ~5,000 Hz water line width. For Cottage Grove, T1 was 287 ms and T2 only 0.794 ms, too short for the several-ms shaped pulses and gradient switching. Poor RF homogeneity arose from a compromised coil geometry. The authors plan to lower the frequency to 60 MHz (expecting T2 over 4 times longer), add multislice imaging, and use faster gradient coils.

## Laboratory MRI Investigation in the Effects of Invert Oil Muds on Primary MRI Log Determinations

- ID: SCA1997-01, year: 1997
- Authors: D. M. Marschall, G. Coates
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 cutoff, irreducible water volume BVI, invert oil mud, wettability alteration, MRI log permeability, emulsifier filtrate
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-01.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-01)

Lab NMR on North Sea cores shows emulsifiers in invert mud filtrate shift wettability, underestimating BVI; a core-calibrated correction at 90.5 ms cutoff restores BVI and permeability.

MRI logs in North Sea wells drilled with invert oil muds underestimated BVI and overestimated permeability relative to core. Six sandstone plugs were tested at ~1 MHz NMR, first to validate the T2 cutoff (22.6 and 33 ms) and permeability model, which did not reproduce the anomaly. Flushing with pressed mud filtrate caused no wetting change, but simulated filtrates containing 2–20 ppb emulsifier reduced short-T2 BVI in all but the lowest-permeability sample (244), with little dependence on concentration. Interfacial tension measurements indicated the pressed filtrate held less than 2 ppb emulsifier. Restored-state cores were already shifted toward oil-wet. A correction using a 90.5 ms cutoff and a linear DBVI relation (eq. 4) matched core BVI and permeability on the field log.

## Pore Network Simulation of Low-Field NMR Relaxometry Under Conditions of Drainage and Imbibition: Effects of Pore Structure and Saturation History

- ID: SCA2001-18, year: 2001
- Authors: D. Chang, M.A. Ioannidis
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, NMR T2 relaxation, late pore filling, drainage and imbibition, diffusive coupling, mercury porosimetry
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-18.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-18)

A pore network model with a late pore filling correction simulates NMR decay spectra during drainage and imbibition, showing saturation history strongly shapes T2 distributions.

Interpreting NMR T2 spectra of partially saturated rock requires separating the effects of pore structure, wettability and saturation history. The authors build a simple cubic pore network (15^3 pores, square throats, Weibull size distributions, 10 realizations) and solve coupled magnetization balances by matrix diagonalization at each saturation step of drainage and imbibition. A late pore filling model (Brooks-Corey form, parameters Sw* and exponent h) represents rough or fractal surfaces and reproduces gradual mercury-porosimetry-like capillary pressure curves. Sw* lowers residual non-wetting saturation, while h changes curve gradualness but not the residual. Drainage removes slow decay rates as large pores are invaded; imbibition yields a second fast-rate peak from water in pores with trapped oil, so spectra at the same saturation (Snw=0.7) differ with history.

## Core Analysis with Two Dimensional NMR

- ID: SCA2002-38, year: 2002
- Authors: Boqin Sun, Keh-Jim Dunn
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 2D NMR, magic angle spinning, internal field gradient, T1-MAS, relaxation-diffusion correlation, wettability
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-38.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-38)

Introduces T1-MAS and relaxation-diffusion (RD2D) 2D NMR methods to separate oil from water, probe internal field gradients, and infer wettability change in cores.

Conventional 1D NMR relaxometry struggles with fluid typing because internal field gradients broaden proton spectra and enhance T2 decay through diffusion. The authors developed T1-MAS 2D NMR, which spins crushed rock at the magic angle (10 kHz) to remove susceptibility broadening and resolve oil (1.4 ppm) from water (5.1 ppm) against T1. They also developed RD2D, a split CPMG sequence with varying first-part echo spacing, run on a 2 MHz spectrometer, to map internal gradient versus T2. On diatomite, fresh, extracted and brine-flooded plugs showed distinct oil and water T1 peaks. After flooding, oil T1 rose to about 174 ms and its line width halved, consistent with a water-wet surface. RD2D showed smooth or bimodal gradient distributions in sandstones, and separated crude oil from irreducible water in diatomite by their different apparent gradients.

## Time-Dependent Diffusion and Surface Relaxation in Reconstructed Porous Rock

- ID: SCA2002-43, year: 2002
- Authors: Sadiq Olayinka, Marios A. Ioannidis
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR random-walk simulation, time-dependent diffusion, surface relaxivity, stochastic reconstruction, Padé approximant, permeability prediction
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-43.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-43)

Random-walk simulations in stochastic 3D rock replicas show bi-exponential NMR relaxation, non-monotonic time-dependent diffusion, and permeability predictable from NMR relaxation and diffusion parameters.

The paper uses random-walk simulations to study NMR time-dependent diffusion and surface relaxation in five stochastically reconstructed 3D porous media (porosity 0.126-0.198, correlation length 7.6-59.9 µm) whose geometry is independently known. The algorithm was verified against a periodic swiss-cheese model. Short-time diffusion depends only on surface-to-volume ratio, independent of surface relaxivity, and a Padé fit gives √D0θ about a third of V/S. Effective diffusivity is non-monotonic with time, and the long-time limit with surface relaxation underestimates true tortuosity, so formation factor is underestimated by a factor of 2 to 3. Magnetization decay is essentially bi-exponential, with the long-time mode tied to the pore size controlling permeability. Permeability predicted from relaxation times (k ∝ φ⁴T²) and from diffusion-derived S/V and 1/α agrees well with computed values.

## Determining Carbonate Content of Cores by $^{13}\text{C}$ NMR

- ID: SCA1989-01, year: 1989
- Authors: P.B.RoemerShell Development Co
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 13C NMR, carbonate content, whole core, chemical shift powder pattern, T1 relaxation, nondestructive analysis
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1989/SCA1989-01.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1989-01)

A large 13C/1H coil on a 2 Tesla imager lets natural-abundance 13C NMR measure carbonate and oil carbon in whole core within minutes, accurate to about 5%.

Carbonate content is normally found by destructive acid dissolution of small ground samples. The authors built a 5 1/2 in. 13C/1H double-resonance coil for a GE CSI 2 Tesla imager so that 4 in. OD whole core, even inside a fiberglass barrel, gives usable natural-abundance 13C signal. The carbonate powder pattern (120-195 ppm) is separable from aliphatic oil (10-40 ppm), and T1 of carbonate rocks ranged 25-146 s (average 88 s), much shorter than the ~40 min of a pure calcite crystal, with no dependence on crystal orientation. Interleaved 1H and 13C acquisition with an automated core table adds essentially no time for carbonate data. Cyclohexane in plugs was recovered within 5%, dolomite/calcite-sand mixtures were linear with accuracy of 5% or better except at the lowest concentration, and a heavy-oil Monterey whole core was measured in ten minutes versus about three months for Dean Stark.

## Petrophysical Applications of Automated Proton High Resolution NMR Spectroscopy

- ID: SCA1990-20, year: 1990
- Authors: P. N. TutunjianH.
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: proton NMR spectroscopy, sponge core analysis, T1 relaxation viscosity, chloroform/methanol extraction, Dean-Stark extraction, freon-11
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-20)

Automated high-resolution 1H NMR quantifies oil and water in freon, chloroform/methanol and xylene core extracts accurately, and estimates crude viscosity from T1 relaxation.

Routine core extraction solvents are normally discarded, though they contain oil and water that can be measured. Shell used automated high-resolution 1H NMR spectrometers with 120-tube sample changers and microVAX processing to analyze freon-11 extracts of polyurethane sponge liners, chloroform/methanol miscible flood extracts, and xylene Dean-Stark extracts. Spiked tests gave standard errors of about 0.04 cc (2%) for sponge extracts, 0.05 cc for oil and water in chloroform/methanol, and 0.03 cc (1.5%) in xylene. Distillation/GC over-read oil by 1-2 cc below 3 cc because residual urethane was counted as oil. T1 of the oil in dilute extracts correlates with undiluted crude viscosity. NMR of eleven chloroform/methanol flushes showed pure methanol still removed 0.6 cc brine (33% of initial water) after oil was fully extracted. The method takes about 10 minutes per sample and is in routine use.

## NMR Imaging of Fluid Saturation Distributions in Core Samples Using a High Magnetic Field

- ID: SCA1990-15EURO, year: 1990
- Authors: Catherine Chardaire, Jean-Claude Roussel
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, high magnetic field, chemical shift selective excitation, spin-warp sequence, oil-water distribution, Fontainebleau sandstone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-15EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-15EURO)

Using a 9.4 Tesla MRI spectrometer, the authors image 50 µm slices of limestone and sandstone cores and separate oil from water by chemical shift without doping.

The paper explores high-field (9.4 T) NMR micro-imaging to visualize fluid distributions in porous media. Experiments used 3 cm by 1 cm cores of Fontainebleau sandstone (15% porosity, 2 D) and bioclastic limestone (30% porosity, 300 mD), saturated with water, with Soltrol 130 oil, or with both. Spin-warp sequences gave 512x512 images of 50 µm-thick slices, about 30 times thinner than the 1.5 mm CT slices. A chemical-shift selective sequence separated oil and water images without doping agents. The FLASH fast sequence gave no image on water-saturated limestone, possibly because of local magnetic susceptibility heterogeneity at high field.

## Improving Estimation of NMR Log

- ID: SCA1998-22, year: 1998
- Authors: Capillary Pressure Measurements
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: T2 cutoff, bulk volume irreducible, NMR log calibration, signal-to-noise ratio, regularization, Coates permeability
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-22)

Two methods calibrate NMR T2cutoff for logs, adding noise to core echoes or matching capillary-pressure BVI to log spectra, improving BVI and permeability estimates.

Core-derived T2cutoff values are often inconsistent with NMR logs because logs have lower SNR, stronger regularization effects, and different TE and echo counts than core data. The authors show that noise and regularization broaden short-T2 spectra and bias BVI, using core plugs and synthetic log data. Method one adds Gaussian noise to the Sw=1 core echo trains (3-4 realizations) to match log SNR before computing T2cutoff. In 13 clastic core samples, original T2cutoff values below about 10 ms shifted noticeably upward at SNR=10, with the discrepancy greatly reduced at SNR=20. Method two finds T2cutoff by matching log cumulative porosity to depth-matched capillary-pressure BVI; in a North Sea clastic reservoir nine samples gave 80 ms, with a 1:1 BVI crossplot and good agreement of core and log permeability using Coates parameters (C=10.91, m=4, n=1.73).

## NMR Relaxivity Grouping or NMR Facies Identification Is Key to Effective Integration of Core Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Data with Wireline Log

- ID: SCA1999-42, year: 1999
- Authors: Henry A. Ohen, Paulinus M. Enwere, Jerry Kier
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR relaxivity groups, T2 cutoff, flow zone indicator, surface relaxivity, NMR permeability model, mercury injection capillary pressure
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-42.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-42)

Core NMR T2 data are grouped into relaxivity groups (NMR facies) to assign T2 cutoffs and permeability models for log calibration, validated on two sandstone studies.

NMR log interpretation suffers from fixed T2 cutoffs, inappropriate permeability models and unusual rock relaxivity. The authors define relaxivity groups from log T2 versus porosity group (φ/(1-φ)), where each group has a characteristic relaxivity product 1/(r2·Sgv), and calibrate them with core porosity, permeability, MICP, centrifuge desaturation and NMR T2 data. In Example 1 (~400 sandstone plugs, Africa) eight groups were found, with mean 1/(r2Sgv) from 1.163 s (Group 1) to 0.011 s (Group 8), and T2c (136 samples) ranging 3-70 ms and rising with group quality. Groups correlate with depositional environment, grain size, clay and iron-mineral content (Groups 1-4 mostly <3% iron minerals). Example 2 (18 North American sandstones) gave three groups; FZI–BVI models per group or EOD feed an NMR permeability equation that matched core permeability better than a single 33 ms cutoff.

## A Mechanism for the Temperature Dependence of the Surface Relaxation Rate in Carbonates

- ID: SCA2002-27, year: 2002
- Authors: Christian Straley
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR surface relaxation, temperature dependence, dipole-dipole relaxation, carbonates, D2O saturation, paramagnetic relaxation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-27.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-27)

Comparing NMR T2 of two limestones saturated with H2O versus 10% H2O in D2O shows surface hydrogen-hydrogen relaxation is significant and could explain carbonate temperature dependence.

Some carbonates show T2 increasing with temperature, which paramagnetic-only surface relaxation theory cannot explain. The author tests whether surface hydrogen-hydrogen dipole-dipole (hh) relaxation contributes by comparing 2 MHz CPMG T2 of two limestones (a temperature-dependent Middle East sample S and Leuder's limestone L) saturated with H2O and a 10% H2O/90% D2O mixture at 30°C. Solving the two-rate equations, with bulk-corrected rates and BET surface area, gives r_d of about 0.2–0.3 mm/s, below the theoretical limit of 0.6 mm/s. For S the paramagnetic and hh rates are similar (0.23 and 0.295 mm/s), while for L paramagnetic relaxation dominates by about tenfold, consistent with S's temperature dependence and L's lack of it. Applying a constant r_d of 0.34 mm/s to eleven samples from an earlier study accounts for about 78% of the T2 shift between 30 and 130°C.

## An Improved Technique for Deriving Drainage Capillary Pressure from NMR T2 Distributions

- ID: SCA2003-25, year: 2003
- Authors: S.H. Al-Mahrooqi, A. K. Moss, A. H. Muggeridge, X.D. Jing
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, drainage capillary pressure, triangular pore model, irreducible water saturation, surface relaxivity, spectral BVI
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-25.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-25)

A triangular-pore model accounts for water left in crevices of drained pores, improving NMR T2-derived drainage capillary pressure at high pressures in sandstones without empirical scaling.

NMR-derived drainage capillary pressure methods overpredict desaturation at high capillary pressure because they ignore wetting fluid retained in crevices of drained pores. The authors use an equilateral triangular pore model to calculate retained water saturation and the shifted T2 of drained pores, then build an effective cumulative T2 distribution combining fully saturated and calculated drained distributions. Capillary pressure is then obtained with Volokitin's kappa-scaling. Tested on eleven sandstones (outcrop and reservoir, 0.22 to 2220 mD) with porous plate, centrifuge and mercury injection data, the model predicted irreducible water saturation at 100 psi well. Fitted surface relaxivity ranged 1.2-4.0 µm/s for outcrop samples, and the corrected Pc curves agreed better with measurements, especially at low saturation and in lower-permeability cores.

## Advanced Imaged-based Workflow for 3D Pore Surface Roughness Quantification

- ID: SCA2023-037, year: 2023
- Authors: Yiteng Li, Xupeng He, Hyung Kwak, Hussein Hoteit
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: surface roughness, spherical harmonics, pore separation, micro-CT, NMR T2 relaxation, Berea sandstone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-037.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-037)

A workflow using pore separation, spherical parameterization and spherical harmonic expansion quantifies 3D pore surface roughness from μ-CT images; applied to Berea sandstone, mean roughness coefficient is about 1.45.
