Pore irregularity affects NMR T2 simulation in digital rocks, but roughness is usually measured only in 2D by microscopy. The authors propose a workflow that segments μ-CT images, separates pores with the SNOW watershed algorithm plus skeleton-based second-level splitting, and reconstructs each pore surface by spherical parameterization (CALD smoothing) and spherical harmonic expansion (maximum degree 40). A reference surface built from a subset of coefficients defines local roughness at each vertex, and an area-weighted average gives a 3D roughness coefficient. Applied to a 256³ Berea sandstone image at 2.7745 µm, separating pores gave 520 bodies versus 1494 when separating grains. Using smoothed triangular meshes rather than voxelized pores improved reconstruction. More than 80% of pores had roughness coefficients below 2, with an average of about 1.447, lower than values reported from high-resolution 2D measurements.

## Combination of NMR and Ultracentrifuge Techniques for Effective Carbonate Reservoir Characterization

- ID: SCA1997-03, year: 1997
- Authors: Konstantin Mirotchnik, Kevin Allsopp, Apostolos Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, ultracentrifuge, asphaltene adsorption, wettability alteration, carbonate reservoir, irreducible water saturation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-03)

NMR T2 spectra combined with centrifuge drainage and waterflood on carbonate cores indicate adsorption of heavy crude components in the largest pores, implying wettability alteration.

The paper addresses detecting adsorption of heavy crude oil fractions and associated wettability alteration in carbonate reservoir rock. Six aged reservoir carbonate plugs, three clean carbonates, and Berea and Baker dolomite references were brine saturated, centrifuged to Swi (6000 RPM), oil saturated (16 mPa.s oil), aged at 82 °C, and waterflooded in the centrifuge, with NMR T2 measured at each step. Bulk oil mean T2 correlated with viscosity on a log-log line, and NMR porosity and Swi matched mass balance (e.g., sample R2 Swi 0.087 vs 0.083). Oil-saturated carbonates showed shorter T2 components than 100% water saturation, and waterflooded cores showed longer T2, with the shift inversely related to Swi. Produced oil spectra shifted to higher T2 and showed new long-T2 components, interpreted as heavy-component retention and possible in-situ upgrading; Baker dolomite and Berea showed much smaller effects.

## Determination of Mud Invasion Characteristics of Sandstone Reservoirs Using a Combination of Advanced Core Analysis Techniques

- ID: SCA1998-15, year: 1998
- Authors: Konstantin Mirotchnik, Peter Kubika, Leslie Randall, Alex Starosud
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, mud invasion, NMR permeability models, wettability alteration, gas chromatography, MRI
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-15.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-15)

Combines low- and high-field NMR, MRI, centrifuge and GC on six cores to characterize mud invasion, saturation, permeability models and wettability alteration for NMR log calibration.

The study supports NMR log interpretation of mud invasion in sandstone reservoirs. Six plugs were saturated with brine, waxy crude oil and oil-based mud (IPAR-3/CaCl2 brine) at 80°C, and measured with a 1 MHz relaxometer, 100 MHz MRI/NMR, ultracentrifuge and GC-MS. T2 geometric means separate native oil-saturated from mud-invaded states, and high-field T2 values are shorter owing to internal gradients. NMR porosity matched mass balance within 0.3 porosity units, but invaded-zone porosity is uncertain without knowing the fluid mixture. Common NMR permeability models (including FFI/BVI) gave errors up to over an order of magnitude and standard T2 cutoffs were unreliable. Sample 8A (dolomite cement, mesopores) showed adsorption of polar components and wettability alteration, with GC showing displaced oil depleted in polar and C20+ components (mix-70 had 1.44 times more C20+ than mix-8A), while sample 70 stayed water-wet.

## Discrimination of Bound Water in Small Pores and Thin Films in Porous Media by NMR Method

- ID: SCA2000-44, year: 2000
- Authors: S. Akselrod, K. Mirotchnik, A. Kantzas, K. Allsopp
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: NMR T2 cutoff, surface-bound water, free fluid index, irreducible water saturation, centrifuge displacement, T2 distribution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2000/SCA2000-44.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2000-44)

Proposes an NMR T2-bin method that splits each bin into free and surface-bound water, showing bound water exists above T2cutoff and declines with increasing pore size.

Conventional NMR log interpretation treats all signal above T2cutoff as free fluid, ignoring surface-bound water in larger pores. The authors derive a relaxation-rate weighting model that gives the free and surface-bound water volume in each T2 bin, using T2sb taken from the T2 distribution at irreducible saturation. T2 distributions were measured on carbonates, shaly sandstones, Berea sandstones and artificial porous media at full saturation and after centrifuging at 6000 RPM. Surface-bound water was always found in the T2 > cutoff range, for example Wsb of 0.84-1.82% in carbonates and 2.5-3.4% in several sandstones. The relative bound-water saturation falls with increasing bin T2, meaning increasing pore size, in both sandstones and carbonates. Artificial media with uniform pores showed little bound water (0.3-0.74%).

## Low-Field Nuclear Magnetic Resonance

- ID: SCA2013-002, year: 2013
- Authors: Catherine Kirkland
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: T1-T2 correlation, solid-echo pulse sequence, kerogen, oil shale, partial least squares regression, Rock-Eval TOC
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-002.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-002)

T1-T2 spin-echo and solid-echo LF-NMR on crushed oil shales, combined with PLSR, predicts TOC and Rock-Eval S2 with cross-validated R2 of about 0.84-0.88.

Unconventional source rocks contain hydrogen in solid kerogen and bitumen, whose relaxation differs from fluids in pores. The authors ran 2 MHz LF-NMR T1-T2 correlations (spin-echo and solid-echo) on 18 crushed oil shales, discussing four (Alum, Glen Davis, Kukersite, Timahdit), and compared a few samples with 300 MHz high-field data. Solid-echo increased total signal, with the kerogen peak rising about 2.5-3 times for three shales and about 50% for Timahdit. PLSR of the spectra against LECO TOC and Rock-Eval data gave R2 of 0.993 (calibration) and 0.876 (cross-validation) for TOC, and 0.994 and 0.843 for S2, with 5-6 factors. S1 prediction was limited and S3 was poor. The sample set was too small for an independent test set.

## M. Fleury

- ID: SCA2014-014, year: 2014
- Authors: M. Fleury
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: T1-T2 maps, shale, deuterium exchange tortuosity, organic matter, methane NMR relaxation, clay hydroxyls
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-014.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-014)

T1-T2 NMR maps at 23.7 MHz on dry, water- and methane-saturated shales and extracted organic matter separate hydroxyl, organic matter, water and methane proton signatures; deuterium exchange gives tortuosity.

Characterizing shale pore networks and fluids is difficult with conventional petrophysics. The study used a 23.7 MHz Maran Ultra spectrometer with a small 10 mm probe (5 µs dead time) and a 200 bar PEEK cell to acquire T1-T2 maps on organic matter extracted from shales of different maturity and on a Horn River shale (RM9) in dry, water and methane saturated states. Hydroxyls from clay appear at T2 < 0.1 ms with wide T1 range, water lies near T1/T2 ~ 2, and methane near T1/T2 ~ 10–20 depending on pressure. Dry organic matter shows T1/T2 ~ 50 when immature versus 180–250 in oil and gas windows, and hydrogen content of 59, 30 and 21 mg/g for immature, oil and gas OM. Deuterium exchange on RM9 (NMR porosity 7.1%, 2 nD) showed full connectivity, with Deff = 0.62e-6 cm2/s, tortuosity 41.8 and Archie m = 2.45, while mercury injection resolved only about half of the porosity.

## Hydrogen Index measurements of highly saline brines under pressures up to 15'000 psi and temperatures up to 300°F

- ID: SCA2016-042, year: 2016
- Authors: S. A. Hertel, B. C. Anger, K. Smith, M. Appel
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: Hydrogen Index, NMR porosity, brine density model, NaCl brine salinity, high-pressure high-temperature NMR, Curie law correction
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-042.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-042)

An NMR high-pressure, high-temperature setup measures brine Hydrogen Index and validates a density-based model for NaCl brines, showing HI drops up to 17% at high salinity and temperature.

NMR porosity calibration needs the Hydrogen Index (HI) of pore fluids, which deviates from unity for saline brines at reservoir conditions. The authors built a heated, pressurized ceramic vessel inside an Oxford GeoSpec 2 (2.3 MHz) spectrometer, with Fluorinert heating and cooling loops, to measure HI up to 300°F and 12,000 psi. NaCl brines of 47 to 260 kppm were measured and compared with the Mao and Duan density model. Data agree with the model within ±0.5% at STP, and within about ±2% in temperature runs because of temperature uncertainty and Curie-law correction. HI falls by up to 11% with salinity at STP and reaches 0.83 for saturated brine at 300°F, a 17% decrease. Pressure raises HI only about 2% over 14.5 to 14,500 psi, so temperature and salinity dominate.

## Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Imaging of Sodium-23 in Cores

- ID: SCA1991-11, year: 1991
- Authors: P. N. Tutunjian, H. J. Vinegar, J. A. Ferris
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 23Na NMR imaging, brine salinity, spin-lattice relaxation T1, sodium diffusion coefficient, formation factor, filtrate invasion
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-11.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-11)

Presents the first 23Na NMR measurements and images in cores, showing linear salinity response, short T1, 1-D diffusion profiling and imaging of nonuniform brine transport in sandstones.

Sodium-23 is the main cation in formation brines, but its NMR sensitivity is about 1/10 that of the proton. Quadrupolar relaxation gives T1 values of tens of ms (62 to 38 ms from 0.005 to 5 M NaCl), allowing fast signal averaging. NaCl brines and brine-saturated Indiana limestone showed linear NMR intensity versus salinity, with the limestone linear from 500 to 200,000 ppm. NMR sodium mass in carbonates and sandstones agreed with weighed values to 3.1% on average, with underestimation increasing with shaliness. Solid sodium minerals have linewidths at least ten times broader than mobile sodium, so a narrowband probe rejects them. 1-D diffusion in Berea gave an effective D of 0.58e-5 cm2/s, a diffusional formation factor of 10.0 versus an electrical value of 13.7. Imaging of salt effusion and infusion in Berea and Bentheim cores showed nonuniform transport that 1H images largely failed to reveal.

## Determination of Saturation Profiles via Low-Field NMR Imaging

- ID: SCA2009-09, year: 2009
- Authors: Michael Rauschhuber, George Hirasaki
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, D-T2 profiling, RARE sequence, saturation profile, frequency-encoding gradient, bipolar gradients
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-09.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-09)

Frequency-encoded RARE T2 and D-T2 profiling on a low-field Maran spectrometer give 1D oil-water saturation profiles in a sandpack without D2O, before and after waterflood.

The paper develops two low-field NMR imaging methods on a Maran Ultra spectrometer: RARE for T2 profiles and a stimulated-echo D-T2 profiling sequence for diffusion and T2 versus height. Parameter selection (gradient strength and duration, dwell time, number of points) is guided by diffusion-attenuation models balancing resolution against T2 attenuation. Bipolar diffusion gradients and a (π/2)y pulse were needed to obtain stable echoes. Tests used a 12-inch sandpack of 20/40 sand with 22 cP crude oil, measured after oilflood and after a 0.5 PV waterflood. RARE could not separate oil and water because their T2 distributions overlap, but D-T2 profiling could. The oilflood profile averaged So = 0.76 versus 0.78 by mass balance. After waterflood So rose from about 0.06 at the inlet to about 0.45 mid-core, with an average of 0.34 versus 0.43 by mass balance. Echo spacing and diffusion time limit sensitivity to fast-relaxing clay-bound and capillary-bound water.

## Multi-Frequency NMR and TGA Characterization of TOC in Shales

- ID: SCA2016-025, year: 2016
- Authors: B.C. Angera, M. Rydzya, N.P. Ramskillb, S. Hertela, A.J. Sedermanb, M.D. Mantle, L.F. Gladdenb, M. Appel
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: T1-T2 NMR relaxation, T1/T2 ratio, total organic carbon, TGA-MS, multi-frequency NMR, organic shale
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-025.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-025)

T1-T2 NMR at 2, 12 and 23 MHz plus TGA-MS on crushed shales show T1/T2 rises with TOC, T2 is frequency-insensitive, and NMR volume tracks baking mass loss.

The paper examines how T1/T2 ratio from multi-frequency NMR relates to TOC and frequency in two crushed gas shale sets (A and B, TOC about 0.1–10.7 wt%). T1-T2 maps were acquired at 2.3, 12.7 and 23.3 MHz, and TGA-MS was run to 850 °C. T1/T2 increases with TOC, with an apparent threshold near 4–5 wt% TOC, though the expected ωτ frequency dependence was not observed. T2 was nearly frequency independent while T1 varied by more than an order of magnitude between 2 and 23 MHz, suggesting faster T2 acquisition at higher field, with a potential signal gain above 30. After baking shale A under He at 120 and 220 °C, high and low T1/T2 peaks decreased at the same rate, and integrated NMR volume correlated with gravimetric mass loss; TGA-MS indicated mainly immobile organics below 5% TOC and more mobile hydrocarbons above.

## Optimizing NMR Data Acquisition and Data Processing Parameters for Tight-Gas Montney Formation of Western Canada

- ID: SCA2017-020, year: 2017
- Authors: Babak Salimifard, Mike Dick, Derrick Green, Douglas W. Ruth
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR T2 inversion, regularization smoothing parameter, echo spacing, recycle delay, Montney tight gas, signal-to-noise ratio
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-020.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-020)

Acquisition and inversion parameters for low-field NMR on a tight Montney plug are optimized; short echo spacing, long recycle delay and a fixed smoothing parameter of 0.2 work best.

Fast-relaxing tight rocks like the Montney make NMR acquisition and T2 inversion difficult. A 1.5" Montney plug (5.7 pu porosity, 208 nD permeability) was scanned on a 0.3 T benchtop analyzer while varying echo spacing, number of echoes, recycle delay, SNR, digital filter and regularization. A fixed smoothing parameter of 0.2, calibrated against imbibed brine volume (0.607 ml), gave stable bimodal T2 distributions at low SNR, outperforming BRD and GIT for T2lm and free-to-bound ratios. A 125 kHz filter removed all background signal, and at τ=200 µs 77% of micropore volume was lost versus 50 µs. Short recycle delays lost macropore and micropore signal, and repeatability tests showed heating and drying effects at short τ.

## The Use of Paramagnetic Dopants in Optimising the Accuracy of Critical and Residual Oil Saturation Determination

- ID: SCA2017-063, year: 2017
- Authors: Phil Mitchell, Lukasz Gromala, Mahmood Siddiqui
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: paramagnetic dopant, manganese chloride, NMR T2, residual oil saturation, critical oil saturation, Dean-Stark extraction
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-063.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-063)

Manganese doping of brine separates oil and water NMR T2 signals in carbonate cores, giving non-destructive residual and critical oil saturations that agree well with Dean-Stark and retort.

Overlapping oil and water T2 signals make NMR saturation determination difficult in SCAL work. The authors added MnCl2 to the brine to shift the water signal and separate it from the oil peak, using a 2.2 MHz benchtop spectrometer at 35 °C. Two Middle-East carbonate case studies were run: residual oil in a gas-cap zone (8 samples, compared with Dean-Stark) and critical oil saturation in an oil-water transition zone (23 samples, compared with retort). NMR residual oil saturations generally agreed with extraction values, and the transition-zone data bracketed critical oil saturation near 0.05, with the lowest-saturation sample (0.044) apparently sub-critical. Tests on samples with gravimetrically known water volumes (0.5–4 ml) showed retort was considerably more accurate than Dean-Stark, which systematically under-measured water at low volumes.

## Direct Magnetic Resonance Measurement of Average Pore Size

- ID: SCA2019-035, year: 2019
- Authors: Florin Marica, Armin Afrough, Derrick Green, Laura Romero-Zeron, Bruce Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: Brownstein-Tarr theory, T1-T2 correlation, non-ground modes, pore size, surface relaxivity, SE-SPI
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-035.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-035)

Non-ground relaxation modes in T1-T2 maps give average pore size and surface relaxivities directly; sandstone results match SEM within a factor of 2, and a spatially resolved version is demonstrated.

Conventional NMR T2 distributions need a separate relaxivity calibration to yield pore size. The authors use Brownstein-Tarr theory to identify ground and first non-ground modes in 2D T1-T2 maps, with varied regularization in the inversion. An optimization then solves for pore size and the relaxivities ρ1 and ρ2. For brine-saturated Buff Berea, Castlegate and Nugget sandstones, pore sizes were 21, 29 and 27 µm, versus SEM values of 40, 35 and 37 µm, within a factor of 2. Derived relaxivities were over an order of magnitude above literature values, which the authors attribute to diffusion-limited relaxation, not rapid exchange, in the conventional analysis. An inversion-prepared SE-SPI T1-T2 profile on a Berea/Indiana limestone composite gave 24 µm and 53 µm, consistent with bulk and SEM results, though the small-pore mode of the limestone was not seen.

## Comparison of Experimental NMR Measurements with Simulated Responses on Digitized Images of Mono-Mineralic Rocks Using Xray-CT

- ID: SCA2009-44, year: 2009
- Authors: C.H. Arns, Y. Melean, L. Burcaw, P.T. Callaghan, K.E. Washburn
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, surface relaxivity, internal magnetic field gradients, Xray-CT digital rock, carbonate micro-porosity, CPMG random walk simulation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-44.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-44)

Multiscale Xray-CT/SEM-based random-walk NMR simulation with explicit internal fields is compared with low- and high-field CPMG data on two carbonates, showing field-dependent roles of surface relaxivity and internal gradients.

NMR T2 interpretation in carbonates is complicated by surface relaxivity being tuned to mercury injection data and by internal field gradients. The authors built multiscale digital models of two vuggy Middle-East carbonates from Xray-CT (about 2.6-2.9 µm) and SEM (100-200 nm) images, using Gaussian-field 2-point correlation models for micro-porosity. Internal magnetic fields were computed separately from surface relaxation, and CPMG responses were simulated by random walk at about 100 nm resolution. Simulations were compared with experiments at 2 MHz and 400 MHz on brine-saturated plugs. At low field, internal fields contribute nothing and a surface relaxivity of just above 1 µm/s matched best, though simulated distributions were narrower than measured. At high field, T2 shifted markedly to shorter times with increasing echo spacing, and surface relaxivity had little influence at large spacings, so internal gradients dominate.

## How to Obtain Primary Drainage Capillary Pressure Curves Using NMR T2 Distributions in a Heterogeneous Carbonate Reservoir

- ID: SCA2017-066, year: 2017
- Authors: Fabian Brandimarte, Martin Eriksson, Adam Moss
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, mercury injection capillary pressure, scaling factor, heterogeneous carbonate, transition zone water saturation, NMR log
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-066.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-066)

Saturation-dependent T2-to-Pc scaling factors calibrated on carbonate plug MICP and NMR data convert NMR logs to capillary pressure and water saturation matching Archie values.

Conventional T2 cut-off methods give only bound water and miss transition-zone saturations. The authors calibrated a variable scaling factor function between brine-saturated NMR T2 distributions and mercury injection capillary pressure curves on 16 carbonate plugs. Plugs with trim-to-plug porosity differences above 20% were excluded as heterogeneous, as checked by CT scanning, and data were stress corrected with the Juhasz method. A global polynomial scaling factor function reproduced the MICP curves, with over 80% of samples having RMS saturation error below 8 saturation units. Applied to a 400 m NMR log section with a gas leg, it showed transition-zone water saturation over at least 100 m above the FWL, which default (100 ms) and SCAL-optimised (173 ms) T2 cut-offs did not capture. The results were similar to Archie saturations.

## NMR Investigation of Invasion Process of Formate Mud in Sandstone Cores

- ID: SCA2011-52, year: 2011
- Authors: Jilin Zhang, Jinhong Chen, Arve Konrad Thorsen, Monica Constable, Nnamdi Stephen Nwaneri
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: formate mud invasion, NMR T2 spectra, bulk volume irreducible, D2O saturation, NMR porosity correction, sandstone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-52.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-52)

Low-field NMR T2 monitoring of formate mud injected into D2O- and oil-saturated sandstone plugs shows rapid invasion into BVI and BVM, then slow migration into small pores.

Formate mud invasion alters fluid composition and NMR porosity and T2 response in wells drilled with solid-free formate mud. Four Tarbert and Ness sandstone plugs (1 in. diameter) were saturated with D2O brine; two were flushed with 1.85 g/cc formate mud (300 psi, 90 min) to mimic a water zone, and two were flushed with mineral oil and then 2.15 g/cc mud to mimic an oil zone. T2 spectra were measured at 2 MHz repeatedly afterwards, using a 33 ms BVI/BVM cutoff. Formate mud appeared in both BVI and BVM within the first 3 hours, consistent with fast molecular diffusion. In sample Z11, the formate fraction in BVI rose from about 50% to 60% over 144 hours while that in BVM fell from about 45% to 25%, indicating gradual migration to small pores; oil-zone behavior differed between samples and remains unexplained.

## Applications of a New Technique to Acquire Spatially Resolved NMR Petrophysical Data

- ID: SCA2012-32, year: 2012
- Authors: Derrick Green, Dragan Veselinovic, Bruce Balcom, Florin Marica
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: spatially resolved T2, SE-SPI, irreducible water saturation, T2 log mean, centrifuge saturation profile, fluid redistribution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-32.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-32)

A spin-echo single point imaging method gives spatially resolved T2 distributions in core plugs, used to predict irreducible water saturation, track fluid redistribution after centrifuging, and detect heterogeneity.

The paper presents a spin-echo single point imaging (SE-SPI) technique that acquires T2 distributions along a core plug, run on a 2.4 MHz GeoSpec2 with 64 points over 7 cm. Three applications are shown on 1.5-inch sandstone plugs: detecting improper saturation, predicting irreducible water saturation, and monitoring redistribution. Four plugs were centrifuged at 2500, 5000 and 7500 rpm, and saturation versus T2 log mean was extrapolated to estimate Swir. The linear relationship held only at higher saturations, and saturation correlated better with the log of T2LM. Predicted Swir was 26.9, 18.2, 23.2 and 16.9 s.u. versus measured 31.1, 19.8, 19.3 and 13.8 s.u. Time-series scans of sample 109S (35 min each, up to 145 h) showed fluid moving from high to low saturation, first into partially filled smaller pores.

## Application of Compressed Sensing MRI to Laboratory Core-Floods

- ID: SCA2015-041, year: 2015
- Authors: N.P. Ramskill, I. Bush, A.J. Sederman, M.D. Mantle, L.F. Gladden
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: compressed sensing MRI, RARE pulse sequence, 3D saturation imaging, core flood, Bentheimer sandstone, temporal resolution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-041.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-041)

Compressed sensing combined with RARE MRI gives 3D fluid images of a Bentheimer core flood in 16 minutes instead of over 2 hours, an eightfold speedup.

Conventional 3D MRI is too slow to follow dynamic displacements in core floods. The authors combine compressed sensing (CS) with the RARE pulse sequence at 2 T to image a Bentheimer sandstone plug (38 mm by 68 mm, about 24% porosity). CS simulations on a fully sampled reference show that a 30% sampled reconstruction recovers contrast and is visually close to the full image. With 25% sampling, acquisition took about 16 minutes versus 2 h 9 min for full sampling, an eightfold gain, and about 60 hours for a standard spin-echo sequence. In a water-water displacement with 8 mM GdCl3 solution at 0.025 ml/min, 3D images from 82 to 574 minutes show the advancing invasion front. Quantitative assessment of the reconstruction is deferred to future work.

## Nuclear magnetic resonance laboratory study of a tight sandstone for robust permeability prediction

- ID: SCA2022-54, year: 2022
- Authors: Jun Gao, Hyung Kwak, Abdullah Alkhaldi, Gabor Hursan
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2cutoff, Timur-Coates permeability, tight gas sandstone, irreducible saturation, desaturation pressure, BVI BVM
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-54.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-54)

Presents a lab NMR procedure fitting T2cutoff versus desaturation pressure and optimizing Timur-Coates parameters for tight gas sandstones, validated in four wells.

NMR log interpretation in tight sandstones needs core-based calibration of T2cutoff and Timur-Coates permeability parameters, but desaturation to irreducible saturation is hard to achieve and usually found by trial and error. The authors present an experimental and analysis procedure using cores from two tight gas wells, desaturated at different pressures. A modified exponential model fits T2cutoff versus desaturation pressure to obtain an asymptotic T2cutoff at irreducible saturation. Average measured T2cutoffs of 16.2 and 32.1 ms for two sample groups converge to about 15 ms. Timur-Coates parameters (C, m, n) for effective and total porosity are obtained by optimization that minimizes deviation from the 1:1 line of measured versus predicted permeability. The resulting models were applied to four tight gas wells and agreed well with production and laboratory data.

## NMR Imaging of Fluid Saturation Distributions in Cores

- ID: SCA1987-14, year: 1987
- Authors: B. A. Baldwin, W. S. Yamanashi
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, centrifuge saturation gradient, fluid redistribution, Berea sandstone, chalk, relaxation time
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1987/SCA1987-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1987-14)

A medical NMR imager is used to map fluid saturation, heterogeneity and post-centrifuge redistribution in Berea, chalk and shaly sandstone cores, showing slow redistribution and relaxation-dependent image intensity.

The paper applies medical NMR imaging (0.5 T, 21.3 MHz, TE 26 ms, 1.2 mm/pixel) to Berea sandstone, chalk and shaly sandstone plugs saturated with brine or heptadecane. Images revealed mm-scale heterogeneity in Berea, finer scale in chalk, an undersaturated chalk center (about 80% saturation) that gravimetry missed, and internal cemented burrow structure confirmed by slabbing. After centrifuging, NMR intensity needed a calibration from porous-plate desaturation to agree with gravimetric saturation, since larger pores desaturate first. Heptadecane redistributed in Berea over about 216 hours but in chalk did not equilibrate even after 92 to 330 days, while brine showed no movement over 19 days; the Hassler 100% saturation at the plug bottom was not achieved. Water flooding of residual heptadecane in water-wet Berea moved oil from surfaces and small pores into large pores within 48 hours, and a chlorite- and magnetite-bearing shaly sandstone could be imaged only at a 2 ms echo time.

## NMR Imaging: Principles and Recent Progress by

- ID: SCA1988-12, year: 1988
- Authors: Leo D. Brown, GE NMR Instruments
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: NMR imaging, shielded gradients, spin echo, T1 and T2 relaxation, chemical shift, eddy currents
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1988/SCA1988-12.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1988-12)

Tutorial on NMR imaging physics and pulse sequences for core analysts, with shielded-gradient advances shown on a phantom (25 micron resolution) and a limestone core.

The paper introduces MRI to core analysts, comparing it with x-ray CT: MRI sees only fluids, and measures proton density, T1, T2, chemical shift and flow, which relate to porosity and oil and water saturation. It explains NMR fundamentals using the rotating frame model, inversion recovery and spin echo sequences, and gradient-based spatial encoding with Fourier transformation. Recent shielded gradient technology reduces eddy currents and gradient settling, improving signal-to-noise and resolution. A test phantom in a 5 mm tube showed 25 micron in-plane resolution with 1 mm slices. A limestone core was imaged with 1/3 x 1/3 x 2/3 mm voxels, over one hundred slices in about 110 minutes at a 2.2 ms echo time. Other nuclei (13C, 23Na, 2H, 19F) are discussed as ways to separate oil and water, measure salinity, or trace filtrate invasion.

## Appliciations of Magnetic Resonance Imaging in Special Core Analysis Studies

- ID: SCA1990-16EURO, year: 1990
- Authors: J. Lewis A. Williams, Paulinus M. Enwere, John S. Archer, David G. Taylor
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: magnetic resonance imaging, chemical shift imaging, saturation distribution, waterflood channelling, T1 T2 relaxation, Portland limestone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-16EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-16EURO)

Chemical shift MRI at 0.4 T images oil and water distributions in limestone, sandstone and bead-pack cores, including static, centrifuge and low-rate waterflood experiments.

The paper evaluates magnetic resonance imaging, especially chemical shift imaging (CSI), as a tool for mapping fluid saturations in special core analysis. Experiments used a 0.4 T system with voxels of about 0.94 x 0.94 x 4.5 mm on 25.4 mm Portland limestone (12%, 30 md), Lochaline sandstone (20%, 3000 md) and glass bead packs. Oil (n-decane) and water are separable by a roughly 3.7 ppm chemical shift. Line broadening by the rock is strong in some sandstones (linewidths up to 18.6 ppm). 1-D profiles take about 4.5 min and 2-D 64x64 images about 4.5 h. A low-rate waterflood (10 cm/day) in Portland limestone showed strong water channelling that persisted on repeat flooding and through to residual oil, and a Xanthan polymer bank could be imaged. Irreducible water after centrifuging (0.35 PV) was imaged, but short T2 signals were underestimated at a 40 ms echo time.

## Net Pay Application for Nuclear Magnetic Resonance, Nugget Sandstone, Wyoming Thrust Belt

- ID: SCA1990-26, year: 1990
- Authors: William J. Sercombe
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR bound fluid porosity, net pay, fractional flow, connate water saturation, Nugget sandstone, Buckles number
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-26)

An NMR-based net pay method combining relative permeability and capillary pressure partitions Nugget sandstone porosity to predict water cut and deliverability on log strip charts.

Poor and inconsistent production from low-porosity (<8%) eolian Nugget sandstone reservoirs in three Wyoming thrust belt fields could not be explained by facies or core permeability. Deliverability differences were attributed to natural open fractures (Anschutz wells with effective permeability of 8 md/ft versus 0.05 md/ft at Bessie Bottom) and connate water saturation effects. A net pay method was developed that links NMR bound/free fluid porosity to water saturations chosen from relative permeability, fractional flow and mercury capillary pressure data. Linear regressions on core samples gave free fluid = 0.67*porosity − 1.1 at 40% Sw (first water cut) and 0.41*porosity − 1 at 70% Sw (80% fractional flow). Overlaying porosity*Sw against the bound-fluid curves on a log strip chart separates water-free, increasing water cut and water-productive zones, and would have predicted Bessie Bottom performance.

## Proton MR Two - Component Chemical Shift Imaging of Fluids in Porous Media

- ID: SCA1991-14, year: 1991
- Authors: C.T. Chang, C.M. Edwards, Texas A
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: chemical shift imaging, NMR imaging, water-oil separation, phase correction, Bentheim sandstone, oil flooding
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-14)

A modified Dixon-type spin-warp chemical shift imaging method with phase correction separates water and oil images quantitatively in sand packs and cores, even with overlapping resonance lines.

Relaxation-based and spectrally selective MRI methods struggle to separate water and oil in porous media when resonance lines overlap and signals decay quickly. The authors improve Horsfield's phase-shift technique by acquiring in-phase and opposed-phase images to map field inhomogeneity and susceptibility phase errors, then acquiring ±90° phase-shifted images that give separated components by vector sum and difference. Tests used a water/machine-oil Ottawa sand pack (3.5 ppm shift, 2.4 ppm linewidth), Bentheim sandstone cores (23% porosity) saturated with brine, Soltrol 130 or both, and a carbonate core during oil flooding at 0.1 ml/min. In Bentheim cores, cross-contamination in the single-fluid cores was under 4%, and the combined water and oil signal in the mixed core agreed with the ordinary image. The carbonate flood gave time-lapse water and oil saturation maps with well-behaved displacement fronts. A limitation is a noisy phase map where water and oil signals are about equal.

## Quantitative Porosity Mapping of Reservoir Rock Cores by Physically Slice Selected NMR

- ID: SCA1992-01EURO, year: 1992
- Authors: Alun J. Lucas, Greg K. Pierens, Mark Peyron, T. Adrian Carpenter, Laurance D. Hall, Robert C. Stewart, Daniel W. Phelps, Gary F. Potter, Herchel Smith, Robinson Way
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR porosity profile, physical slice selection, B1 field map, stretched exponential decay, long core samples, gravimetric porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-01EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-01EURO)

A physically slice-selected NMR probe maps axial porosity profiles in long brine-saturated cores, agreeing with gravimetric porosity within about 0.5 porosity units for homogeneous cores.

The paper develops quantitative physical slice selection NMR for porosity profiling of long cores, which exceed the size of a typical NMR probe. A 100 mm diameter short-strut probe with 30 mm slice thickness (and an RF-screened variant) was built, and its slice profiles and B1 maps were characterized at 0.66 T. A pulse-acquire protocol with stretched-exponential extrapolation over the receiver deadtime was calibrated against a MnCl2 water reference. Berea sandstone gave 13.0±0.3% NMR porosity versus 13.4±0.1% gravimetric, and Bedford limestone 13.8±0.3% versus 13.3±0.1%. A five-core composite gave 19.8±0.6% versus 20.8±0.1%, and the smoothed profile was reproduced by convolving the slice profile with the gravimetric porosity profile.

## Particle Filtration in Sandstone Cores: Application of a Chemical Shift Imaging Technique

- ID: SCA1992-17, year: 1992
- Authors: Christian Straley, Dan Rossini, Lawrence M. Schwartz
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: chemical shift imaging, NMR T1 relaxation, drilling mud invasion, bentonite particle filtration, Berea sandstone, inside-out NMR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-17.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-17)

Phase-encode (chemical shift) MRI maps T1 profiles in sandstone cores after drilling mud invasion; spud muds depress T1 deeply, while polymer-gel or barite muds largely do not.

Borehole NMR T1 can be corrupted by invasion of submicron clay particles from drilling muds. Because internal field gradients and a roughly 7200 Hz linewidth in Berea sandstone would limit readout-gradient MRI to about 8.5 mm resolution, the authors used a phase-encode chemical shift sequence with TE of about 2 ms, giving millimeter resolution and sensitivity to about 70% of pore water. Berea 100, Berea 600 and Clashach cores were invaded with bentonite spud muds, gel, polymer and barite-weighted muds in a modified Baroid OFI mud press. T1 was measured before and after by imaging and by an inside-out spectrometer probing about 2 cm depth. Spud mud cut inflow-end T1 by 35% in Berea 100 and 65% in Berea 600, and about 50% in Clashach, with a T1 minimum deep in the core from an initial particle spurt. Gel, polymer and barite muds changed T1 only a few percent, and the imaging and inside-out results agreed qualitatively.

## NMR Imaging for Core Flood Testing

- ID: SCA1992-18, year: 1992
- Authors: J. S. Archer
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, saturation profiles, capillary pressure gradient, porosity distribution, chemical shift imaging, Portland limestone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-18.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-18)

NMR imaging maps porosity and oil/water saturation in sandstone and limestone plugs, tracks 1-D saturation profiles during flooding, and uses them to estimate capillary pressure gradients.

Core flood interpretation usually assumes homogeneous cores and negligible capillary pressure gradients. The authors used a 0.4 T, 17.12 MHz medical MRI system with chemical shift imaging (0.94 mm resolution, 4-mm slice) on Clashach and Lochaline sandstones and Portland limestone. Porosity images showed significant non-uniformity even in sandstones regarded as homogeneous; Portland limestone porosity ranged about 0.02-0.3 in 2-D and 0.12-0.2 along a 1-D profile. Separate oil and water images in limestone showed poor water distribution and discontinuous residual oil. During a low-rate (3.6 cm/day) drainage flood, 1-D profiles tracked the front until breakthrough. An imbibition saturation profile combined with the capillary pressure curve gave capillary pressure gradients of about 10-19 kPa/cm, comparable to the phase pressure gradients. Limitations include long imaging times, which restrict flow rate, and susceptibility effects that may prevent oil and water spectra from being resolved in some rocks.

## NMR Microscopy for Fluid Imaging at Pore Scale in Reservoir Rock

- ID: SCA1992-22, year: 1992
- Authors: Daryl A. Doughty, Liviu Tomutsa
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR microscopy, 3D projection reconstruction, pore-scale fluid imaging, T2 relaxation, Bentheim sandstone, residual oil saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-22)

A modified high-field NMR spectrometer using 3D back-projection imaging produces pore-scale images of brine and oil in sandstone microplugs at about 40 micron resolution.

Fluids in reservoir rock have very short T2 (0.2-1 ms at 270 MHz) because of paramagnetic minerals, which rules out most slice-selective NMR imaging sequences. The authors modified a JEOL GX270 spectrometer with a 170 Gauss/cm gradient coil, a new solenoidal probe (2 microsec 90-degree pulses) and a 3D projection-reconstruction protocol with 65536 projections. Bead packs were imaged at 15-30 micron pixels, and Bentheim sandstone microplugs (5.3 mm diameter) were imaged at 40 micron resolution saturated with MnCl2-doped brine, then with Soltrol oil at initial saturation and after waterflooding. Brine appeared as isolated droplets at initial oil saturation, while at residual oil some oil was connected and some floated as droplets in pore centers. Each oil image took about 48 hours, so only end-point saturations can be imaged; Bartlesville sandstone was also imaged single-phase.

## A Multi-Laboratory Comparison of Isotherm Measurements on Antrim Shale Samples, Otsego County, Mi

- ID: SCA1993-03, year: 1993
- Authors: D. E. Lancaster, S. A. Holditch, D. G. Hill
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: methane adsorption isotherm, Antrim Shale, interlaboratory comparison, Langmuir parameters, gas shale, equilibrium moisture
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-03)

Six laboratories measured methane isotherms on four Antrim Shale samples; after resolving reporting differences, results still differed by 25-50%, and no standard protocol exists.

GRI coordinated the first systematic multi-laboratory comparison of methane isotherm measurements on four crushed Antrim Shale samples (Lachine, Paxton, Norwood) from the Nomeco Bagley East B3-11 well. Six labs (five volumetric, one gravimetric) were asked to measure isotherms at 75°F up to 1,000 psia, dry and at equilibrium moisture. Initial results at 450 psia ranged from about 20 to 125 scf/ton for Sample 1, because labs differed in reporting total versus adsorbed methane, dry versus as-received conditions, standard conditions and ton definitions. After these were resolved, isotherms still differed by 25 to 50% or more, and reported Langmuir parameters and porosities varied widely (porosity by 15-20%). Identified sources of error include sample preparation, cell calibration, temperature control, bulk density measurement and equilibrium criteria, and methane content increased with TOC in all labs. The authors conclude that no standard isotherm protocol exists and that users must understand what is reported, since using total or dry-sample isotherms can overstate gas in place.

## The Measurement of Fluid Velocities in Porous Media

- ID: SCA1993-10, year: 1993
- Authors: Carl M. Edwards, Chii-Tzong Chang, Subhendra Sarkar, Texas A
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR velocity imaging, pulsed field gradient, averaged propagator, stimulated echo, Bentheim sandstone, velocity distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-10.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-10)

PFG stimulated-echo NMR imaging maps average velocity and velocity distributions in bead packs and Bentheim sandstone, showing results depend strongly on observation time, diffusion and narrow-pulse validity.

The paper addresses how to measure fluid transport, not just storage, in porous media using NMR pulsed-field-gradient velocity imaging. Single-phase water (and Soltrol-130) flow was imaged at a 2T system in a 0.2 mm glass bead pack and a Bentheim sandstone using stimulated echo sequences. The measured quantity is the averaged propagator, which becomes a velocity distribution only if a time-averaged velocity is defined, and it depends strongly on observation time. Random-walk simulations between parallel plates define upper (Δ << a²/2D) and lower (Δ >> 2D/v²) limits on observation time, which are hard to meet for water at reservoir flow rates; a more viscous fluid helps. Velocity images agreed with Dupuit-Forchheimer velocity in the bead pack (0.21 mm/s) but were 30% lower in Bentheim (0.19 mm/s), and Bentheim showed a large stagnant fraction. Average velocity from phase shift is reliable only for small phase and narrow gradient pulses.

## NMR Imaging Application to Carbon Dioxide Miscible Flooding in West Texas Carbonates

- ID: SCA1993-11, year: 1993
- Authors: R. D. Hazlett, J. W. Gleeson, H. Laali, R. Navarro
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR imaging, CO2 miscible flooding, Wolfcamp limestone, residual oil distribution, in-situ polymerization, bypassing
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-11.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-11)

NMR imaging and in-situ styrene polymerization of Wolfcamp limestone cores after CO2 miscible floods show core-scale heterogeneity and bypassing, not median relaxation time, control residual oil.

Median NMR relaxation time correlated with CO2 miscible displacement efficiency in San Andres dolomite, but not in Wolfcamp limestone. Gravity-stable CO2 floods were run on Wolfcamp cores (end-point oil saturations 15-26%), then 1 mm 3D NMR images of porosity, remaining oil and T1 were acquired. Residual oil was found stranded downstream of low-porosity or low-permeability features such as laminations, bioturbation-related cemented zones and fusulinid foraminifera tests. In-situ polymerization of dyed styrene showed patchy well- and poorly-swept domains tied to early marine cement and foram chambers. Bypassing was the main stranding mechanism, and core-scale heterogeneity explained the distributions.

## Predicting Permeability from Nuclear Magnetic Resonance and Electrical Properties Measurements

- ID: SCA1993-12, year: 1993
- Authors: G. A. LaTorraca, K. J. Dunn, R. J. S. Brown
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T1 relaxation, permeability prediction, formation factor, geometric mean T1, NMR porosity, brine saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-12.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-12)

NMR T1 geometric mean combined with formation factor predicts clastic permeability within a factor of 3.2 (2 for a single field), plus porosity and saturation from NMR amplitudes.

The study evaluates published NMR-based permeability predictors (Kenyon, Sen, Borgia) using a Chevron dataset of 260 clastic samples, with the amplitude-weighted geometric mean T1 (T1G) replacing the stretched-exponential T1. T1G and the stretched exponential T1 are linearly related (r²=0.993). The best predictor, (T1G/F)², gave air permeability within a factor of 3.2 across 7 reservoirs, and within a factor of 2 for a 57-sample single-field subset. NMR porosity matched Archimedes porosity with a standard deviation of 1.07 p.u. for 278 clastic and carbonate samples, and NMR brine saturation matched gravimetric saturation within 3.2 saturation units for 28 samples. For 5 chalk samples, the product of Sw and average relaxation rate equaled the fully saturated rate, though this requires adequate short-time sampling of the decay.

## The Use of Magnetic Resonance Spectroscopy to Probe Microscopic Structures and Fluid Distributions in Porous Media

- ID: SCA1994-03, year: 1994
- Authors: Hsie-Keng Liaw, Ra.ghavendra Kulkarni, A. Ted Watson
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR spin-lattice relaxation, Tikhonov regularization, surface relaxivity, restricted diffusion, pore-size distribution, B-spline
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1994/SCA1994-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1994-03)

A B-spline, Tikhonov-regularized inversion of NMR T1 data, with surface relaxivity calibrated by NMR diffusion V/S, gives pore-size and fluid distributions in sandstones and limestone.

Conventional pore-structure methods (mercury porosimetry, nitrogen adsorption, thin sections) are destructive and poorly suited to multiphase systems, so the authors develop an NMR spin-lattice relaxation approach. The T1 distribution is recovered from inversion-recovery data by representing it with B-splines and solving a nonnegativity-constrained, second-derivative Tikhonov problem, with the regularization parameter set to the largest value that does not degrade the fit. Surface relaxivity is obtained by comparing average relaxation time with pore volume-to-surface area ratios (V/S) from short-time restricted diffusion measurements, which agreed with values calculated for glass bead packs. For Bentheimer, Berea and Texas Cream limestone, surface relaxivities were 9.3, 9.8 and 7.1 µm/s and V/S were 11.6, 6.9 and 3.5 µm. Pore-size distributions were consistent with thin-section observations, and the maximum V/S of about 30 µm satisfied the fast-exchange condition. Distributions at partial saturation showed water leaving the large pores first during drainage.

## Effects of Magnetic Susceptibility Contrasts on Oil and Water Saturation Determinations by NMR Tz Laboratory and Well Log Measurements

- ID: SCA1996-19, year: 1996
- Authors: Gerald A. LaTorraca, La Habra
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, magnetic susceptibility, induced gradients, chlorite, brine saturation, echo spacing
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-19.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-19)

Paramagnetic ferric iron in chlorite induces pore-scale gradients that shorten NMR T2 of brine and oil, distorting saturations; T1 matches Dean-Stark and is preferred in the lab.

Magnetic susceptibility contrasts between paramagnetic grain coatings and pore fluids induce internal field gradients that shorten T2 through diffusion, biasing NMR saturation and permeability estimates. Ten oil-preserved North Sea core plugs were measured at 2 MHz (TE 0.35 ms) and compared with Dean-Stark; T1 saturations matched across the range while T2 deviated at high brine saturation. A chlorite-rich sample saturated with brine and decane gave 31% brine from both T1 and T2 (TE 0.2 ms) versus 34% by weight, but T2 at TE 0.5 ms had no clear peak separation. Magnetic susceptibility correlated strongly with ferric oxide and chlorite content and inversely with permeability. In logs from a gas field drilled with oil-based mud, the oil filtrate T2 peak dropped from 400 to 250 ms and the brine peak from about 10 to 8 ms in the chlorite-rich zone, explaining failure of saturation and permeability algorithms there.

## The Use of NMR Spectroscopy to Validate NMR Logs from Deeply Buried Reservoir Sandstones

- ID: SCA1996-21, year: 1996
- Authors: H. Rueslbtten, T. Eidesmo, K.A. Lehne, O.M. Relling
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR logging MRIL, T1 and T2 relaxation, chlorite, wettability, bound volume irreducible, permeability estimation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-21)

Laboratory NMR on five deeply buried sandstone plugs validates MRIL-C logs, showing chlorite and wettability effects on relaxation and oil-related porosity and permeability underestimation.

A deeply buried Lower Jurassic oil sandstone offshore Mid Norway was logged with the MRIL-C tool, and the logs were compared with standard logs and core data. Five plugs from chlorite-rich Formation A and asphalt-stained Formation B were measured with laboratory T1 and T2 NMR at Swi and Sor with crude oil and at Swi with n-decane. NMR porosity matched helium porosity closely (e.g., 0.194 vs 0.200). Long T1 components indicated water-wet A and mixed-wet B samples. Chlorite shortened the long T2 components of decane through internal field gradients and diffusion. MRIL porosity was under-estimated by about 5 PU in oil-bearing zones, growing with oil saturation, which led to permeability under-estimation. The mixed-wet Formation B gave lower BVI, partly compensating, and the BVI/MPHI ratio is proposed as a wettability indicator.

## MR Laboratory Measurements: Requirements to Assure Successful Measurements That Will Enhance MRI Log Interpretation

- ID: SCA1997-04, year: 1997
- Authors: Dave Marschall, John Gardner, Federica Manni
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, bulk volume irreducible, T2 cutoff, interecho spacing, NMR permeability model, internal gradients
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-04.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-04)

Provides guidelines for planning low-field NMR core measurements, from sample selection and desaturation to acquisition settings, and shows how lab T2 data calibrate BVI and permeability models for logs.

The paper gives practical guidance for designing low-field MR laboratory projects that support MR log interpretation. It covers sample selection and preparation, brine saturation, a test sequence with brine-saturated and air-brine desaturated states at several interecho spacings, and choosing a desaturation pressure to represent BVI. A worked example shows how wait time, interecho spacing, pulsing time and signal-to-noise, if set wrongly, distort T2 distributions, for example turning a bimodal distribution into an apparent unimodal one. Examples interpret micropore/clay-bound water, internal gradients from Fe-bearing minerals, multiphase effects and wettability change. Lab data are used to calibrate cutoff T2 BVI models and permeability models (Straley average-T2, Coates FFI/BVI with slope 6.2 in the example), with strengths and weaknesses compared in a table.

## Blending Magnetic Resonance Imaging and Numerical Simulation

- ID: SCA1998-19, year: 1998
- Authors: S. Nicula, A. Brancolini, A. Cominelli, S. Mantica
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: MRI core imaging, CPMG T2 distribution, J-function capillary pressure scaling, ECLIPSE displacement simulation, NMR permeability correlation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-19.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-19)

2D MRI porosity, permeability and saturation maps from a water-oil core displacement initialize ECLIPSE simulations; scaling local capillary pressure reproduces the observed front and breakthrough time.

The paper uses 2D MRI to quantify porosity, permeability and water saturation during a displacement in a 3.9 cm by 5.3 cm sandstone plug (29% porosity, 37 mD). Water (60 g/l brine) was displaced by a fluorinated oil of 20 cp at 0.05 cc/min, imaging a sagittal slice every 20 min with a CPMG multi-slice multi-echo sequence (TE 4.8 ms) and a continuous T2 distribution fit per pixel. MRI saturation differed from gravimetric values by 3 to 11%. The front split and advanced faster through the high-permeability region. ECLIPSE 100 on a 79x54 grid built from the MRI maps, with uniform Pc and kr, gave breakthrough at 160 min versus 175 min observed. Scaling Pc locally with the J-function matched the 175 min breakthrough and the final front, with simulated average Sw of 33% versus 35% measured. Additionally scaling Swi gave breakthrough at 150 min and an average Sw of 38%.

## Diffusion in Internal Field Gradients

- ID: SCA1998-23, year: 1998
- Authors: Gigi Qian Zhang, George J. Hirasaki, Waylon V. House
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: internal field gradient, NMR T2 diffusion relaxation, chlorite, magnetic susceptibility, echo spacing, North Burbank sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-23)

Chlorite-coated North Burbank sandstone and chlorite powderpacks show strong internal-gradient diffusion shortening of T2 with echo spacing, unlike Berea and kaolinite; a flake-microchannel model explains the gradients.

Chlorite-coated sandstones may violate default NMR assumptions because iron-rich chlorite creates strong internal field gradients. T1 and T2 (TE 0.2 and 2 ms) were measured at 2 MHz on Berea and North Burbank sandstones with brine, Soltrol 130 and SMY crude, and on chlorite and kaolinite powderpacks saturated with brine, hexane, Soltrol and crude. North Burbank shows strong echo-spacing dependent T2 shortening, even of the oil peak, while T1 stays at bulk values, so it is not a wettability change; Berea shows negligible effect. Gradients from 1/T2 vs t² slopes are an order of magnitude larger for chlorite than kaolinite and far exceed logging-tool applied gradients. A model of chlorite flakes forming microchannels gives maximum gradients G = ΔχB0/R consistent with the measured orders of magnitude. Crude oil shows little diffusion effect because of low diffusivity and fast bulk relaxation.

## Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Versus Air/Brine Capillary Pressure Permeability Correlations : How Good Are Our Assumptions in the Empirical Determination of Permeability ?

- ID: SCA1998-24, year: 1998
- Authors: J.M. Hawkins, R.A. Skopec, Petrophysical Applications International
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR permeability, Swanson correlation, Timur-Coates equation, air/brine capillary pressure, Rapoport and Leas, surface relaxivity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-24)

Tests Timur, Coates, Swanson, Rapoport-Leas and a new critical-saturation permeability correlation on 160 air/brine capillary pressure curves; Swanson and Rapoport-Leas perform best, and NMR analogs struggle in shaly sands.

NMR permeability correlations derive from capillary pressure work, so the authors tested them on 160 air/brine capillary pressure curves from sandpacks and twenty sandstone fields (0.02 to 22,900 md, 124 ambient and 36 stressed samples). Swanson's and Rapoport-Leas's equations predicted permeability with a standard error of about a factor of two without adjusting constants. Timur and Coates overestimated permeability, especially at low values, with standard errors of about 3.7 to 3.9. A new critical-saturation equation using Corey parameters and krw=0.01 (a=674, b=1.82) matched Swanson's accuracy. Ambient and stressed data were statistically indistinguishable for all models. In a very shaly sand case with six plugs, NMR analogs of Swanson, critical saturation and Seevers performed poorly, likely because surface relaxivity was high (median 48 µm/s) and variable. Adjusted Timur and Coates models with a 6 ms T2 cutoff worked best.

## To Study the Mechanism of Waterflooding in Sand Rocks

- ID: SCA1998-31, year: 1998
- Authors: Using NMR Techniques
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, pore size distribution, waterflooding, surface relaxivity, moveable oil cutoff, mercury injection
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-31)

Low-field NMR T2 spectra, scaled to pore size with image analysis and mercury injection, track oil recovery by pore size during D2O waterflooding of sandstone plugs.

The paper uses low-field NMR (5 MHz, CPMG) to follow oil displacement by D2O in four sandstone core plugs, so that only oil signal is detected. T2 distributions were converted to pore size distributions using a surface relaxivity of about 0.1, calibrated against petrographic image analysis and mercury injection data. NMR was measured at irreducible water saturation and after 0.1, 0.3, 1.5 and 3.0 PV of injection. Oil in pores below a cutoff size (2.58 for plug No.1) was not displaced, which allows moveable oil percentage to be computed. Large pores reach a fixed recovery quickly, medium pores recover steadily, and small pores recover slowly, leaving residual oil mainly in medium and small pores.

## Fluid Identification in Heavy Oil Reservoirs by Nuclear Magnetic Resonance at Elevated Temperature

- ID: SCA1999-17, year: 1999
- Authors: Matthias Appel, J. Justin Freeman, Rod B. Perkins
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, heavy oil, temperature dependence, activation energy, bulk oil viscosity, steam flood
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-17.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-17)

Temperature-dependent T2 NMR on heavy oil core and bulk oil identifies oil and bound brine peaks via activation energies; oil signal stays below ~30 ms at 210F.

Wireline NMR interpretation in a San Joaquin Valley steamflood heavy oil reservoir is hard because oil T2 overlaps capillary-bound brine and diffusion contrast fails owing to restricted diffusion. T2 spectra were measured at 2 MHz on a compacted unconsolidated core plug (~35% porosity) and on bulk produced oil from 75F to 210F. Core spectra showed three peaks that merged with temperature; peaks were decomposed with log-normal fits and Arrhenius analysis gave activation energies, with bulk oil peaks having higher values than the oil peaks in core. This allowed assignment of the two short-T2 core peaks to oil and the long-T2 peak to capillary-bound brine, with little oil signal beyond 30 ms even at 210F. Viscosity from standard T2 correlations failed above ~500 cp, but the ratio of the two bulk-oil peak areas correlated exponentially with measured viscosity. NMR-derived bound water was under 10% of pore volume versus 25% from routine analysis, a discrepancy left unresolved.

## Estimation of Capillary Bound Water in Carbonate Reservoir Samples by NMR Imaging and Relaxation Measurements

- ID: SCA1999-47, year: 1999
- Authors: Leslie Randall, Ken Green, Tim Prichard
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 cut-off, capillary bound water, vuggy dolostone, porous plate desaturation, centrifuge saturation gradient, NMR permeability prediction
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-47.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-47)

Low-field NMR relaxation and NMR imaging on 35 vuggy dolostone plugs show good porosity prediction but very low bound-water cut-offs (<30 ms) and poor NMR permeability predictions.

The study evaluates NMR logging interpretation for a vuggy sucrosic dolostone reservoir using 35 plugs measured with 2 MHz low-field CPMG relaxation and 2.35 T NMR imaging. Centrifuge desaturation (4000 rpm) produced saturation gradients in two of three samples that persisted up to two weeks, so porous plate desaturation at 100 psi was used instead, requiring 60 days. Resulting capillary bound water saturations were abnormally low relative to porosity and permeability, confirmed by mercury injection, giving T2 cut-offs mostly below 30 ms and some below 10 ms. NMR porosity matched gravimetric porosity well (r = 0.97), and vug porosity from a 750 ms cut-off agreed reasonably with high-field results. SDR (r = 0.69), vug-removed SDR (r = 0.56) and Coates (r = 0.61) permeability predictions were no better than porosity-permeability correlation (r = 0.71).

## Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Log Evaluation of Low Resistivity Sandstone Reservoirs by-Passed by Conventional Logging Analysis

- ID: SCA2000-50, year: 2000
- Authors: G.M. Hamada, M.S. Al-Blehed, M.N.J. King
- Method: field or reservoir case study
- Keywords: NMR logging, low resistivity pay, modified differential spectrum, T1/T2 fluid typing, bound water, irreducible water saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2000/SCA2000-50.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2000-50)

NMR logging identifies productive zones in low-resistivity and low-contrast-resistivity sandstone reservoirs that conventional resistivity-based log analysis misses, shown with two field examples.

Conventional density, neutron and resistivity logs struggle in low resistivity and low resistivity-contrast sandstone reservoirs. The paper uses NMR log data (T1/T2 ratio, relaxation properties, NMR versus total porosity) to identify fluids, bound versus free water, and lithology-independent porosity. In a Kharita Formation gas well in Egypt, resistivity of about 1 ohm-m implied 80-90% water saturation, yet the well produced water-free hydrocarbon. NMR with a 33 ms cutoff showed considerable free fluid below a key depth, and true porosity was about 25 p.u. while the porosity logs read about 10 p.u. In a Saudi Arabian low-contrast reservoir, the modified differential spectrum isolated oil and water signals, and formation tester samples confirmed oil in all three sands.

## Complementary Methods for Characterising Slick Rock Aeolian Sandstone

- ID: SCA2001-42, year: 2001
- Authors: S. Baraka-Lokmane, I. Main, S. Elphick, B. Ngwenya
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: Slick Rock sandstone, X-ray CT, ESEM wettability, NMR iron interference, point counting, CW-MRI
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-42.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-42)

Multiple complementary techniques (CT, ESEM, petrography, XRD, XRF, NMR/MRI) characterise Slick Rock sandstone; iron content defeats NMR/MRI but CT confirms its heterogeneous distribution.

Sixteen Slick Rock Aeolian sandstone samples were characterised before brine/oil flow tests to link microstructure to permeability changes. Methods included CW-MRI, NMR, X-ray CT, ESEM contact angles, thin-section point counting, particle size analysis, XRD and XRF. Conventional MRI and NMR failed because heterogeneous iron (hematite, under 1%) shortened relaxation times, though CW-MRI imaged a capillary water front. CT revealed heterogeneous calcite cement, iron banding and a calcite-filled fracture perpendicular to the core axis in sample 13. Point-counted 2-D porosity was 20-23% with cement 5-18%, image analysis gave similar values, and grains were well sorted (So<1.4) with mean size about 204-217 µm. ESEM showed fresh quartz and feldspar water-wet and uncemented quartz intermediate-wet; the methods were mutually consistent.

## A Comparative Study of Reservoir Pore Structure by 2D and 3D Image Analysis, Mercury Porosimetry and NMR Relaxometry

- ID: SCA2001-59, year: 2001
- Authors: O. Onasanya, S. Olayinka, M.A. Ioannidis, I. Chatzis
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, surface relaxivity, stretched exponential model, SEM image analysis, autocorrelation function, multi-exponential inversion
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-59.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-59)

NMR T2 relaxation of glass bead, sandstone and dolomite samples is compared with SEM image analysis and mercury porosimetry, giving a surface relaxivity near 100 mm/s for fused beads.

The study addresses uncertainties in converting NMR T2 distributions to pore size, including echo spacing and multi-exponential inversion choices. CPMG measurements at 0.6 T were made at Sw=1 and residual saturation on fused and packed glass beads, sandstones and dolomite, and inverted by NNLS and a simulated annealing algorithm or fitted with a modified stretched exponential model. Backscatter SEM images gave porosity, autocorrelation functions and specific surface area. For fused bead samples the surface relaxation strength was r = 100 ± 17 mm/s, higher than a literature value, and pore radii from T_D agreed with image correlation lengths. NMR and SEM porosities agreed, and T2 distributions from different inversions and instruments agreed when echo spacing was sufficiently small. Random-walk simulation in a 3D stochastic Berea replica broadly matched predictions from pore-size partitioning, with differences attributed to diffusional averaging.

## Review of the Winland R35 Method for Net Pay Definition and Its Application in Low Permeability Sands

- ID: SCA2001-63, year: 2001
- Authors: Mike Spearing, Tim Allen, Gavin McAulay
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: Winland R35, mercury injection, net pay cut-off, pore throat radius, tight gas sands, Sherwood Sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-63.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-63)

Tests the Winland R35 net-pay method on 40 Sherwood Sandstone samples using mercury injection; facies-specific correlations differ from Winland's but cut-off differences are modest.

The Winland R35 method estimates pore throat radius from porosity and permeability and uses a 0.5 µm cut-off for net pay, but its validity in low permeability gas sands is uncertain. Forty Sherwood Sandstone samples from two Morecambe Bay wells were tested by mercury intrusion to 60,000 psi and grouped by facies (fluvial, aeolian, sheet-flood) and permeability (<5 mD, >5 mD). All curves were sigmoid, so R35 can be applied physically, and Pittman's concern about the lack of a modal aperture was not borne out. New linear regressions for each sub-group differed significantly from Winland's equation and fitted poorly (best r² 0.853 for aeolian, others around 0.6), probably because of small sample sizes. Permeability cut-offs at 0.5 µm differed in some sub-groups (for sheetflow, 0.5 mD from Winland versus 1 mD from the new fit), and Winland's equation sometimes gave negative cut-offs. The authors question whether rock-type-specific correlations are worth the cost, since differences are small compared with those from choosing a different threshold radius.

## Evaluation of Filtration and Capacitive Properties of Rocks by Nuclear Magnetic Resonance in Terrigenous Non-Consolidated Section with Swelling Clay Minerals

- ID: SCA2001-64, year: 2001
- Authors: V.G. Toporkov, V.A. Murtsovkin, S.Yu. Rudakovskaya
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 cutoff, clay-bound water, swelling clays, NMR permeability model, irreducible water saturation, unconsolidated sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-64.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-64)

NMR relaxometry on frozen-prepared core from Western Siberian poorly cemented sandstones separates reservoir from clay-rich non-reservoir rocks and estimates porosity, Swirr and permeability.

The study uses NMR to evaluate net pay in poorly cemented Albian-Cenomanian sandstones, aleurolites and clays of Western Siberia, where porosity (30-40%) is similar in reservoir and non-reservoir rocks. Plugs were cut from core frozen in liquid nitrogen and encased in shrink plastic to avoid destroying them. Gas permeability of dry samples is misleading, since non-reservoir rocks with swelling clays show hundreds of mD dry but drop by a factor of 10^3-10^4 under confining pressure. Capillary pressure data showed residual water saturation of 75-90% in these rocks, and a 33 ms T2 cutoff best estimated Swirr. A 3 ms cutoff identifies clay-bound water, whose large amount marks non-reservoir rock. A new 3-D cubic capillary lattice model gives permeability from T2 spectra and agrees better with measured values than the Timur-Coates and Kenyon equations, but still overestimates permeability in non-reservoir rocks.

## An Evaluation of the Application of Low Field NMR in the Characterization of Carbonate Reservoirs

- ID: SCA2002-30, year: 2002
- Authors: An Mai, Apostolos Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 cutoff, carbonate pore connectivity, CT scanning, irreducible water saturation, T2 geometric mean, moveable fluid volume
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-30.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-30)

NMR T2cutoff values in about 80 Western Canadian carbonate plugs vary widely, correlate with spectrum features, and CT images link them to pore connectivity.

Sandstone-based NMR interpretation often fails in carbonates, where vugs and intercrystalline porosity give variable connectivity. About 80 plugs from six Western Canadian formations were measured by gas-expansion porosity, NMR (1 MHz, TE 0.3 ms) at full saturation and after centrifuging at 6000 rpm (~200 psi), and CT scanning. NMR and CT porosities agreed with gas expansion (slopes 1.09 and 0.99, R² 0.92 and 0.89). T2cutoff ranged from about 10 to 1000 ms and correlated with the geometric mean of the saturated spectrum, the fraction and geometric mean of the last peak, and the last-peak fraction at Swi, though with scatter. CT slices showed low T2cutoff (<100 ms) samples had high matrix porosity or well-connected vugs, while high T2cutoff (>100 ms) samples had tight matrix and poorly connected vugs.

## Strategy to Reduce Uncertainties in Core Analysis Programs Through the Use of Magnetic Resonance Imaging: A New Perspective

- ID: SCA2002-36, year: 2002
- Authors: Leslie Randall, Ken Green, Douglas Fisher, Jon Goldman, Tim Prichard
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: magnetic resonance imaging, carbonate heterogeneity, vug porosity, high-field NMR T2, core screening, deuterium oxide saturation profiles
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-36.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-36)

Thin-slice MRI screening of carbonate core plugs reveals vugs, fractures and heterogeneity non-destructively, and monitoring oil displacement of brine before aging can reject flow-anomalous plugs.

The paper summarizes 11 years of experience applying MRI to reduce uncertainty in core analysis, especially for carbonates. Measurements used a 2.35 T Bruker system with spin-echo imaging (about 5 ms echo time), thin 1-2 mm slices, long-echo-time vug imaging, high-field T2 relaxation and 1D saturation profiles with D2O brine. Carbonates have NMR linewidths below 300 Hz, whereas iron-rich sandstones exceed 2000 Hz and give blurred images. Full-volume imaging produces a "full moon" effect in carbonates, while thin slices resolve vugs, micro-porosity and fluid-filled versus mineralised fractures, with more detail than CT. High-field relaxation separates vuggy and microporous samples that look similar at low field. Monitoring oil displacement of brine before aging can screen out plugs with flow heterogeneity, and lengthwise scans take about 30 minutes per sample.

## Comparison of NMR Laboratory and Log Measurements in a Bitumen Sand

- ID: SCA2002-37, year: 2002
- Authors: M. Fleury, F. Deflandre, C. Salze, E. Cheruvier
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: bitumen sand, NMR T2 relaxation, CMR log, hydrogen index, water saturation, temperature-dependent NMR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-37)

Raw CMR log data and temperature-varied lab NMR on preserved bitumen sand give coherent bitumen signatures and porosity, but water saturation disagrees (24% log vs 6.6% lab).

NMR response of a very viscous bitumen (8° API, 13°C reservoir) lies at the detection limit of standard lab and logging instruments, so porosity is underestimated. Raw CMR B log data from a Surmont bitumen sand were reprocessed with MEA inversion, using both long and short (EPM) echo trains, and compared with Maran 2 Ultra lab T2 measurements on preserved plugs from 13 to 86°C. Short echo trains raised SNR from 9 to 25 and resolved the bitumen peak below 1 ms, matching the lab signature; differences were attributed to SNR. Lab porosity from the elevated-temperature method was 35.6 p.u. (37.4 p.u. combined with weight), consistent with density-neutron porosity (~35 p.u.), with water saturation 6.6% versus 24% from the log. Effective bitumen hydrogen index depended on temperature, SNR and echo spacing (about 0.39 lab, 0.29–0.37 log), and T1/T2 was ~27.

## Using Low Field NMR to Predict Viscosities of Crude Oils and Crude Oil Emulsions

- ID: SCA2002-39, year: 2002
- Authors: J. Bryan, A. Kantzas, C. Bellehumeur
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, heavy oil viscosity, bitumen, crude oil emulsions, relative hydrogen index, water cut
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-39.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-39)

Low-field NMR correlations using T2 geometric mean, relative hydrogen index and water fraction predict heavy oil, bitumen and emulsion viscosity to within an order of magnitude.

Conventional viscosity measurements become inaccurate for heavy oil and bitumen, so the authors test low-field (1 MHz) NMR T2 as an alternative. Viscosity was measured with Brookfield and Haake cone-and-plate rheometers on bitumens, oils and emulsions (12–52% water) at 25–80°C. A correlation using RHI and the geometric mean T2 of the oil peak fits 112 samples with R2 = 0.97, spanning about 1 cP to over 3,000,000 cP. Applied to emulsions with more than about 12% water, this model underestimates viscosity.
