Adding a water-fraction term, 1/(1−xw)^4.46, with water identified from later T2 peaks, gives order-of-magnitude emulsion predictions with R2 = 0.92. Droplet size effects were not included, as surface relaxivity was unknown.

## NMR Applications for the Determination of Sedimentary Facies and Porosity Systems in Carbonatic Core Plug Samples

- ID: SCA2002-42, year: 2002
- Authors: Neel Montoya, Pedro Romero, José Méndez
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, Timur-Coates permeability, T2 cut-off, carbonate porosity types, sedimentary facies, vuggy porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-42.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-42)

NMR T2 distributions of about 40 Venezuelan carbonate plugs show shapes characteristic of sedimentary facies and porosity types, enabling facies recognition and Timur-Coates permeability by facies.

The paper asks whether NMR T2 distributions can identify sedimentary facies and porosity systems in carbonate reservoir rock, beyond routine petrophysical estimates. About 40 one-inch plugs from the Escandalosa Formation, member O (Borburata field, Venezuela), were saturated with formation water and measured on a 2 MHz Maran Ultra at 100% saturation and at irreducible water saturation. NMR porosity correlated with gravimetric porosity at 91%, and Timur-Coates permeability, with facies-specific T2 cut-offs, agreed well with Klinkenberg permeability. T2 distributions showed one to four peaks depending on intracrystalline, intercrystalline, fracture, synsedimentary fracture, moldic and vuggy porosity, with vugs and fractures appearing beyond about 750 ms to 1 s. Cut-offs ranged from about 54 to 157 ms, and distribution shapes matched thin sections and facies, though the result is validated only for member O in one well.

## Determination of Rock Quality in Sandstone Core Plug Samples Using NMR

- ID: SCA2002-51, year: 2002
- Authors: Pedro Romero, Gabriela Bruzual, Ovidio Suárez
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, Timur-Coates permeability, FFI/BFV ratio, pore throat radius r40, Pittman equation, rock typing
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-51.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-51)

Rock quality classes of 42 Venezuelan sandstone plugs defined by pore throat radius r40 are shown to correspond to NMR FFI/BFV ratios, avoiding capillary pressure measurements.

Conventional rock quality classification relies on the dominant pore throat radius from mercury capillary pressure curves and Pittman equations, which is laborious. The authors measured NMR T2 at 100% water saturation and at irreducible saturation, plus capillary pressure and Klinkenberg permeability, on 42 El Furrial (Naricual) sandstone plugs. The apex method gave 40% Hg saturation as the dominant throat, and r40 gave the best Laplace-Pittman correlation (73%, versus 61% for r30 and 69% for r35). Calibrated Timur-Coates permeability is K = 0.1·φ^3.05·(FFI/BFV)^1.74; comparing it with the Pittman r40 equation shows log r40 is equivalent to FFI/BFV. Rock types map to FFI/BFV ranges (mega >12, macro 4-12, meso 1.5-4, micro 0.1-1.5) with characteristic T2 shapes, though the physical basis remains to be understood.

## Determining Clay Type by Using Low-Field Nuclear Magnetic Resonance

- ID: SCA2002-52, year: 2002
- Authors: Florence Manalo, Apostolos Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: low-field NMR, T2 distribution, clay type identification, clay-bound water, montmorillonite swelling, geometric mean T2
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-52.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-52)

Low-field 1 MHz NMR T2 spectra of illite, kaolinite and montmorillonite clays wetted with 10-20 wt% fluid show distinct peak positions that indicate clay type.

Clay-bound water affects producible fluid calculations, and clay type may be identifiable by NMR. Isolated illite-rich, kaolinite-rich and montmorillonite-rich clays were wetted stepwise to 10, 15 and 20 wt% with distilled water, tap water or 5% NaCl brine. Their T2 distributions were measured at 1 MHz with a CoreSpec 1000 (TE 0.3 ms) over eight hours per step, and particle size was measured by laser diffraction (median diameters 10-14 µm). All spectra were unimodal with peaks below 10 ms, and salinity had little effect. Montmorillonite peaked near 1 ms, kaolinite near 5 ms and illite near 6 ms. Montmorillonite also showed scattered total amplitudes, with NMR-derived and mass-balance water differing by up to 50%, attributed to swelling.

## NMR Imaging with Diffusion and Relaxation

- ID: SCA2003-24, year: 2003
- Authors: Boqin Sun, Keh-Jim Dunn, Gerald A LaTorraca, Donald M Wilson
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR imaging, T1 relaxation, diffusion coefficient, Tanner stimulated echo, internal field gradient, bound volume irreducible
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-24)

Presents NMR imaging sequences that encode T1 relaxation or diffusion before imaging, giving spatially resolved T1 and diffusion distributions in test fluids and core plugs.

Conventional MRI of cores gives fluid distributions but not pore size or wettability information, because internal field gradients limit resolution. The authors combine T1 inversion-recovery or Tanner stimulated-echo encoding with 1D and 2D imaging sequences, using FFT and BRD inversion to produce maps of position versus T1 or diffusion coefficient. On a three-cell test sample, distilled water (T1 about 3 s), crude oil (about 200 ms) and doped water (about 20 ms) were clearly separated, and water diffusion near 4e-5 cm2/s was about 10 times that of the oil. 2D T1 imaging of a 1.5-inch plug revealed small vugs and one large vug, with free-fluid and bound-fluid (33 ms cutoff) maps showing no clay in the vugs. 1D diffusion imaging of a second plug showed larger apparent diffusion at the ends, attributed to internal field gradients, and strong variation in the middle, attributed to restricted diffusion or gradient variation.

## Application of NMR Diffusion Editing as Chlorite Indicator

- ID: SCA2003-26, year: 2003
- Authors: M. D. Hürlimann, A. Matteson, J. E. Massey, D. F. Allen, E. J. Fordham, U.S.A. Ridgefield, F. Antonsen, H. G. Rueslåtten
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: diffusion editing, D-T2 maps, chlorite, internal magnetic gradients, oil saturation, T2 cutoff
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-26)

Diffusion-editing NMR separates oil and water in sandstone cores and, via signal at large apparent diffusion coefficients, gives an indicator of chlorite content correlated with FTIR chlorite and iron.

Chlorite strongly affects reservoir quality, and its internal magnetic gradients distort NMR T2 interpretation and oil/water separation. Eight Norwegian Sea sandstone plugs (0–4.5 wt% chlorite, 0.9–963 mD) were measured by CPMG at 2 MHz and by diffusion editing in a 13.2 G/cm static gradient (1.76 MHz) at brine, drainage, imbibition and D2O-exchanged states. In low-chlorite samples D–T2 maps separated oil from water, with NMR oil saturation within 3 saturation units of gravimetric values using a diffusion cutoff of 8e-6 cm²/s. In chlorite-rich samples, a growing fraction of signal appeared at apparent D above 5e-5 cm²/s due to internal gradients; this fraction correlates with FTIR chlorite and Fe2O3 content. The indicator also trends with T2 cutoff, cementation exponent and grain density, though it prevents saturation determination in high-chlorite samples.

## The Scaling of Surface-to-Pore-Volume Ratio Estimates and Its Use in Predicting NMR Log Derived Permeabilities in Clastic Rocks. Model Driven Field Applications from the Lublin Basin, Poland

- ID: SCA2003-27, year: 2003
- Authors: Hendrik Rohler, RWE Dea AG
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 log-mean, BET surface area, Kozeny-Carman permeability, pigeon hole fractal model, MICP, lamellar factor
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-27.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-27)

Uses a fractal pigeon-hole model to scale BET, MICP and NMR surface areas on Lublin Basin cores, deriving a TNMR Kozeny-Carman permeability estimator for logs.

Surface-to-pore-volume estimates from BET, MICP and NMR operate at different length scales, so permeability prediction from them needs scale conversion. About 50 Permian and Carboniferous core plugs from four Lublin Basin wells were measured for BET, MICP, T1/T2 (MARAN 30, TE=60 µs) and permeability. The Pape pigeon-hole fractal model (Df=2.3566) gives the Paris equation for BET, and the data fit it with lamellar factors of 1 to 2; samples with more than 9 wt% clay deviate. MICP and BET surfaces were related by a linear relation, and log-mean T2 correlated with BET surface, giving K·F = 137.39 T2,LM² (K in D, T2 in s), implying surface relaxivity of 5-25 µm/s. The calibrated TNMR permeability matched Sen and Timur-Coates estimators via multipliers and agreed with formation tests and k-lambda in one well, but overestimated permeability by more than an order of magnitude where authigenic clay blocked pore throats.

## Using NMR Spectra to Determine Compositions of Unconsolidated Sand/Clay/Brine/Heav Y Oil Samples

- ID: SCA2003-59, year: 2003
- Authors: F. Manalo, D. Lastockin, J. Bryan, A. Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, clay bound water, heavy oil, geometric mean T2, oil sands, sand/clay mixtures
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-59.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-59)

Low-field NMR spectra of sand/clay/brine/heavy oil mixtures are used to predict oil, water and clay content, showing strong agreement with mass balance for illite and kaolinite.

Low-field NMR cannot capture the full heavy oil signal, and clay-bound water relaxes in the same range as heavy oil, which defeats T2 cut-off methods. The authors prepared clay/brine suspensions (8-32 wt% clay) and sand/clay mixtures (5-100 wt% illite-, kaolinite- or montmorillonite-rich clay) wetted with 2 wt% NaCl brine and Cold Lake heavy oil (4.5-22 wt%). They measured them with a 1 MHz NUMAR CoreSpec 1000 and processed spectra with non-negative least squares. Increasing clay content shifted T2 peaks to lower values and grew the 10-100 ms clay-bound water peak. The geometric mean T2 correlated strongly with clay content, independent of oil presence, with montmorillonite giving lower T2gm. Predicted clay, brine and oil contents agreed with mass balance for illite and kaolinite, while montmorillonite mixtures showed more scatter and were excluded.

## Shale Volume Estimates from NMR Core Data

- ID: SCA2003-66, year: 2003
- Authors: A.K. Moss, A. Zacharopoulos, High-Point Rendel
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, clay content, water vapour adsorption, smectite, shale volume, outcrop plugs
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-66.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-66)

NMR T2 signal from outcrop plugs equilibrated at 100% relative humidity correlates with XRD clay content, giving an empirical model for estimating clay volume.

Quantifying shale volume from core is needed for reservoir evaluation and NMR log calibration. Six outcrop plugs spanning clay-carbonate-quartz compositions were characterised by XRD, helium porosity and grain density. NMR CPMG T2 was measured at 36 °C after water vapour adsorption at 100% relative humidity to constant mass, and again after brine saturation. Brine-saturated T2 distributions showed pore geometry differences but could not discriminate clay content; for example, samples with 44% and 24% clay both centred at 1.68 ms. Total NMR signal of the water-adsorbed samples correlated with clay content (R² = 0.89; Clay = (Signal − 0.145)/0.618) and more strongly with smectite content (R² = 0.94), though the sample set is small.

## NMR Core Plug Measurements to Compliment SCAL Studies

- ID: SCA2004-47, year: 2004
- Authors: A.K. Moss, The Birches Industrial Estate, Imberhorne Lane, East Grinstead, West Sussex
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, NMR porosity, duplicate sample selection, drilling mud invasion, Archie saturation exponent, pore structure change
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-47.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-47)

Shows how lab NMR T2 measurements on core plugs support SCAL programs: early porosity and saturation, duplicate sample matching, mud invasion and pore change detection.

NMR core plug data are usually acquired only for log calibration, but this paper shows they can support SCAL programs. NMR porosity, calibrated against brine/D2O references, agreed with helium porosity within an average 0.41 porosity units for unconsolidated sandstones (682-7776 mD). NMR saturations after porous plate desaturation at 180 psi differed from gravimetric/helium values by 0.23% pore volume on average, and Archie saturation exponents differed by 0.01, allowing resistivity interpretation months earlier. Comparing T2 distributions of plugs and trims identified suitable twins for mercury injection (0.03 mD pair matched; 1.46 mD pair did not). T2 changes also indicated drilling mud invasion and pore alteration in a vuggy carbonate, where NMR porosity rose from 14.95% to 15.35% and T2 components above 100 ms appeared after repeated cleaning.

## A New Experimental Procedure for NMR HTHP Measurements of Crude Oil Samples in the Oxygen-Free State

- ID: SCA2004-49, year: 2004
- Authors: G.Q. Zhang, J. Ren, H.T. Kwak, C. Edwards, S. Chen
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, dissolved oxygen, crude oil, HTHP, freeze-thaw deoxygenation, hexadecane
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-49)

Heating sealed light crude oil and hexadecane to 120°C removes dissolved oxygen, lengthening T2, giving a simple replacement for freeze-thaw deoxygenation before HTHP NMR measurements.

Dissolved oxygen shortens NMR T2 of light oils and biases viscosity estimates, but freeze-thaw deoxygenation is slow and risks re-exposure during transfer. The authors ran 2 MHz CPMG measurements in a HTHP cell on hexadecane, a 57.4 API light crude and a 12.6 API heavy oil, stepping temperature from ambient to 120°C and back at pressures up to 3000 psi. For hexadecane and light crude, T2,LM rose slowly at 90–120°C over hours (light crude from 2800 to 6400 ms at 120°C), while heavy oil showed no change. After cooling, the light crude kept longer T2 (3700 vs 1600 ms at 60°C). The heated sample's T2 distribution matched a freeze-thaw deoxygenated sample (3–4 cycles) and reverted to the original after air exposure, indicating oxygen removal by reduced solubility or chemical consumption. The proposed procedure measures oxygenated properties on heating and oxygen-free properties on cooling with one sample.

## NMR Monitoring of Water Saturation During Gravity Drainage and the Determination of Wetting Phase Relative Permeability

- ID: SCA2004-51, year: 2004
- Authors: R. Perunarkilli, I. Chatzis, M.A. Ioannidis, C. Lemaire
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: free fall gravity drainage, NMR CPMG relaxation, wetting phase relative permeability, glass bead pack, Van Genuchten model, capillary pressure
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-51.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-51)

NMR CPMG measurements track water saturation during free-fall gravity drainage of glass bead columns; simulations show NMR-based Chen model predicts water relative permeability slightly better than Van Genuchten.

The study addresses determining wetting-phase relative permeability during gravity drainage using NMR. Free-fall gravity drainage was run in 110-125 cm columns of 300 μm glass beads (100 Darcy, porosity 38.3%), with CPMG measurements at 25, 40 and 55 cm using a Bruker DMX 500 spectrometer. Magnetization decays were fitted with a modified stretched exponential model to get saturation and the long-time diffusion/relaxation constant Td (~145 ms when fully saturated). Td correlated well with Sw independent of location, indicating a homogeneous-medium relative permeability that does not vary with position. CompFlow simulations using the Chen et al. (NMR-based) and Van Genuchten models reproduced production history and saturation profiles, with the Chen model giving better predictions, including gas front position.

## Low Field NMR Applications in Oil Sands Mining and Extraction

- ID: SCA2005-23, year: 2005
- Authors: A. Kantzas, J. Bryan, A. Mai, F. Hum
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: low field NMR, oil sands, bitumen saturation, Dean-Stark, T2 deconvolution, amplitude index
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-23)

Low field NMR spectra with first-peak deconvolution predict bitumen, water and solids content of oil sand ores and froths within a few percent of Dean-Stark.

Dean-Stark extraction, the standard for oil, water and solids content in oil sands ores and froths, takes about 48 hours, so faster methods are needed for process control. The authors measured 550 usable ore samples from five Northern Alberta wells and 49 froth samples with a 0.3 ms echo spacing low field NMR at 30°C. Oil and water signals were separated by deconvolving the fast-relaxing first peak using a second-derivative inflection point. Oil amplitude index was obtained from an empirical correlation with geometric mean T2. Compared with Dean-Stark on sister samples, ore predictions had average errors of 0.92% (water) and 1.04% (bitumen), and froth predictions 1.86% and 3.00%. Solids were obtained by difference, and the degree of overlap between the oil and bound-water peaks indicated ore processibility, with clay-rich samples showing poorer grade.

## Protocols for Calibrating NMR Log-Derived Permeabilities

- ID: SCA2005-37, year: 2005
- Authors: John L. Shafer, Songhua Chen, Daniel T. Georgi, Reservoir Management
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: NMR log permeability, Coates model, T2 cutoff, profile permeability upscaling, wettability restoration, heterogeneous sands
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-37)

Reviews protocols for calibrating Coates permeability parameters and T2 cutoff for NMR logs using core data, showing heterogeneous Gulf of Mexico sands favor geometrically averaged profile permeability.

The paper reviews how to calibrate NMR log-derived permeability (Coates model, T2 cutoff) against core permeability, Dean-Stark saturation, capillary pressure and laboratory NMR, with emphasis on heterogeneous rock. Regression approaches are described, with m fixed at 4 and C and n fitted. Examples come from a homogeneous North Sea set and heterogeneous unconsolidated deepwater Gulf of Mexico sands. For the North Sea, default parameters overpredicted permeability by nearly an order of magnitude; regressed values were C=15.22, m=4, n=2.29 (R²=0.56). In the GoM, plug-based regression gave R² of 0.24, versus 0.6 for geometrically averaged profile permeability (10/ft). Core-to-core regressions against capillary pressure Swir and Dean-Stark Sw gave R² of 0.945 and 0.79, and calibrated NMR permeabilities varied by up to one order of magnitude. Lab T2 cutoffs for GoM plugs were 11 ms (100% brine), 25 ms (Swi plus viscosity-matched oil) and 11 ms (Swi plus OBM filtrate), and a wettability-restoration NMR protocol is proposed.

## Using Low-Field NMR to Understand Fines Behaviour

- ID: SCA2005-46, year: 2005
- Authors: F. Hum, A. Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: low-field NMR, T2 geometric mean, clay-bound brine, oil sands fines, montmorillonite, heavy oil
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-46.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-46)

Low-field NMR T2 measurements on synthetic clay/sand/brine/heavy-oil mixtures yield nomographs relating T2gm to clay content, which predict fines content of oil sands ores and flag montmorillonite.

Oil sands recovery depends on fines content, but heavy oil and clay-bound brine relax at similar T2 values, which breaks the conventional T2 cut-off method. The authors prepared synthetic samples of illite, kaolinite or montmorillonite with sand, 2 wt% brine and Cold Lake heavy oil (57 clay-only and 144 clay/sand samples), with clay dispersed or layered. They measured them at 1 MHz and compared results with eight Fort McMurray ores after Dean-Stark extraction and laser particle size analysis. Brine relaxed at about 4 ms in illite, 2 ms in kaolinite and 0.5 ms in montmorillonite. T2gm decreased with clay content and was consistently lowest for montmorillonite (about 4 ms versus 30 ms for 50 wt% kaolinite in clay/brine samples). Nomographs from synthetic samples predicted ore fines content in reasonable agreement with PSA, although oil and brine contents must still be known.

## Improved Method for Measuring Surface Relaxivity

- ID: SCA2005-48, year: 2005
- Authors: Jostein Kolnes, Ola Ketil Siqveland, Aksel Hiorth, Svein M. Skjæveland
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: surface relaxivity, NMR T2 relaxation, fast diffusion regime, planar glass pore, bulk relaxation of water, temperature control
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-48.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-48)

Water-filled planar glass slit pores with improved temperature control give surface relaxivity (about 6.4-6.8 µm/s) and extend the verified fast diffusion regime to ρa/D≈0.15.

Surface relaxivity values are uncertain because pore geometry and surface composition are poorly defined. The authors built planar slit pores from window glass and HSQ-300 quartz glass plates separated by Teflon spacers, and measured water T2 at 35 °C with a 2 MHz MARAN 2 using CPMG. Removing the sample holder and sealing the compartment bottom kept temperature stable, giving bulk T2B errors below 0.01 s; T2B depended on echo spacing, ion and oxygen content. Window glass gave ρ of 6.4-6.7 µm/s, with a linear fast-diffusion trend up to d = 135 µm (ρd/2D = 0.146), a threefold extension. Non-polished quartz gave 6.8 µm/s, while fire-polished quartz gave 0.9 (mean T2) or 2.9 µm/s (mono-exponential); AFM showed only ~25% area difference, so polishing altered surface relaxing properties. Modeling showed the zeroth mode carries 98.6% of decay up to ρa/D = 1, and the T2S approximation has ≤3.3% error for ρa/D ≤ 0.1.

## Improved Porosity Estimation in Tight Gas Reservoirs from NMR and Density Logs

- ID: SCA2005-57, year: 2005
- Authors: M.M. Abu-Shanab, G.M. Hamada, M.El. Oraby, A.A. Abdel Wally
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR porosity, density log, gas-corrected porosity, tight gas sand, DMR porosity, core calibration
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-57.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-57)

A density-magnetic resonance (DMR) porosity transform, calibrated to core in one well, gives gas-corrected porosity in Obaiyed tight gas sands across three facies without neutron logs.

Neutron-density porosity is unreliable in the Obaiyed tight gas sands (Western Desert, Egypt) because of iron-rich clays, chlorite and siderite. The authors combine the NMR and density porosity response equations to solve for gas-corrected porosity as a linear combination, DMRP = A·φD + B·φNMR. Calibrating to stress-corrected core porosity in well D7 (facies I), with the trend line forced through (1,1), gives A=0.65 and B=0.35. Applied unchanged to D18 (facies II) and D13 (facies III), DMRP matched core porosity well. Root square errors were 0.55, 0.54 and 0.61 for DMRP, versus 0.79, 1.08 and 2 for density porosity and 1.48, 1.54 and 3.3 for NMR porosity. The method needs neither fluid density nor gas hydrogen index, and permits faster logging since full gas polarization is not required.

## Modeling Residual Water Saturation by NMR, Semipermeable Membrane and Ultracentrifugation in Hydrophobic and Partially Hydrophobic Pore and Cavern Carbonate Reservoirs

- ID: SCA2005-71, year: 2005
- Authors: Denisenko A.S
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: residual water saturation, NMR T2 cutoff, semipermeable membrane, ultracentrifuge, hydrophobic carbonates, silicification
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-71.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-71)

Residual water saturation in hydrophobic, silicified Permian carbonates is compared across NMR, semipermeable membrane and ultracentrifuge; the T2 cutoff varies with silicification and wettability, and membrane data match at 12 bar.

Residual water saturation (Swr) was estimated on a Lower Permian carbonate core collection from the Timan-Pechora province using semipermeable membrane, NMR and ultracentrifugation. NMR and ultracentrifuge gave substantially lower Swr than the membrane method, and a fixed T2 cutoff of 90 ms did not reproduce membrane results. Irregular silicification (0-80% SiO2) partly occluded large pores and throats, causing wide variation in the T2 cutoff; the membrane method reflects throat sizes while NMR reflects pore sizes. Partial to complete hydrophobicity meant that Swr on cleaned cores was overestimated by 2 to 4 times relative to native-state cores. Membrane and NMR results on cleaned samples became compatible at a displacement pressure of 12 bar, and the cutoff can be corrected using SiO2 content from the lithodensity log.

## SCA2005-73

- ID: SCA2005-73, year: 2005
- Authors: Wang Xiaowen Xiao Lizhi Xie Ranhong, Zhang Yuanzhong
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR porosity, echo spacing, wait time, T2 splicing, MRIL-P, clay-bound water
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-73.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-73)

Lab NMR on 40+ cores shows MRIL-style acquisition underestimates porosity of Chinese terrestrial sandstones; shorter TE and TW settings cut core-NMR differences to under 1 pu.

NMR logging porosity in Chinese terrestrial formations differs from core porosity by 2-6 pu when using standard Halliburton MRIL-P acquisition parameters. Laboratory NMR measurements on more than 40 samples (artificial ceramic, Berea, and sandstones from Daqing, Xinjiang and Dagang) replicated the MRIL acquisition and T2 splicing. The standard method (TWL 12 s, TEL 1.2 ms; TWS 20 ms, TES 0.6 ms) gave under 1 pu error for ceramic and Berea but 2-6 pu for terrestrial sandstones, attributed to micro-pores missed at the longer echo spacing. A revised method (TWL 5 s, TEL 0.9 ms; TWS 10 ms, TES 0.3 ms; clay-bound water bins 0.25-16 ms) reduced differences to under 1 pu for most samples. The authors recommend core-based calibration of acquisition parameters for each region.

## NMR Diffusional Coupling: Effects of Temperature and Clay Distribution

- ID: SCA2006-13, year: 2006
- Authors: Vivek Anand, George J. Hirasaki, Marc Fleury
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, diffusional coupling, coupling parameter, surface relaxivity, temperature dependence, shaly sand clay distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-13)

Extends a micro-macropore NMR coupling parameter to temperature and clay distribution, showing coupling rises with temperature and in dispersed clays, where inversion beats a fixed T2 cutoff.

NMR T2 interpretation assumes pores relax independently, but diffusional coupling between micro- and macropores can distort results. The authors add Arrhenius temperature dependence of surface relaxivity and water diffusivity to a coupling parameter α. Silica gels (three grain sizes, 30–95 °C) and a Thamama carbonate (21.2% porosity, 13 mD, 25–80 °C) went from bimodal toward unimodal T2 with temperature, as relaxivity fell and diffusivity rose. Model shaly sands (fine sand with 0–10% kaolinite or bentonite) showed unimodal T2 when clay was dispersed (coupled) but separate clay and sand peaks when laminated (decoupled). A 33 ms cutoff misestimates irreducible saturation in dispersed systems, while the inversion gave irreducible saturation within 4% and microporosity fraction within 5% average absolute deviation.

## Pore Structure Analysis of Coals Using Low Field NMR Measurements and Thermogravimetry Analysis

- ID: SCA2006-27, year: 2006
- Authors: Benjamin Nicot, Serge Gautier, Marc Fleury, Sevket Durucan
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: coal microporosity, low field NMR, FID Gaussian-exponential fit, solid echo, thermogravimetry, cleat porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-27.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-27)

Combining 2 and 23 MHz NMR (T2, T1, FID, solid echo) with thermogravimetry on four coals shows microporosity is a large fraction of porosity that standard CPMG underestimates.

Coal pore structure matters for CO2 storage, but micropore NMR signals overlap with the signal from solid protons. Four coals (anthracite to high-volatile bituminous) were measured as small dried pieces at 23 MHz (T1, T2, FID, solid echo) and as dodecane-saturated 45 mm plugs at 2 MHz, with thermogravimetry and mass spectrometry on the small pieces. Fitting the FID with a Gaussian (solid) plus exponential (liquid) term separated the liquid micropore water. Its fractions (0.7–7.9%) were comparable to TGA mass loss below 300°C (1.4–4.7%), although NMR overestimated samples A2 and A3. CPMG at 2 MHz cannot resolve micropore T2 because of the long inter-echo time, and dodecane does not enter the water-filled micropores. Total porosity was 1.7–8.7%, with microporosity 0.41–0.91 of the total.

## An Improved NMR SCAL Technique

- ID: SCA2006-47, year: 2006
- Authors: Hege Christin Widerøe, Geir Humborstad Sørland, Henrik Walbye Anthonsen, Åsmund Ukkelberg
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: internal magnetic field gradient, SE-CPMG, 2D inverse Laplace transform, surface coating indicator, T1/T2 ratio, chlorite sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-47.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-47)

A single spin echo CPMG (SE-CPMG) 2D NMR method yields internal gradient distributions and a surface coating indicator Kgi that correlates with T1/T2 (R²=0.94) in brine-saturated sandstones.

The paper addresses extracting more information from NMR on brine-saturated sandstone plugs than conventional 1D T2 measurements allow. It introduces an SE-CPMG sequence that varies the first inter-echo spacing before a fixed-spacing CPMG train, and uses 2D inverse Laplace transform to obtain joint T2 and internal field gradient (Gi) distributions. Scaling Gi by T2 gives a surface coating indicator, Kgi, which is independent of pore size and sensitive to susceptibility contrast at the mineral surface. Measurements on seven 1.5-inch sandstone plugs (quartz up to 73 wt%, with feldspar, mica, illite and chlorite/kaolinite) at 2 MHz resolved four Gi regions (clay, small, medium, large) where T2 showed only two. Kgi correlated linearly with average T1/T2, a known chlorite indicator (R² = 0.94), and the plug with the strongest gradient did not have the highest Kgi. SE-CPMG is faster than inversion recovery for this purpose.

## A 12 MHZ Rock Core Analyzer for Improved Characterisation of 1.5" Rock Core Plugs

- ID: SCA2006-48, year: 2006
- Authors: Geir Humborstad Sørland, Hege Christin Widerøe
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 12 MHz NMR, T1-T2 map, pulsed field gradient diffusion, pore size distribution, clay-bound brine, eddy current dead time
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-48.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-48)

Presents a 12 MHz benchtop NMR core analyzer with pulsed field gradients, showing better resolution in T2, T1-T2 maps, diffusion and pore size distributions than 2 MHz.

NMR SCAL at 2 MHz is limited by sensitivity, so a 12 MHz rock core analyzer for 1.5" plugs was developed with Oxford Instruments. It also has a pulsed field gradient option of about 225 Gauss/cm, with eddy current dead times of ~300 µs achieved using preparatory gradient pulses and sinusoidal gradient shapes instead of active shielding. At 12 MHz the T2 distribution shows a narrower main peak and three further regions, versus a single peak with a shoulder at 2 MHz. T1-T2 maps of a clay-rich brine-saturated plug show two brine components, one near bulk diffusion (2.9e-5 cm2/s) and clay-bound brine about 5 times slower. PFG diffusion on a fresh-state plug separated brine from a crude-oil-like component with mobility more than 10 times lower. Short observation time diffusion (~1 ms) combined with T1-T2 gives a combined pore size/T1 distribution, with details not obtainable from mercury injection.

## New Insights into in-Situ Measurements of Oil Sand Samples

- ID: SCA2006-50, year: 2006
- Authors: A. Mai, J. Bryan, A. Kantzas
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: low field NMR, oil sands, signal de-convolution, bitumen content, Dean-Stark, middlings
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-50.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-50)

Low-field NMR de-convolution predicts oil and water content in oil sand ores from a new field and in middlings, with errors of about 2-3 saturation units.

The paper tests a previously developed low-field NMR de-convolution method for separating bitumen and water signals on 18 oil sand samples from a new reservoir, using Dean-Stark and particle size data as references. The method worked for "normal" samples with well-defined fast-relaxing peaks, but gave oil T2gm values that were too high for "odd" samples. These odd samples contain more than 10 wt% clay, silt and very fine sand, which increases overlap of water and bitumen signals. Overall average errors were 1.2 saturation units for water and 1.8 for oil, giving error bars of about ±2 and 3 saturation units. In 16 middlings samples from BEU jar tests, bitumen was taken as the signal under 10 ms with a constant amplitude factor. Water matched well, while very low bitumen contents were measured poorly, showing a lower detection limit, though NMR could still distinguish higher-bitumen samples.

## The Impact of Reservoir Confining Stress on NMR T2 and Pore Frequency Distribution in Some Carbonate Samples

- ID: SCA2007_20, year: 2007
- Authors: P Mitchell, M Niedzielak, I A Al-Hosani, M Z Kalam
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, mercury intrusion, confining stress, surface relaxivity, pore throat size distribution, carbonate
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_20)

NMR T2 and mercury intrusion were measured on four carbonate samples at ambient and 3200 psi confining stress; both show distribution narrowing, and surface relaxivity varies fivefold.

The paper examines how reservoir confining stress changes pore frequency distributions from high-pressure mercury intrusion and NMR T2 in carbonates. Four samples from two Middle-East reservoirs (porosity 0.123-0.194, permeability 0.21-38.4 mD) were tested at ambient and 3200 psi hydrostatic stress using a new PEEK NMR confining vessel, with centrifuge oil-brine drainage at stress for comparison. Surface relaxivity was derived by fitting NMR-derived drainage capillary pressure to centrifuge data, giving about a fivefold range (0.0038-0.0191 µm/ms). Stress generally narrowed distributions and raised peak height, with some microporosity loss in both methods; one sample was nearly insensitive to stress and one bimodal sample showed a shift in intergranular modal size by NMR. Mercury and NMR capillary pressure curves agreed with each other better than with centrifuge data, possibly due to water trapping in centrifuge tests.

## Probing the Connectivity Between Pores in Rock Core Samples

- ID: SCA2007_42, year: 2007
- Authors: Ketil Djurhuus, Hege Christin Widerøe, Jan R. Lien Arne Skauge
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR diffusion, pore connectivity, surface-to-volume ratio, time-dependent diffusion coefficient, pore size distribution, carbonate core
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_42.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_42)

NMR diffusion measured at observation times scaled to each sample's surface-to-volume ratio lets pore connectivity be compared between brine-saturated carbonate cores; higher-permeability F48 shows better connectivity than O30.

The paper addresses how to probe pore-to-pore connectivity with NMR, since earlier attempts failed because the signal decayed before the tortuosity limit was reached. The approach scales the observation time-dependent diffusion coefficient D(t)/D0 by the inverse surface-to-volume ratio (S/V)^-1, which gives a universal initial slope of -4/(9√π). Measurements used a 12 MHz Maran DRX at 35 °C on two brine-saturated carbonate cores (O30: 7.2 mD, V/S 4.1 µm; F48: 37.8 mD, V/S 6.3 µm), plus polystyrene sphere packs of 45 and 98 µm as a reference. Pore size distributions peaked at 2.1 µm for O30 and 3.7 µm for F48. Both cores follow the universal slope up to about (S/V)^-1 ≈ 1, then diverge, with F48 keeping a higher diffusion coefficient. This is consistent with its higher permeability and better pore connectivity. The method probes root mean squared displacements up to about 100 µm.

## Thermogravimetric Analysis of Barnett Shale Samples

- ID: SCA2007_45, year: 2007
- Authors: T. Greg Easley, Richard Sigal, Chandra Rai MPGE
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: thermogravimetric analysis, shale core cleaning, clay bound water, total organic carbon, FTIR mineralogy, Barnett shale
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_45.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_45)

Thermogravimetric analysis of three Barnett shale samples shows that drying at 100 °C removes only water, not kerogen, minerals or clay-bound water, validating the cleaning practice.

Conventional solvent cleaning does not work on ultra-low-permeability shale, so the standard practice of drying fragmented samples at 100 °C needed verification. Three Barnett shale samples (about 40 mg fragments) were heated in a TGA at 3 °C/min to about 900 °C under nitrogen, with and without HCl treatment to stabilize carbonates. Weight losses were compared with FTIR mineralogy and TOC; for example, sample _261 lost 10% versus 9% predicted, and the clean sample's 4.2% loss matched its 4.1% TOC. The smectite-rich sample showed 5.7% post-400 °C loss attributed to clay-bound water, consistent with 16% smectite from FTIR. Gas chromatography of vapor released up to 100 °C showed no hydrocarbons, indicating that 100 °C drying mobilizes only water and gives a good effective porosity estimate.

## Determination of Grain Size Distribution from NMR Relaxation Time Using Pore Scale Modeling

- ID: SCA2007_49, year: 2007
- Authors: J. Chen, M. Gladkikh, S. Chen, D. Jacobi, H. Kwak
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, grain size distribution, pore-scale modeling, surface roughness factor, surface relaxivity, Berea sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_49)

Pore-scale geometric modeling with a surface roughness factor converts NMR T2 distributions to grain size distributions in quartzose and feldspathic sandstones, matching measured grain sizes.

Estimating grain size distribution from NMR T2 is complicated by mixed grain sizes, mineralogy and diagenesis. The authors use a pore-scale model built from Delaunay tessellation of dense sphere packings to compute S/V and T2, and fit log-normal or Weibull grain size distributions by minimizing the misfit to measured T2. They tested unconsolidated sands, Berea outcrops (quartz-dominated and feldspar-rich) and reservoir sandstones. A surface roughness factor Rs accounts for weathered and dissolved feldspar surfaces. For quartz-dominated samples, ρ2·Rs was 33.7 ± 6.5 µm/s. For sample B3, ρ2 was 10 and 25 µm/s and Rs 4 and 18 for quartz and feldspar. Feldspar Rs increased linearly with its weight percentage, and calculated grain size agreed well with measurements.

## NMR and Calorimetry Determination of Pore Size and Porosity

- ID: SCA2007_55, year: 2007
- Authors: Fiñana A, Ramia M.E, Martín C.A
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, differential thermal calorimetry, surface relaxivity, pore size distribution, freezing point depression, sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_55.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_55)

Combines NMR T2 distributions with calorimetric freezing-point depression of pore water to derive surface relaxivity and absolute pore size distributions for two sandstones, without mercury injection.

The paper combines low-field (2 MHz) NMR CPMG T2 measurements with differential thermal calorimetry (DTC) on the same brine-saturated plugs to obtain absolute pore size distributions. Two friable, high-porosity quartz-rich sandstones (93-95% quartz) from the San Jorge Gulf basin, Argentina, were measured in a holder suited to both techniques. The freezing/fusion temperature shift (Gibbs-Thomson, k = 5.7e-8 K·m) was linked to T2 by matching centers of mass of the two distributions, as is done when T2 is matched to mercury injection. Surface relaxivity came out at about 21e-6 m/s-type units for Rock 1 and 15e-6 for Rock 2, with roughly 8% error dominated by temperature measurement. Rock 1 relaxivity is close to tabulated sandstone values, and Rock 2 lies between sandstone and dolomite. The method is non-destructive and offers an alternative when mercury injection data are unavailable.

## A NMR Permeability Model Based on Partial Least Square (PLS) Regression Analysis for a Low Permeability Gas Sand in Santos Basin

- ID: SCA2008-43, year: 2008
- Authors: Vinicius de Franca Machado, Rodrigo Bagueira de Vasconcellos Azeredo, Paulo Frederico Ramos, Marcio Roque Coutinho, Sandra Reveriego Carneiro
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR permeability, partial least squares regression, SDR model, Timur-Coates model, T2 relaxation, tight gas sand
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-43.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-43)

PLS regression on NMR T2 spectra of 13 tight gas sand plugs predicts log permeability far better than recalibrated SDR and Timur-Coates models.

Classical SDR and Timur-Coates NMR permeability models reduce the T2 spectrum to one variable and perform poorly in low-permeability gas sands. T2 spectra (2 MHz, 200 µs echo spacing) were measured on 13 Santos Basin plugs (0.01-15.5 mD, 8.1-21.3% porosity) at Sw=100% and at Swi after 600 psi centrifuging. SDR and Timur-Coates coefficients were refitted and a PLS model was built from the T2 bins, excluding bins above the average T2 cutoff (32 ms) and normalizing to unit area. All three matched the 13 plugs, but applied to the full log and compared with 600 core permeabilities, SDR and Timur-Coates overestimated by orders of magnitude. The gas signal pushed log T2LM to 248.8 ms versus 32.7 ms for the calibration plug, inflating SDR by about 4 orders of magnitude. The one-latent-variable PLS model explained over 80% of variance and gave a much better log match, being insensitive to porosity errors.

## An Integrated Petrophysical Analysis to Evaluate Low Resistivity Low Contrast (LRLC) Pays in Clastic Reservoirs in Se Asia

- ID: SCA2008-44, year: 2008
- Authors: Lutz Riepe, A. Sharby BA Hamid, M Hafiz RB Hamzah, M Nizam BM Zain
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: low resistivity low contrast pay, irreducible water saturation, NMR T2 cutoff, saturation height function, shaly sand saturation models, capillary pressure
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-44.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-44)

Integrates logs, NMR and SCAL (capillary pressure, electrical, T2) to reconcile Swirr and water saturation in low resistivity low contrast clastic pays in Malaysian basins.

Low resistivity low contrast pays (2-4 Ohmm) in Malay, Sarawak and Sabah Basin clastics are often missed as net pay because of high irreducible water saturation or incorrect resistivity-derived Sw. The authors present a workflow reconciling Swirr from three core measurements (NMR T2 at different Sw, capillary pressure curves, and electrical data including multi-salinity, resistivity index and CEC) with resistivity and NMR logs. T2 cutoffs from core were applied to NMR logs, and electrical parameters (m, n, Cx) and saturation models were varied until Sw agreed within ±5 saturation units with saturation height functions. In a Malay Basin gas example (1.5-3 Ohmm, log Sw 60-80%, porosity 25-28%, permeability 5-50 mD), bimodal pore sizes with mostly micropores explained the high bound water. The capillary-pressure saturation height function placed the free water level about 10 m deeper than assumed, increasing reserves and justifying deeper perforation. Permeability prediction and relative permeability work are planned.

## Two-Phase Simulation and Correlation of NMR Magnetic Decay in Equilateral Triangular Pores

- ID: SCA2008-51, year: 2008
- Authors: Unn H. á Lað, Aksel Hiorth, Jan Finjord, Svein M. Skjæveland
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, triangular pore, fast diffusion limit, surface relaxivity, random walk simulation, Mayer-Stowe-Princen
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-51.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-51)

Random-walk simulations of two-phase NMR decay in triangular pores yield a biexponential correlation that represents decay better than the fast diffusion limit, especially at low contact angle and high relaxivity.

NMR interpretation usually assumes monoexponential surface relaxation (T2S = V/Sρ) in each pore, but this is untested for angular pores holding two phases. The authors used random-walk simulation on a 301×301 triangular grid with 5·10^5 walkers per phase, with fluid configurations from Mayer-Stowe-Princen theory, for six pore sizes. Decay curves for oil and water were fitted with biexponential sums, and the intensities and relaxation times were correlated with relaxivity, contact angle and fluid geometry. The wetting-phase relaxation time increases and the non-wetting-phase time decreases as contact angle rises. The FDL assumption becomes inaccurate at high relaxivity and low contact angle, likely because the pore wall near the meniscus is inaccessible. The correlation is valid for primary drainage, contact angles of 0–55°, relative pore sizes of 1–50 and relative relaxivity of 1–10.

## Characterization of Pore to Pore Diffusive Exchange Using NMR T2-Store-T2 2D Experiments

- ID: SCA2009-21, year: 2009
- Authors: M. FLEURY
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: T2-store-T2, diffusional pore coupling, 2D NMR relaxation, coupling parameter, double porosity carbonate, clay-bound water
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-21)

Defines a coupling parameter from a two-pore exchange model and applies T2-store-T2 NMR to a clay gel, shaly sandstone and carbonate to quantify diffusional pore coupling.

Diffusional pore-to-pore exchange can make a multimodal pore system look unimodal in 1D NMR T2 distributions. Using an analytical two-zone exchange solution, the author defines a coupling parameter α = 2(ka+kb)T2a and shows how micropore peak amplitude and T2, macropore T2 and off-diagonal peak intensity vary with α. T2-store-T2 experiments (23.7 MHz, 40x40 compressed data, 2D inverse Laplace) were run on a smectite gel (α=0.28, weak coupling, intra-flock porosity underestimated by ~10%), a Vosges sandstone (α=0.73, intergranular V/S underestimated by a factor of 4) and a Lavoux limestone. In the limestone the two-pore model failed quantitatively. Water was strongly coupled (α about 1-10), and lowering diffusivity tenfold with water/glycerol revealed a trimodal distribution consistent with MICP and SEM, with micro-meso and micro-macro exchange but none between meso and macro.

## Characterization of Bitumen Bearing Carbonate Rocks

- ID: SCA2009-40, year: 2009
- Authors: J. Bryan, J. Wang, A. Kantzas
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: X-ray CT, low-field NMR, bitumen carbonate, open porosity, T2 geometric mean, initial water saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-40.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-40)

CT density and low-field NMR on two native-state bitumen carbonate cores separate matrix from open porosity and estimate initial water and bitumen saturations.

Bitumen-bearing carbonates are heterogeneous, so rock and fluid properties must be understood before choosing a recovery process. Two full-diameter native-state cores from northern Alberta (a vuggy dolomite and a dolomite mudstone) were scanned by X-ray CT and measured by low-field NMR. CT density histograms were split into matrix and open (vug/fracture) porosity; Core 2 had higher matrix porosity (25% vs 12%) but only 7% of pixels in the open range versus 21% in Core 1, consistent with its lower permeability. NMR water T2gm was 268 ms vs 35 ms (water-saturated) and 85 ms vs 14 ms (native state) for Cores 1 and 2. Combining NMR water porosity with CT porosity gave Swi of 0.139 and 0.206, with bitumen saturation by difference.

## Analysis of T2-D Relaxation-Diffusion NMR Measurements for Partially Saturated Media at Different Field Strength

- ID: SCA2010-17, year: 2010
- Authors: Christoph H. Arns, Tariq AlGhamdi, Ji-Youn Arns, Lauren Burcaw, Kathryn E. Washburn
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: T2-D NMR, relaxation-diffusion correlation, internal gradients, random walk simulation, micro-CT, partial saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 5
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-17.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-17)

Random-walk simulations on micro-CT images of sandstones and limestone reproduce main features of measured T2-D NMR spectra at 2, 12 and 400 MHz without free parameters.

T2-D NMR interpretation is uncertain because of internal gradients, restricted diffusion, limited signal-to-noise and non-unique inversion. The authors simulated DRCOSY T2-D responses by lattice random walks (2, 12, 400 MHz) on ~3 µm micro-CT images of Bentheimer, Berea and Savonnière samples. Internal fields were computed from phase susceptibilities, and partial saturations were generated with a maximal-sphere morphological approach. Simulations used a consistent parameter set (e.g. quartz relaxivity 3 µm/s) and were compared with experiments on oil-saturated and partially water-saturated samples. At low field the simulations matched experiment well, including the water peak. At high field simulated diffusion coefficients were higher than measured, likely from overestimated internal gradients. Long diffusion times lowered the apparent D, and Savonnière limestone showed little internal gradient effect.

## Characterization of Nanoporous Systems with Low Field NMR: Application to Kaolinite and Smectite Clays

- ID: SCA2011-23, year: 2011
- Authors: M. Fleury, S. Gautier, F. Norrant, E. Kohler
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, smectite interlayer water, kaolinite, free induction decay, T1-T2 map, freezing experiment
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-23)

Low-field NMR (CPMG, FID, T1-T2, freezing) on kaolinite and smectite quantifies hydroxyls, external and interlayer water; interlayer T2 is about 0.6 ms and holds 80% of water at 100% RH.

The study uses low-field (23.7 MHz) NMR to separate structural hydroxyl protons, external water and interlayer water in kaolinite and a natural Na/Ca smectite. Powders and compacted discs were equilibrated at 100% RH, dried at 100°C or ambient humidity, or exchanged with ethylene glycol, and measured with CPMG, FID and T1-T2 sequences, plus D2O exchange and freezing down to -60°C. Hydroxyl protons (T2 < 50 μs) were quantified by FID and agree reasonably with structural-formula predictions (kaolinite 2.0 vs 1.9 mg; smectite 0.54 vs 0.68 mg). At 100% RH kaolinite shows T2 about 5-6 ms versus about 1 ms for smectite, reflecting surface area differences. Interlayer water in smectite is in fast exchange with external water and appears only with ethylene glycol or after freezing external water; Arrhenius extrapolation of the T2 of 0.16 ms at -16°C gives 0.62 ms at 30°C. At 100% RH about 80% of smectite water is interlayer (298 mg/g clay), higher than literature values of about 200 mg/g.

## Water Evaporation from Porous Media

- ID: SCA2011-51, year: 2011
- Authors: Lorne A. Davis Jr
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, drying, constant rate period, falling rate period, montmorillonite, evaporation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-51.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-51)

Low-field NMR T2 monitoring of water drying from consolidated and unconsolidated media identifies constant-rate and falling-rate regimes and links them to pore size, geometry and transport mechanism.

The paper studies evaporation of water from porous media using low-field (2 MHz) NMR relaxometry, tracking water content and T2 distributions every half hour during drying at 33 C. Samples were seven consolidated plugs (Bentheim sandstone, alumina, fired clay) and packs of silica sand, kaolinite and montmorillonite. Three regimes were seen: a constant rate period linear in Sw, a first falling rate period with square-root-of-time decline, and a second falling rate period below Swir. In CRP drying the T2 log mean scales one-to-one with Sw, consistent with film thinning, whereas in FRP1 the main T2 peak stays static, implying blob-type transport. Drying mode depended on geometry (Bentheim drying radially stayed CRP to Swir, axially entered FRP1 at Sw~0.5), and montmorillonite interlamellar exchange did not slow drying relative to kaolinite.

## Magnetic Resonance Imaging of Chemical EOR in Core to Complement Field Pilot Studies

- ID: SCA2012-30, year: 2012
- Authors: J. Mitchell, J. Staniland, A. Wilson, A. Howe, A. Clarke, E.J. Fordham, J. Edwards, R. Faber, R. Bouwmeester
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: low-field MRI, alkaline surfactant flood, remaining oil saturation, capillary end effect, NMR T2 relaxation, carbonate chemical EOR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-30.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-30)

Low-field MRI monitoring of alkaline-surfactant floods in North Oman limestone plugs gives spatially resolved remaining oil (~17 s.u.) consistent with single-well field pilots.

Short-plug core floods can give misleading remaining oil saturation because of capillary end effects, so the authors used low-field (56 mT) MRI with D2O brine to monitor alkaline-surfactant floods in two North Oman microporous limestone plugs (about 29 p.u., 5-7 mD) at 69°C. Plug A received 8 PV of AS and plug B 2.4 PV. Oil saturation fell from about 25 s.u. after waterflood to 16-17 s.u. after AS, the outlet capillary end effect was removed, and an oil bank formed. Differential pressure dropped from 70 to 20 kPa during AS injection, so there was no injectivity problem as seen in the field. Co-solvent was distinguished by T2 at about 3 s.u., and surfactant recovery was 78% for plug A and 55% for plug B. Middle-region ROS agreed with single-well pilot (18 s.u.) and SWCT (19 s.u.) values, while PNC logs disagreed, likely owing to uncertain flood geometry.

## A New NMR $T_1$ Measurement Technique for Gas Shale, Heavy Oil, and Microporosity Characterizations

- ID: SCA2012-37, year: 2012
- Authors: Songhua Chen, Lilong Li
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR T1 distribution, saturation recovery, hybrid saturation-inversion-recovery, Fréchet distance, gas shale, heavy oil
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-37)

A hybrid saturation-inversion-recovery NMR T1 sequence is shown by simulation to recover fast-relaxing components better than saturation recovery at equal acquisition time, with optimal TW near 10 ms.

Saturation-recovery (SR) T1 measurements have poor sensitivity to fast-relaxing components found in gas shale, heavy oil and carbonate microporosity, while inversion recovery is too slow for logging. The authors propose a hybrid saturation-inversion-recovery (HSIR) sequence and derive its magnetization evolution. Simulations of carbonate, heavy-oil and gas-shale T1 models were run at equal total acquisition time, with noise at core level (SNR 200) and logging level (SNR 12-44), using at least 100 noise realizations and two-step inversion. Fréchet distance and curve misfit showed HSIR closer to the true distribution in all cases, with the largest gains at low SNR (e.g., shale mean Fréchet distance 5.20 for SR vs 3.76 for HSIR). Carbonates showed small improvement, and the optimal TW was about 10 ms for both heavy-oil and shale cases.

## Butane Condensation in Kerogen Pores and in Smectite Clay: NMR Relaxation and Comparison in Lab Study

- ID: SCA2012-46, year: 2012
- Authors: Jilin Zhang, Jin-Hong Chen, Guodong Jin, Terrence Quinn, Elton Frost
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: capillary condensation, butane, NMR T2 relaxation, kerogen nanopores, smectite clay, Woodford shale
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-46.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-46)

Butane vapor below saturation pressure condenses in Woodford shale kerogen nanopores and smectite clay, showing NMR T2 below 10 ms and correlating with TOC.

The paper tests whether light hydrocarbon gases condense by capillary condensation in kerogen nanopores and between clay crystals, which would affect NMR interpretation in shale gas. Twenty-five Woodford shale samples (TOC 1-6 wt%) and a pure Na-smectite (SWY2) were dried and saturated with butane vapor at up to 1 atm (glass flask) or ~2.5 atm (steel cell). Condensed butane was quantified by weight gain and by Maran II CPMG NMR (TE 300 µs, RD 0.5 s), and the two agreed (e.g. sample 206 plug: 1.78% by weight vs 2.0% by NMR). Condensed volume correlated generally positively with TOC, rose with butane pressure, and was larger in crushed samples; sandstone controls took up almost none. T2 of condensed butane was about 0.5-7 ms in shales and peaked near 1 ms in smectite, so condensed gas may fall in the bulk volume irreducible. Desorption into a closed vial reduced the NMR signal over time.

## NMR Measurement of Oil Shale Magnetic

- ID: SCA2013-052, year: 2013
- Authors: Catherine M. Kirkland, Sarah J. Vogt
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: high-field NMR, T1-T2 correlation, oil shale, kerogen, CPMG relaxation, inverse Laplace transform
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-052.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-052)

High-field 300 MHz NMR T2 and T1-T2 correlation measurements on crushed oil shales from around the world reveal short-T2 kerogen signals and differentiate shales by T1 components.

Low-field NMR struggles to capture the very short T2 signals from solid organic matter and tight pores in shales. The authors used a 300 MHz Bruker system with CPMG (22 µs echo time) and inversion-recovery CPMG sequences to measure T2 and T1-T2 correlations on crushed oil shales from many global formations, including Kukersite, Green River, Julia Creek and New Albany. Most signal lies at the shortest T2 times, and T1-T2 maps split into distinct T1 populations. Kukersite shows a long T1 (over ~10 s) plus a ~500 ms component at short T2, Green River and New Albany show ~50 ms T1 components, and Julia Creek has T1 below ~1 ms, suggesting more mobile kerogen in a restricted environment. The data differentiate oil shales, though full interpretation is still ongoing.

## Pore Characterization of Reservoir Rocks by Integrating SCAL and Petrography

- ID: SCA2014-052, year: 2014
- Authors: Xiangmin Zhang, Albert Hebing
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, mercury injection capillary pressure, T2 surface relaxivity, glauconitic sandstone, chalk, petrography SEM
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-052.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-052)

Integrates NMR T2, MICP and petrography on chalk and sandstones, showing matching pore distributions and deriving apparent T2 relaxivities of 9.29 µm/s for calcite and about 5.0 µm/s for glauconite.

Pore structure controls petrophysical and flow properties, so the authors combined lab NMR T2, high-pressure MICP, thin section and SEM on chalk, glauconitic, kaolinitic/illitic shaly and salt-cemented sandstones. NMR was run at full and irreducible saturation (after ultracentrifuge) on a 2.04 MHz MARAN-2; MICP went up to 60,000 psia on trims of the same plugs. T2 distributions generally resembled MICP pore-throat distributions. Chalk showed narrow uni-modal distributions, giving a calcite relaxivity of 9.29 µm/s. Glauconitic sandstones were bi-modal, with intra-grain glauconite pores of about 20-50 nm and apparent relaxivity of about 5.0 µm/s. Kaolinitic sandstones were less clearly bi-modal, illitic ones were ill-defined uni-modal with tails, and the salt-cemented sandstone with grain-boundary fractures gave a broad T2 distribution versus triple-peaked MICP.

## Developing a Model to Estimate Permeability from Other Petrophysical Data

- ID: SCA2014-063, year: 2014
- Authors: Babak Salimifard, Douglas W. Ruth, Derrick Green, Dragan Veselinovic
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: permeability prediction, NMR T2, mercury injection porosimetry, Timur-Coates, Swanson parameter, REV method
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-063.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-063)

Compares four permeability prediction methods (Timur-Coates, SDR, Swanson, REV) on 11 sandstone and carbonate plugs using NMR and MIP data; MIP-based methods predicted better.

Permeability prediction from pore structure data was tested on 11 plugs (5 sandstones, 6 carbonates) spanning 0.45–7000 mD and 9.3–28.8% porosity. NMR T2 at 100% brine saturation, high-pressure mercury injection and steady-state gas permeability (Klinkenberg and Forchheimer corrected) were measured. Four models were compared: Timur-Coates, SDR, Swanson and the REV method based on Purcell's model modified with electrical data. Swanson's method predicted best but only after fitting C and m to the data. The REV method needs no fitting parameters and was the most consistent, though samples 4 and 10 (vugular limestones) fell outside the factor-of-2 trust region. Overall MIP-based methods outperformed NMR-based ones, and Timur-Coates outperformed SDR.

## NMR Petrophysics for Tight Oil Shale Enabled by Core Resaturation

- ID: SCA2014-073, year: 2014
- Authors: Ravinath Kausik, Kamilla Fellah, Erik Rylander, Philip M. Singer, Richard E. Lewis, Steven M. Sinclair, Matador Resources
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 relaxometry, core resaturation, tight oil shale, Vycor porous glass, T1/T2 ratio wettability, Eagle Ford
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-073.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-073)

High-pressure resaturation of Eagle Ford shale cores with formation oil and brine, combined with 2D T1-T2 NMR, separates organic-pore oil, inorganic-pore oil, and bitumen/bound water.

Fluid loss during core retrieval makes NMR on native shale cores unrepresentative of reservoir conditions. The authors built a high-pressure resaturation cell (up to 2,000 psi) and validated it on 4 nm Vycor glass, where NMR porosity (32.9-33.9) matched helium (about 31) and BET porosity (about 31.7). Lower Eagle Ford shale fragments were resaturated with 42° API formation oil and brine and measured by 2D T1-T2 NMR at 2 MHz. Re-injected oil in organic pores appears at T2 of 1-10 ms with T1/T2 of 3-8, oil in inorganic pores at T2 above 10 ms with T1/T2 of 1-2, and bitumen plus clay-bound water below 1.5 ms with T1/T2 of 6-15. Injected brine appears at T2 of 6-80 ms with T1/T2 of 1.3-2.5, so oil T1/T2 discriminates organic from inorganic pores better than water does.

## Mapping NMR T2 to Capillary Pressure

- ID: SCA2014-076, year: 2014
- Authors: Andrea Valori, Farhan Ali, Ahmad Al-Zoukani, Reza Taherian
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, differential T2 distribution, capillary pressure, pore body to throat ratio, surface relaxivity, centrifuge desaturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-076.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-076)

Differential NMR T2 distributions measured during stepwise centrifuge desaturation of a carbonate plug give pore body to throat size ratios of about 0.25-2.25.

The paper tackles the difficulty of relating NMR T2 (pore body size) to capillary pressure (throat size). A water-saturated outcrop carbonate plug (1.5 in diameter, 2 in long) was desaturated by centrifuge in air in small pressure steps, with a T2 measurement at each step. Echo-decay data at consecutive pressures were subtracted in the time domain and inverted to give differential T2 distributions of the fluid produced at each step. Using a relaxivity of 20 μm/s, a cylindrical pore assumption and the Young-Laplace equation, the authors derived body-to-throat ratios of roughly 0.25-2.25. Values below 1 are unphysical, but a relaxivity above 7e-5 m/s would move all points into the physical region. Scatter is attributed to pores of similar size with different throats and vice versa, so results hold only on average.

## NMR T1-T2 Response of Moveable and Non-Moveable Fluids in Conventional and Unconventional Rocks

- ID: SCA2014-091, year: 2014
- Authors: Ali Tinni, Carl Sondergeld, Chandra Rai
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 maps, T1/T2 ratio, solid bitumen, Wolfcamp shale, moveable hydrocarbons, wax
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-091.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-091)

T1-T2 NMR maps at 2 MHz show moveable brine has T1/T2 near 1.5, moveable oil near 3, and solid bitumen and wax far higher (20 and 400).

Liquid-rich shales can contain high-viscosity or solid bitumen that is not producible but resembles moveable oil on conventional logs. The authors acquired T1-T2 maps (2 MHz, TE = 100 µs) on bulk fluids, brine-saturated Berea, Lyons and Bossier sandstones, two Wolfcamp shales, and wax and solid bitumen. Bulk fluids show T1=T2, brine-saturated sandstones show T1/T2 = 1.5, and methane-saturated Berea shows 3. In Wolfcamp shales, native and brine-imbibed signal has T1/T2 of 1.3, while dodecane imbibition gives 3 and 2.5. Solid bitumen and wax have T1/T2 of 20 and 400, falling to 2-3 when melted. Solid wax in Berea keeps a ratio of 400, so solids are unaffected by surface relaxation.

## Reservoir Rock Typing Using NMR & Centrifuge

- ID: SCA2014-096, year: 2014
- Authors: Jorge Costa
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 cut-off, ultracentrifuge capillary pressure, reservoir rock typing, carbonate pore size distribution, free fluid index, irreducible water saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-096.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-096)

Combines NMR T2 spectra and air-brine centrifuge desaturation on nine carbonate plugs to derive T2 cut-offs, finding cut-offs vary with depth and microporosity rather than being constant.

The paper addresses static and dynamic reservoir rock typing in a heterogeneous cyclic Middle East carbonate. Nine plugs from up-, mid- and down-structure positions in a transition zone were characterized by NMR T2 and conventional core analysis, then desaturated in five to six steps by multispeed air-brine ultracentrifuge with NMR scans after each step. NMR T2 distributions were unimodal but shifted to shorter times with depth, consistent with a shift from grain-supported to mud-supported limestone and falling permeability. T2 cut-offs from fully saturated versus Swirr spectra ranged from about 174-220 ms up-structure to 61-78 ms down-structure, with free fluid index near 0.2-0.3% for the down-structure plugs. The cut-off is not a universal value and depends on microporosity, while T2 mean is suggested as a reliable grouping parameter for static rock types.

## Characterizing Fluid Presence and Transport in Rock Cores at Reservoir-Like Conditions via Spatially Resolved NMR Relaxation/Diffusion Maps

- ID: SCA2015-013, year: 2015
- Authors: Huabing Liu, Mark Hunter, Sergei Obruchkov, Evan McCarney, Mitch Robison, Robin Dykstra, Petrik Galvosas
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: spatially resolved NMR, D-T2 correlation, overburden cell, water flooding, residual oil saturation, low-field NMR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-013.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-013)

An NMR-compatible overburden cell in a 2 MHz analyzer enables spatially resolved T1, T2 and D-T2 measurements during water flooding of oil-saturated limestone at up to 80°C and 1000 psi.

Fluid properties in reservoir rocks depend on temperature and pressure, so the authors built a non-magnetic overburden cell with PFPE confining fluid inside a 2 MHz NMR Rock Core Analyzer with a 1D gradient coil. An Edwards Brown limestone plug (36.5% porosity, 90 mD) saturated with diesel was measured at 1000 psi confining pressure and 25, 55 and 80°C using phase-encoded spatially resolved T1 and T2 and frequency-encoded spatially resolved D-T2. T1, T2 and D-T2 distributions shifted to longer times with temperature, consistent with decreasing oil viscosity, and the porosity profile showed the plug's heterogeneity. During water flooding up to 2.2 PV at each temperature, oil saturation dropped quickly and plateaued after about 1 PV, with further decrease at higher temperature. Residual oil saturation stayed above 50% even at 80°C, which the authors attribute to the high flood rate (about 0.25 mL/s).

## Comparison of Pore Size Distribution by NMR Relaxation and NMR Cryoporometry in Shales

- ID: SCA2015-025, year: 2015
- Authors: M. Fleury, R. Fabre, J.B.W. Webber
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR cryoporometry, T2 relaxation, pore coupling, Gibbs-Thomson, T2-store-T2 exchange, shale pore size distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-025.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-025)

NMR cryoporometry and T2 relaxation are compared on calibrated glass mixtures and a shale; cryoporometry gives true pore sizes while T2 is distorted by diffusive pore coupling.

T2 distributions in shales may not reflect pore size because of diffusive coupling between pores. The authors compared NMR cryoporometry (Gibbs-Thomson, kGT=58.2 K.nm, melting cycle, Hahn echo detection) with T2 relaxation on a mixture of 15 and 139 nm calibrated porous glasses and on a Callovo-Oxfordian shale (14.8% porosity, 7.3 nD). On the glass mixture, cryoporometry gave pore sizes of 14 and 147 nm and phase fractions matching weighed values (26/27/47% vs 27/27.3/45.6%), whereas the T2 at 30°C was distorted and T2-store-T2 showed off-diagonal exchange peaks. With inter-grain water frozen at -0.15°C, two distinct T2 peaks (20 and 138 ms) recovered the correct fractions. In the shale, cryoporometry gave a nearly uniform distribution from 2 to 100 nm (about 2 decades wide) while T2 was log-normal and about 1 decade wide, consistent with a diffusion length (~1.4 µm) far exceeding pore size; MICP gave a throat peak near 40 nm.

## Carbonate NMR Permeability Estimates Based on the Winland-Pittman MICP Approach

- ID: SCA2015-037, year: 2015
- Authors: Edmilson Helton Rios, Adam Keith Moss, Timothy Neil Pritchard, Ana Beatriz Guedes Domingues
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR permeability, Winland-Pittman, MICP, T2 saturation cutoff, chalk, size scaling
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-037.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-037)

Adapts the Winland-Pittman MICP critical-aperture idea to NMR T2 cumulative saturation cutoffs, giving better chalk permeability estimates than the logarithmic-mean estimator, especially after size scaling.

Standard NMR permeability estimators based on the T2 logarithmic mean perform poorly in carbonates. The authors adapt the Winland-Pittman MICP approach by choosing a T2 value at a fixed cumulative saturation cutoff, and then correlating it with NMR porosity and permeability. They studied 29 Cretaceous chalk plugs (14 North Sea, 15 Middle East) with MICP, routine gas permeability and porosity, and 2 MHz NMR T2 measurements. T2 distributions were also size-scaled using the ratio of MICP to NMR logarithmic means. With pure T2, R² rose from 0.70 (Seevers-Kenyon) to 0.81 at an 85% cutoff; with size-scaling, it rose from 0.89 to 0.95.

## Study of Rock Pore Space by Combination of Direct and Indirect Techniques

- ID: SCA2015-042, year: 2015
- Authors: Aleksandr Denisenko, Ivan Yakimchuk, Boris Sharchilev
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 map, surface relaxivity, X-ray microtomography, FIB-SEM, pore size distribution, internal gradients
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-042.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-042)

NMR T1 pore size distributions of a West Siberian sandstone are validated against micro-CT, SEM and FIB-SEM, giving consistent relaxivity across pore scales.

NMR gives indirect pore sizes that depend on surface relaxivity, which may vary within a rock and be distorted by internal gradients. The authors combined 20.6 MHz NMR T1 and T2 with micro-CT (2.2 and 0.5 µm pixels), 2D SEM and FIB-SEM on an 8 mm by 10 mm well-sorted West Siberian sandstone plug (porosity 27.8%, permeability 407 mD). Matching T1 to the CT distribution gave a relaxivity of 7.4 µm/s and a micro-CT-invisible microporosity of 29.7% of total porosity (8.2 p.u.). T2 matching gave 24 µm/s, which is overestimated because of internal magnetic gradients, so T1 was preferred. FIB-SEM (50–1000 nm) and SEM (0.4–40 µm) distributions agreed with the small-pore NMR response, indicating similar relaxivity in large and small pores. Comparison of 22 mm and 8 mm plugs showed that coring and drilling fluid alter microporosity near plug edges.

## Numerical Modeling of NMR Surface Relaxation in Porous Media with Improved Pore Surface Area Evaluation and Derivation of Interfacial Absorption Probability

- ID: SCA2015-049, year: 2015
- Authors: Danyong Li, Xuefeng Liu, Feng Huang, Tianpeng Zhao, Weifeng Lv, Sven Roth
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR random walk simulation, killing probability, surface relaxivity, marching cubes surface area, digital rock, tight gas sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-049.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-049)

A new killing probability and marching-cubes smoothed surface area improve random-walk NMR simulations, correcting overestimated surface area and leftward-shifted T2 distributions on digital rock images.

Random-walk NMR simulations on voxelized pore images overestimate surface area, forcing unphysical tuning of surface relaxivity. The authors derive a new killing probability that includes surface-to-volume ratio and global hitting probability of the pore space, and evaluate surface area with an optimized marching cubes algorithm. On standard geometries, the new method gives sphere surface areas close to theory (e.g., 341 vs 314 µm² for 5 µm radius, versus 486 µm² previously) and T2 closer to theoretical values. On digital images of sandstone, carbonate and three tight gas sandstones, surface area decreases by roughly 25-30% and T2 shifts to longer times, with larger correction in tighter, more tortuous rocks.

## Chemically-Selective 3D MRI of Laboratory Oil-Water Displacement Processes

- ID: SCA2016-015, year: 2016
- Authors: N.P. Ramskilla, I. Busha, A.J. Sederman, M.D. Mantlea, B.C. Angerb, M. Appelb, L.F. Gladden
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: compressed sensing RARE, chemically-selective MRI, 3D saturation imaging, dodecane drainage, Estaillades limestone, chemical shift
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-015.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-015)

Chemically-selective compressed-sensing RARE MRI gives independent 3D dodecane and water images in 17 minutes each during drainage of Estaillades limestone, without dopants.
