Core floods with heterogeneous fluid distributions need fast 3D imaging of both phases, ideally without dopants or isotopic substitution that alter the fluids. The authors added a chemically-selective preconditioning stage (Gaussian pulse plus homospoil gradients) to a compressed-sensing RARE sequence on a 2 T magnet. They imaged dodecane drainage at 0.1 ml/min into a water-saturated Estaillades limestone plug (38 mm by 68 mm, porosity 29%). Oil and water NMR peaks were well resolved, and with 25% k-space sampling each 3D image (256×128×128) took 17 min, giving final saturations of Sw 0.62 and So 0.38 after 7 PV. Dodecane advanced as discrete clusters before forming connected pathways, a low-saturation region suggested a low-permeability zone, and oil gains matched water losses in difference maps. Fully sampled spin-warp would have allowed 25 PV injection per image versus 0.2 PV here.

## Magnetic Resonance Imaging of Core Flooding in a Metal Core Holder

- ID: SCA2016-019, year: 2016
- Authors: Ming Li, Dan Xiao, Mojtaba Shakerian, Armin Afrough, Fred Goora Florin Marica, Laura Romero-Zerón, Bruce Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: MRI core flooding, Hastelloy core holder, pi-EPI, SPRITE, SE-SPI T2 mapping, supercritical CO2 flooding
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-019.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-019)

A Hastelloy metal core holder with internal RF probe and gradient waveform correction enables fast 3D pi-EPI and SPRITE/SE-SPI MRI of polymer and supercritical CO2 floods in Berea.

Core flooding MRI has been limited by low sensitivity, slow 3D imaging and the lack of high-pressure, non-magnetic core holders. The authors built a Hastelloy-C276 core holder rated to 5000 psi and 135 °C with the RF probe inside the metal case, and applied gradient waveform correction to counter eddy currents, giving rise times under 100 µs. In a 0.2 T system, 3D pi-EPI imaging (16 min per image, 1.4 mm resolution) tracked water and polymer flooding of a Berea plug (74 mD, 19% porosity): oil saturation fell from 0.68 to 0.42 by waterflood and to 0.38 with polymer, a 5.9% OOIP gain. During supercritical CO2 flooding of decane at 40 °C, 1D SPRITE profiles showed non-piston-like displacement with recovery near 100% OOIP, and SE-SPI T2 maps showed a ~90 ms peak and a growing ~2 ms component. View sharing could improve 3D temporal resolution from 16 to 2 minutes.

## Estimations by Incorporating the Surface

- ID: SCA2016-047, year: 2016
- Authors: Andre Souza, Giovanna Carneiro, Austin Boyd, Martin Hurlimann, Willian Trevizan, Bernardo Coutinho, Vinicius Machado, Rodrigo Bagueira, Fluminense Federal
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR surface relaxivity, Timur-Coates permeability, V/S cutoff, D-T2 experiment, irreducible water saturation, T2 cutoff
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-047.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-047)

Using D-T2 measured surface relaxivity to define a V/S cutoff instead of a T2 cutoff improves Timur-Coates permeability estimates more than twofold and supports Swi estimation.

NMR permeability estimators such as Timur-Coates rely on a T2 cutoff that implicitly assumes a constant surface relaxivity. The authors measured ρ2 directly with 2D D-T2 experiments on 16 sandstone and 10 carbonate outcrop cores and converted T2 distributions to V/S distributions, applying a fixed V/S cutoff. Permeability error (σk) fell from 3.6 to 2.3 for sandstones (V/S cutoff 0.4 µm) and from 5.4 to 2.6 for carbonates (1.8 µm), and 10 twin plugs confirmed the result across about five orders of magnitude in permeability. Correlating T2LM with the cutoff-weighted FFI/BVI gave R² of 0.26 for carbonates with T2 but 0.88 when ρ2 was included; sandstones improved from 0.80 to 0.87. This supports estimating FFI/BVI and Swi from ρ2, though laboratory Swi comparisons are still in progress.

## Surface Relaxivity Estimation and NMR-MICP Matching in Diffusionaly Coupled Rocks

- ID: SCA2016-059, year: 2016
- Authors: Pedro Mesquita, Andre Souza, Giovanna Carneiro, Austin Boyd, Flavio Ferreira, Patrick Machado, Vivek Anand, Lawrence Schwartz
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: surface relaxivity, NMR-MICP matching, diffusional coupling, dual porosity random walk simulation, carbonates, T2 distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-059.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-059)

Random-walk NMR simulations in a dual-porosity model, with diffusional coupling switched off, give T2 distributions that match MICP better and yield improved surface relaxivity estimates in three carbonates.

NMR-MICP matching to estimate surface relaxivity fails when diffusional coupling between micro- and macro-pores distorts T2 distributions. The authors calibrated a micro-grain consolidation dual-porosity random-walk model to the measured T2 distributions of three carbonates (H11, H23, Silurian Dolomite), then reran the simulations with a barrier preventing walkers from moving between micro and macro pores. The decoupled T2 distributions correlated much better with MICP, and matched effective relaxivities were lower than from the coupled data (0.74 vs 1.72, 1.08 vs 1.76, and 6.17 vs 9.78 µm/s). A coupling parameter α computed from model parameters and Brownstein number was 1.38 (H11), 2.37 (H23) and 12.99 (Silurian Dolomite), consistent with the inverse relation between α and coupling strength seen in the T2 distributions. Imperfect matching in H11 suggests different pore families may have different shapes, body-to-throat ratios or relaxivities.

## Tortuosity Prediction from Oxygen Diffusion via T2 (z, t) Relaxation NMR

- ID: SCA2016-063, year: 2016
- Authors: Igor Shikhov, Christoph H. Arns
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: dissolved oxygen, paramagnetic relaxation enhancement, T2(z,t) NMR, effective diffusion coefficient, tortuosity, Mount Gambier limestone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-063.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-063)

Spatially resolved T2(z,t) NMR tracks dissolved oxygen diffusing into Mount Gambier limestone, giving effective diffusion coefficients and tortuosity that capture saturation effects, compared with image-based conductivity tortuosity.

Dissolved oxygen shortens NMR relaxation times, and the paper turns this into a tracer for transport in saturated rock. A Mount Gambier limestone plug (porosity 52%, permeability 4.47 D) initially saturated with oxygen-free decane was exposed to air-saturated decane at the inlet, and T2(z,t) was measured with 3 mm resolution. Fitting an analytical 1D diffusion solution gave effective oxygen diffusion coefficients of 6.9e-5 cm2/s (bulk decane), 6.1e-5 (So=100%) and 3.7e-5 (So=51%, rest D2O). Diffusion-based tortuosity was 0.451 at So=1 and 0.248 at So=0.5 (ratio 1.82), versus 0.186 and 0.054 (ratio 3.44) from Laplace conductivity on a micro-CT image assuming water-wet conditions. The fully water-saturated core showed no T2 difference between air and oxygen-free states, indicating water-wet behavior. Oxygen-based tortuosity is higher than conductivity tortuosity because transport between phases is possible.

## Determining the Saturation Exponent Based on NMR Pore Structure Information

- ID: SCA2016-074, year: 2016
- Authors: Xu Hongjun, Fan Yiren, Zhou Cancan, Hu Falong, Li Chaoliu, Yu Jun, Li Changxi
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: saturation exponent, NMR T2 relaxation, tight sandstone, resistivity index, MICP pore throat radius, centrifuge desaturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-074.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-074)

Tight sandstone plugs were desaturated by centrifuge with NMR T2 and resistivity measured. The log-mean T2 ratio versus Sw gives a saturation exponent tied to pore structure.

In tight sandstones the Archie saturation exponent often departs from 2, and brine is hard to drain for resistivity index measurements. Thirteen Chang7 plugs (porosity 6.2-12.2%, permeability 0.012-0.262 mD) were centrifuged at 150, 600 and 940 psi, with 2 MHz NMR T2 and RI measured at each step, plus MICP. The log-mean T2 correlated with mean throat radius, and the electrical saturation exponent rose as pore structure worsened: about 1.5-2 for good plugs such as N94, and above 2 where micro and nano throats dominate. Log-mean T2 and resistivity followed a power-law relationship during drainage. A new exponent was derived from the ratio of log-mean T2 at full saturation to that at partial saturation versus Sw, and extended to any saturation (down to 30%) using a linear relation between log-mean T2 and the T2 mean value. The NMR-centrifuge exponents (about 1.6-2.2) were compared with RI values in a table, while the any-saturation exponents were lower (1.1-1.7).

## NMR Fluid Typing Using Independent Component Analysis Applied to Water-Oil-displacement Laboratory Data

- ID: SCA2016-088, year: 2016
- Authors: Pedro A. Romero, Manuel M. Rincón
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, independent component analysis, blind source separation, fluid typing, water-oil displacement, centrifuge drainage
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-088.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-088)

Independent component analysis applied to T2 spectra from centrifuge water-oil displacement separates oil and water signals and estimates water saturation in close agreement with displacement values.

NMR fluid typing is difficult when oil and water T2 spectra overlap, and diffusion-based methods need special hardware and inversion. The authors apply blind source separation with the fastica ICA algorithm to T2 distributions measured at four water saturation stages during centrifuge water-oil displacement on two high-permeability core plugs (635 and 1038 mD, porosity 13.7 and 15.3%). ICA returns two independent components interpreted as oil and water spectra plus a mixing matrix, from which saturations are computed. The oil component resembles the T2 of the 100% oil-saturated sample. The water component is bimodal, with a surface-relaxation peak and a bulk-like peak attributed to oil layers separating water from the pore wall. For sample A, ICA water saturations (38.4, 56.6, 81.4%) compare with displacement values (41.9, 58.7, 71.3%), despite non-uniform saturation and no hydrogen index correction.

## Study of Pore Size Evaluation for Low Permeability Rock Core Sample

- ID: SCA2016-096, year: 2016
- Authors: Mai SHIMOKAWARA, Yasuyuki AKITA, Yasuyuki MINO, Satoru TAKAHASHI
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: nitrogen adsorption hysteresis, mercury injection capillary pressure, NMR T2, BJH analysis, t-plot, pore shape
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-096.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-096)

Nitrogen adsorption hysteresis, mercury injection and NMR on tight Japanese silty mudstones show wide pore size ranges and pores combining slit and cylindrical shapes.

Permeability of tight rocks is controlled by pore connectivity, which is hard to evaluate. Outcrop siltstone and silty mudstone samples from 200 to 1000 m depth (permeability below 1 nd, NMR porosity about 3-6%) were characterized by mercury injection, N2 adsorption with repeated hysteresis loops, and NMR. MICP pore throat peaks shift smaller with depth (2.9E-02 um in sample 2 versus 5.1E-03 um in sample 8), and surface area decreases with depth. Isotherms are IUPAC type IV, and t-plot analysis indicates wide pore size distributions with slit-type pores. BJH and INNES analyses of adsorption and desorption branches, compared with NMR's single peak, indicate pores comprising both slit and cylindrical shapes, with sizes from 0.6 nm to 6 um.

## Quick and Simple Porosity Measurement at the Well Site

- ID: SCA2017-013, year: 2017
- Authors: M.J. Dick, D. Green, T. Kenney, D. Veselinovic, J. Tallarita, M.A. Smith
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, drill cuttings porosity, surface water removal, centrifugation, crushed core, benchtop NMR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-013.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-013)

Benchtop NMR porosity of crushed rock and cuttings is hampered by surface water; centrifuging at 1100 rpm removes it, giving porosity within about 3% on crushed sandstone.

Porosity on drill cuttings is hard to obtain while drilling, and NMR could provide porosity and pore size distribution on cuttings. T2 distributions of shale core plugs, crushed plugs and cuttings agreed, but saturated crushed sandstone overestimated porosity by 2.4 to 6.8 porosity units because of water clinging to grain surfaces. Using a cleaned sandstone plug crushed to 1-3 mm pieces as reference, several surface-water removal methods were compared: screening, D2O washing, surfactant in fluorinert, CuSO4-doped water, sonication and centrifugation. Only centrifugation worked well, with 1100 rpm for about 35 min giving porosity within 3-4% of the plug value, versus errors of 20-97% for the other methods. Applied to field cuttings from ALS, porosities remained too high (about 54%) despite the surface water being removed, and the cause (mud contamination or over-crushing) is unresolved.

## Comparison of Dispersion Described by Tracer Tests and NMR Propagators Using the Smim Model

- ID: SCA2017-021, year: 2017
- Authors: M. Fleury, S. Stona, D. Bauer, M.C. Neel
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR propagator, SMIM model, breakthrough curve, dispersion coefficient, mobile-immobile model, grain pack
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-021.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-021)

NMR propagator data fitted with the SMIM model predict tracer breakthrough curves in a bimodal grain pack, but tracer outlet-line dispersion limits comparison in a homogeneous bead pack.

Dispersion is usually derived from tracer breakthrough curves, while PFG-NMR propagators measure displacement distributions directly. The authors compared both on a homogeneous glass bead pack (50-70 µm) and a bimodal pack of crushed micritic wackestone, using KI tracer with UV detection and 20 MHz NMR propagators at 600-1200 ml/h. NMR signals were inverted with the SMIM (stochastic mobile-immobile) model, and the fitted parameters were fed to a numerical tracer model to predict breakthrough curves. For the bimodal pack, the fitted immobile/mobile ratio K was 0.21-0.36, close to the intra/inter-granular porosity ratio of 0.30, and the early breakthrough at V/Vp~0.8 was reproduced. For the bead pack, NMR dispersivity (16-19 µm) was below the tracer-derived value because outlet-line dispersion limits the tracer setup to dispersivities above about 60 µm.

## Relative Permeability Experiments Monitored by NMR

- ID: SCA2017-023, year: 2017
- Authors: P.O. Mangane, G. Dubes, B. Nicot
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR saturation monitoring, D2O brine, spatially resolved T2, unsteady-state relative permeability, capillary end effect, history matching
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-023.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-023)

Unsteady-state waterfloods with D2O brine on Bentheimer sandstone and Sireuil carbonate were monitored by low-field NMR, giving saturation profiles, pore-class oil distribution and Kr history matching without dopants.

Waterflood monitoring by X-rays needs dopants that can alter wettability, so the authors used D2O brine so that only oil is visible to a 2 MHz NMR spectrometer. Two aged outcrop plugs, Bentheimer sandstone (unimodal T2) and Sireuil carbonate (bimodal T2), were flooded with stepwise increased rates to reduce capillary end effects. 1D T2 and saturation profiles gave average remaining oil of 15.5% (Bentheimer) and 36% (Sireuil), agreeing with gravimetric measurements within 5 saturation units, and the unchanged T2 peak showed no wettability alteration. Spatially resolved T2 maps separated oil in small and large pores of the carbonate and revealed saturation gradients before flooding that X-ray would not detect. Kr was history matched (CYDAR, LET and Logbeta functions) for Bentheimer only: cross-point above 50% Sw, Krw(Sor) about 0.15, and Corey exponents 2.1 (Kro) and 3.6 (Krw), consistent with water-wet behavior. The Sireuil end effect was longer (1.5 cm vs 0.6 cm).

## Magnetic Resonance Imaging with a Variable Field Superconducting Magnet that can be rotated for Vertical or Horizontal Operation

- ID: SCA2017-024, year: 2017
- Authors: Sarah Vashaee, Ming Li, Bryce MacMillan, Razieh Enjilela, Derrick P. Green, F. Marica, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: variable field MRI, cryogen-free superconducting magnet, magnetic susceptibility mismatch, 19F oil saturation imaging, 23Na MRI, SPRITE
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-024.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-024)

A cryogen-free, rotatable 0.01–3 T variable-field MRI magnet is demonstrated for core plugs, controlling susceptibility effects and enabling 1H, 19F and 23Na imaging with one RF probe.

Susceptibility mismatch in rocks shortens T2* as field rises, so the optimal MRI field for core plugs is sample dependent. The authors installed a cryogen-free superconducting magnet (0.01–3 T, 17 cm bore, 325 kg) that can be rotated horizontal to vertical. SPRITE imaging of Bentheimer, Berea and Wallace plugs at 1.5 T and 3 T showed lower intensity at 3 T for Berea and Wallace but higher for Bentheimer. Rotation to vertical shifted the 1H resonance by 1.95 kHz (2 kHz predicted) from the earth's field. Fixing the probe at 63.5 MHz, 19F oil-saturation imaging at 1.6 T monitored waterflooding of Bentheimer from Soi 0.79 to Sor 0.33 with 12-minute 2D FSE slices. 23Na at 3 T gave a unimodal T2 peak at 44 ms versus 1H peaks at 400 and 28 ms, with 1H SNR about 100–160 times higher.

## Intepretation of NMR Response to Hydrocarbons: Application on Miscible EOR Experiments

- ID: SCA2017-028, year: 2017
- Authors: S. T. Dang, C. H. Sondergeld, C. S. Rai
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 maps, shale hydrocarbons, huff-and-puff CO2 EOR, Eagle Ford shale, Rock-Eval S1, n-alkane relaxation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-028.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-028)

NMR of n-alkanes, distilled oils and Eagle Ford shale at 31°C sees only liquid-phase hydrocarbons (C17 and lighter); T1-T2 tracks mobility and CO2 huff-and-puff recovery.

Routine core NMR at 25-35°C may underestimate movable hydrocarbons in shale because heavier components are solid and invisible. The authors measured pure n-alkanes (C6-C20) and six synthetic oils made by distilling a crude, using 2 and 12 MHz benchtop instruments at 31°C with 114 µs echo spacing, plus GC-FID and Rock-Eval pyrolysis. NMR signal appeared only for alkanes up to C17, and a frozen C17 sample showed signal growing as it melted. NMR amplitude agreed with Rock-Eval S1 (C17-) for oils and Eagle Ford samples. T1/T2 separation grew with viscosity and was stronger at 12 MHz; at 2 MHz, it split only above about 1000 cP. In CO2 huff-and-puff on crushed Eagle Ford (3500 psi, 170°F), NMR T2 and T1-T2 maps showed light HC loss and a heavier remaining fluid, giving 52% total recovery over 5 cycles.

## A Method for Rapid Determination of Oil and Water Content in Geological Formations

- ID: SCA2017-034, year: 2017
- Authors: D. Vartsky, M. B. Goldberg, V. Dangendorf, I. Israelashvili, I. Mor, D. Bar, M. Weierganz, B. Bromberger, A. Breskin
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: fast neutron resonance transmission, neutron radiography, oil and water saturation, non-destructive core analysis, time-of-flight spectroscopy, porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-034.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-034)

Fast neutron resonance transmission radiography is used to separate oil and water weight fractions in 10 cm Berea and Indiana Limestone cores, giving porosity and saturations with 13-30% uncertainty.

Conventional core analysis is largely destructive, and thermal neutron or X-ray methods cannot distinguish oil from water in thick cores. The authors apply Fast Neutron Resonance Transmission (FNRT) radiography, using 1.7-4.2 MeV time-of-flight spectra at the PTB cyclotron, to 10 cm cubes of Berea Sandstone and Indiana Limestone saturated with water or odorless mineral spirit. Attenuation coefficients measured on dry rock, oil and water were used to solve for areal densities by least squares with bootstrapping or Bayesian inference. Measured fluid weight fractions, such as 5.57% oil in limestone (expected 5.46%) and 10.2% water in sandstone (expected 10%), agreed with gravimetric values, and porosity matched well. Saturation uncertainties were large for limestone (13-30%), attributed to poor counting statistics in the calibration standards; absent fluids were consistent with zero. The authors estimate that an operational facility could analyze a core within minutes.

## Revisiting NMR Core-Log Calibration

- ID: SCA2017-064, year: 2017
- Authors: Wim Looyestijn, Xiangmin Zhang, Albert Hebing
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: NMR T2 cutoff, bound water, MICP capillary pressure, desaturation modeling, NMR log calibration, hydrocarbon correction
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-064.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-064)

Shows that NMR T2 distributions can be converted to capillary curves and desaturation modeled, giving height-dependent T2-cutoffs and NMR log saturations validated against resistivity-derived saturations.

A single T2 cutoff calibrated at one desaturation pressure (500 psi air/brine, centrifuge) is valid only at one height above the free water level. The authors calibrate NMR T2 distributions against MICP capillary curves on sandstone plugs using a Pc–T2 relationship (Pc = 10^A/T2^B), and find the conversion is reasonably consistent between roughly 3 and 300 ms. They also model desaturation by a T2-dependent amplitude profile that reproduces the measured desaturated spectra, allowing prediction of T2 cutoffs at other pressures. The mean cutoff was 32 ms, equal to the sandstone default. Applied to an NMR log in a gas-bearing sandstone drilled with oil-base mud, with hydrocarbon correction, the NMR capillary-curve saturation reproduces the transition zone and FWL, matches Archie saturation, and shows less scatter.

## Enhanced Spatial Resolution of Magnetic Resonance Measurement and Application to Whole Core Shale

- ID: SCA2017-065, year: 2017
- Authors: Jin-Hong Chen, Yang Zhao, Daniel T. Georgi, Mohammad Delshad, Qiushi Sun, Hyung Kwak
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: whole core NMR, spatial resolution, rf-coil response map, shale T2 spectra, fluid content, stepwise deconvolution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-065.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-065)

A stepwise acquisition and coil-response deconvolution method gives 1-inch resolution NMR T2 and fluid content on whole shale cores using a 4-inch coil, without gradients.

NMR spatial resolution is normally limited by rf-coil length, and PFG slice selection fails for short-T2 rocks such as shale. The authors step a core through the coil in increments equal to the desired resolution, measure the coil response map with a doped-water disk standard, and solve the resulting over-determined linear system by least squares to recover coil-end-effect-free signals. They applied it to 35 whole shale cores (4 in diameter, 1 ft long) from two Middle East wells using a 1.83 MHz Ecotek spectrometer with a 4-inch coil, TE 0.17 ms, needing 17 acquisitions per core. One-inch resolution T2 spectra and fluid content were obtained, and fluid content variations matched core breaks seen in CT images. Average fluid content was about 8 p.u. in the top zone of well (a) and most of well (b), and profiles revealed zonation between wells.

## Effectively combining NMR measurements and capillary pressure saturation data: A new joint inversion approach

- ID: SCA2017-079, year: 2017
- Authors: Thomas Hiller, Norbert Klitzsch
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, joint inversion, angular pore model, capillary pressure saturation, surface relaxivity, pore size distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-079.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-079)

A joint inversion of NMR T2 and capillary pressure saturation data with angular pores yields pore size distribution, surface relaxivity and pore shape without calibration.

NMR interpretation normally needs a surface relaxivity calibration and assumes cylindrical pores, which cannot represent residual water in crevices. The authors extend a joint NMR/capillary-pressure-saturation inversion to triangular pores, solving for PSD and surface relaxivity with a regularized Levenberg-Marquardt scheme. On synthetic data with 0.5% noise the surface relaxivity was recovered (10.02 vs 10 µm/s). On a sintered borosilicate and a tight gas sandstone, the method gave a unimodal PSD for borosilicate that fits MICP data well and ρs of 1.4 and 1.1 µm/s. Kozeny-Carman permeabilities from the PSDs (5.9 D and 33 µD) were close to measured values (7 D and 10 µD). Using drainage plus imbibition NMR data recovered pore shape on synthetic data, with errors of about 5° for less acute triangles.

## Measuring Methane Adsorption in Shales Using NMR

- ID: SCA2017-089, year: 2017
- Authors: M.J. Dick, C. Muir, D. Veselinovic, D. Green
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, methane adsorption, shale gas in place, T1-T2 map, Eagle Ford shale, gas isotherm
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-089.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-089)

NMR T2 and T1-T2 monitoring of methane uptake in an Eagle Ford shale and a sandstone separates absorption from adsorption by uptake time, but not in the spectra.

Conventional gas isotherms are measured volumetrically on crushed samples, which destroys the core and gives no pore-size information. The authors exposed an Eagle Ford shale plug and a Carbon Tan sandstone plug to methane at 2000 psi under 2500 psi confinement in a GeoSpec 2-75 with an overburden probe, acquiring T2 over time and T1-T2 maps. The sandstone filled to about its pore volume within minutes and then stayed constant. The shale filled to 75% of pore volume quickly and reached full pore volume after about 1.5 hours. It then took up roughly another 35% of pore volume over several hours by adsorption, giving about 65% absorbed and 35% adsorbed gas and a total of about 8 scm3/cm3. T2 spectra and T1-T2 maps showed methane filling organic and inorganic pores at similar rates, so adsorbed and absorbed gas could not be distinguished in the spectra, probably because of fast exchange or similar T2 values.

## An Investigation into Different Correlation Methods between NMR T2 Distributions and Primary Drainage Capillary Pressure Curves Using an Extensive Sandstone Database

- ID: SCA2018-010, year: 2018
- Authors: Adam K Moss, Tim Benson, Advanced Magnetic Resonance, Tony Barrow
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR T2 distribution, MICP, capillary pressure, surface relaxivity scaling factor, flow zone index, sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 5
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-010.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-010)

Three single and variable scaling-factor methods converting NMR T2 to capillary pressure were tested on sandstones; the variable Rho model, correlated with permeability and flow zone index, fit best.

The paper compares ways of converting brine-saturated NMR T2 distributions to primary drainage capillary pressure curves using MICP data from the ART NMR Sandstone Rock Catalogue (174 plugs, six orders of magnitude in permeability). Seventy-eight plugs with MICP and NMR porosity within 0.015 were used to build models. Three scaling-factor approaches were tested: a simple single Rho (median pore diameter over log-mean T2), a single Rho from matching NMR and reconstructed MICP data in the time domain, and a variable Rho power law of T2. Global correlations used nitrogen permeability, with samples split at a flow zone index of 0.83. On six test plugs the summed mean saturation differences were 34.99 (simple), 27.97 (inversion) and 18.86 (variable Rho), though the simple model fit best at the MICP inflexion point. The variable Rho model is therefore preferred, especially for gas or water-wet light-oil reservoirs drilled with water-based mud.

## Liquid Vapor Isotherms in Nano-Porous Media Under NMR Observation

- ID: SCA2018-048, year: 2018
- Authors: Aleksandr Denisenko
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 correlation, vapor sorption isotherms, porous glass, capillary condensation, nanopores, wettability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-048.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-048)

Combines vapor sorption with low-field NMR T2 and T1-T2 measurements on porous glass to track hexane and water adsorption/desorption, revealing layer and condensation behavior and surface interactions.

Characterizing nanoporous reservoir rocks requires methods beyond conventional ones, so the study couples vapor sorption isotherms with low-field NMR (20.6 MHz) on artificial porous glass. Samples were controlled pore glass (50-300 nm) and 4 and 10 nm porous glass, with hexane and water vapor stepped through relative pressures 0 to 1 while T2 and T1-T2 data were recorded. Hexane gave reversible isotherms without hysteresis, T1/T2 near unity and T2 rising with sorbate volume. Water showed hysteresis, T2 decreasing with uptake, and T1/T2 more than an order of magnitude higher than hexane, consistent with water-wet surfaces. In 50-nm CPG, T1-T2 maps split into two regions (T1/T2 about 4 at long T2, about 25 at short T2); in 10-nm glass hexane signal split into two T1/T2 regions at capillary condensation. The NMR-sorption method matches conventional gas sorption volumes while adding information on fluid-surface interaction.

## X-ray computed tomography supported by nuclear magnetic resonance and mercury porosimetry as novel approach in pore space characterization of tight sandstones

- ID: SCA2018-049, year: 2018
- Authors: Krakowska P, Puskarczyk E, Jędrychowski M, Habrat M, Madejski P, Dohnalik M
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: nano-CT, NMR T2 cut-off, mercury porosimetry, pore throat diameter, semivariogram, tight sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-049.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-049)

Combines NMR, MICP and micro-CT on 15 Polish tight sandstones, introducing ellipsoid-based pore throat diameter and semivariogram heterogeneity analysis from CT pore objects.

Pore space in tight gas sandstones is hard to characterize, so fifteen Paleozoic (Cambrian to Carboniferous) core samples from Polish wells deeper than 3000 m were measured with NMR, MICP and nano-CT. NMR T2 distributions were interpreted with sample-specific cut-offs from Gaussian or Weibull fits instead of the standard 3 and 33 ms, giving higher clay-bound water. MICP detected one to three fracture-porous systems (Swanson parameters), very low effective porosity and average pore diameters from about 0.007 to 100 µm. From CT, bone-analysis parameters were applied to pore objects; pore throat diameter was taken from the best-fit ellipsoid radii (minor radius below 25% of major), and enclosed-volume sphericity proved more reliable than the moment-of-inertia ratio. Semivariograms of pore centroids showed strong heterogeneity, with slight regionalization in only a few samples, and 3D visualization in poROSE was used to check the parameters.

## Measuring Relative Permeability With NMR

- ID: SCA2018-054, year: 2018
- Authors: M.J. Dick, D. Veselinovic, T. Kenney, D. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, unsteady-state relative permeability, D2O brine, Johnson-Bossler-Naumann, saturation profile, carbonate
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-054.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-054)

Unsteady-state relative permeability is measured on a carbonate by tracking dodecane saturation with NMR T2 during D2O flooding, with pressure drop logged simultaneously.

The paper presents an unsteady-state relative permeability measurement in which oil saturation is monitored in situ by NMR T2 rather than by effluent mass or volume. A low-permeability carbonate plug (0.05 mD, 2.74 mL pore volume) saturated with dodecane was confined at 4500 psi in an overburden NMR probe and flooded with NMR-invisible D2O brine at 0.1 ml/min. T2 spectra (about 3 min each) and saturation profiles were recorded about every 14 minutes while inlet and outlet pressures were logged. The T2 area gave oil produced versus time, with a 5 ml dead volume identified from a 50 min delay and a peak pressure of 1400 psi at breakthrough. After breakthrough, polynomial fits of oil produced and pressure drop were used with the Johnson-Bossler-Naumann method to derive oil and water relative permeability curves, available only above water saturation of about 0.4. Work is under way on flow-rate profiling by NMR to enable calculation before breakthrough.

## Supercritical Methane Diffusivity in Porous Media

- ID: SCA2018-055, year: 2018
- Authors: Nicholas J. Drenzek, Prem K. Bikkina, Jarred H. Kelsey, Clint P. Aichele, Jeffery L. White
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: supercritical methane, NMR diffusion, transverse relaxation T2, high-pressure high-field NMR, pore pressure detection, microCT petrophysics
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-055.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-055)

High-field NMR measured supercritical methane diffusivity and T2 in sandstone and limestone microplugs up to 62.1 MPa; pressure effects match models but are too small for downhole NMR.

The study asks whether pressure-driven changes in methane diffusion could be used to detect pore pressure with NMR logging. Kirby sandstone and Indiana limestone microplugs (3 mm x 10 mm), dry and at irreducible brine saturation, were measured with 400 MHz NMR at 60°C from 13.8 to 62.1 MPa, with bulk (annulus) and pore signals resolved. Diffusivities were ~1e-8 m2/s in the annulus, up to three times higher than in pores, and fell by a factor of 2-3 across the pressure range. Diffusivity also dropped by a factor of 1-3 as echo spacing increased from 8 to 64 ms, indicating restricted diffusion at longer times. Results agreed reasonably with mean free path model predictions, but the predicted ~40% change over a reservoir pressure gradient is too modest for current low-field NMR logging tools to quantify.

## Dynamic Adsorption-Diffusion Model for Simulating Gas Production in Shale

- ID: SCA2018-058, year: 2018
- Authors: Zehao Yang, Qian Sang, Yajun Li, Steven Bryant, Mingzhe Dong
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: shale gas production, dynamic adsorption-diffusion model, kerogen dissolved gas, adsorption/desorption kinetics, multilevel single-linkage optimization, diffusion coefficient
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-058.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-058)

An exact cylindrical dynamic adsorption-diffusion model is fitted to shale core gas production, separating free, adsorbed and dissolved gas stages and their shares (45.2%, 34.8%, 20%).

Shale gas production depends on free, adsorbed and kerogen-dissolved gas, and adsorption is treated here as time-dependent rather than instantaneous. The authors derive an exact solution of the dynamic adsorption-diffusion (DAD) model for radial flow in a cylindrical core (2.5 cm diameter, 5 cm long) under constant production pressure, check it against a finite-difference solution, and show that five series terms suffice. They fit the first 5 hours of a measured cumulative production curve using MLSL global optimization, obtaining Da = 1.03×10-7 m2/s, λ = 2.31×10-4 s-1 and µ = 3.04×10-4 s-1. Free gas dominates for about 12.8 minutes, then adsorbed gas dominates. After 5 hours, dissolved gas release is fitted with a plane diffusion model (h = 121 nm), giving Dd ≈ 5.26×10-19 m2/s. The stored gas shares are 45.2% free, 34.8% adsorbed and 20% dissolved.

## A New Apparatus for Coupled Low-field NMR And Ultrasonic Measurements in Rocks at Reservoir Conditions

- ID: SCA2019-023, year: 2019
- Authors: Paul R. J. Connolly, Joël Sarout, Jérémie Dautriat, Eric F. May, Michael L. Johns
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, ultrasonic P-wave velocity, NMR-compatible core holder, supercritical CO2 saturation, Gassmann fluid substitution, Bentheimer sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-023.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-023)

A new NMR-compatible core holder measures P-wave velocity and T2 relaxation on the same rock at reservoir conditions, validated on Berea and demonstrated on Bentheimer with CO2 and brine.

Rock physics models for fluid effects on elastic waves need saturation and pore-scale information that is usually measured indirectly. The authors retrofitted an NMR-compatible core holder with PEEK distribution plugs and ring piezo-ceramic transducers, operating to 22 MPa confining pressure and 45 °C in a 2 MHz NMR spectrometer. Earthing the coaxial lines and metal parts cut the NMR background noise from about 7-8 V to about 0.15 V, and keeping the transducers at the edge of the field of view preserved B0 homogeneity. Dry Berea P-wave velocities agreed closely with a reference triaxial cell during loading and unloading, with uncertainties of about 1%. On a Bentheimer core flooded with brine and supercritical CO2, P-wave velocity fell as CO2 saturation rose, and the largest change coincided with brine displacement from the largest pores, seen in T2-derived V/S distributions.

## Methane Isotherms and Magnetic Resonance Imaging in Shales

- ID: SCA2019-027, year: 2019
- Authors: M. J. Dick, D. Heagle, D. Veselinovic, D. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, methane adsorption isotherm, shale gas, Van der Waals pore gas, degassing diffusion, Bakken
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-027.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-027)

Low-field NMR T2 measurements with Van der Waals pore-gas correction yield methane adsorption isotherms on intact Barnett, Haynesville and Bakken shales, plus degassing diffusion behavior.

Conventional volumetric and low-pressure gas adsorption isotherms require crushed, dried samples and do not give pore-size information or work at reservoir pressure. The authors measured methane T2 distributions on three shales (Barnett, Lower Haynesville fragments, Bakken) at pressures up to 2000 psi, subtracting the dry background and removing free-methane signal by a T2 cutoff. Pore gas was computed from NMR porosity and the Van der Waals equation and subtracted from total gas to give adsorbed gas. Gas content stabilized after about 4 hours in the Barnett sample, and adsorbed methane at 1500 psi was about 0.02 mmol/g (Barnett) and 0.07 mmol/g (Haynesville), consistent with the literature. The high-TOC Bakken sample adsorbed more, and during degassing about 15 scm3/cm3 remained after two days; a diffusion model gave D of 2.6e-9 m2/s, suggesting advection also contributes.

## Novel technique to measure mutual bulk fluid diffusion using NMR 1-D gradient

- ID: SCA2019-042, year: 2019
- Authors: Son Dang, Carl Sondergeld, Chandra Rai
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR 1-D gradient profiling, hydrogen index, bulk diffusion coefficient, methane-oil diffusion, gas injection EOR, shale tortuosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-042.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-042)

NMR 1-D gradient profiling of hydrogen index across an oil-methane interface at 6000 psi yields methane concentration profiles and bulk diffusion coefficients of about 6.5-8.5e-10 m²/s.

Effective diffusion in tight rocks requires both tortuosity and bulk fluid diffusion coefficients, and conventional bulk measurements disturb pressure or fail above the minimum miscibility pressure when the interface blurs. The authors place Meramec dead oil and methane in an NMR-transparent ZrO2 cell (up to 10,000 psi) in a 2 MHz GeoSpec spectrometer at 35 °C. They record 1-D hydrogen index (HI) profiles over a 7 cm window every hour for 7 days at a constant 6000 psi, above MMP. HI in the oil falls with time, fastest near the interface, and all positions converge to the same HIfinal, which gives the maximum methane concentration in oil and the interface concentration Cs. Using the integral of HI to reduce noise and fitting Fick's second law, diffusion coefficients of 6.5e-10 to 8.5e-10 m²/s were obtained, with the apparent rate decreasing over time as an artifact of the finite cell volume.

## Effects of gas pressurization on the interpretation of NMR hydrocarbon measurements in organic rich shales

- ID: SCA2019-044, year: 2019
- Authors: Son Dang, Carl Sondergeld, Chandra Rai
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 maps, gas pressurization, organic-rich shale, huff-n-puff CO2, Rock-Eval S1, hydrocarbon remobilization
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-044.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-044)

Gas pressurization of shale samples in an NMR-transparent cell remobilizes viscous hydrocarbons, revealing a fraction invisible to NMR at ambient conditions and informing huff-n-puff interpretation.

Comparing laboratory and wireline NMR in organic-rich shales is complicated by temperature, system response and gas loss, so part of the hydrocarbons relaxes too fast to be seen. Four shale samples (two preserved, TOC 4.9-7.2 wt%) were measured at 2 MHz with 114 µs echo spacing at 31°C, and pressurized with NMR-transparent gases (CO2, N2, deuterated methane) in a ZrO2 cell up to 10,000 psi. NMR response of bulk oils correlated with the <C17 fraction and Rock-Eval S1. With CO2 pressurization, T2 spectra shifted from 0.1-1 ms to 1-10 ms, and detected HC volume rose by up to 25 vol% at 5000 psi, showing an invisible fraction up to about 20% of extractable HCs. Depressurization showed hysteresis consistent with gas trapping. A four-cycle CO2 huff-n-puff test showed non-movable fluid unchanged while movable matrix fluid decreased and expelled fluid increased in balance.

## Automated Determination of Formation Porosity from Drill Cuttings using Nuclear Magnetic Resonance

- ID: SCA2019-054, year: 2019
- Authors: Joel BILLIOTTE, Mahdi Ammar, Karim Bondabou, Jerome Breviere
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: drill cuttings, NMR T2 relaxation, porosity, compact NMR spectrometer, wellsite measurement, limestone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-054.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-054)

An automated T2-based procedure removes external fluid contributions in NMR measurements on drill cuttings, giving porosity within 1 point of reference on 25 limestone cuttings samples.

Porosity from drill cuttings is a cheap early petrophysical input, but removing excess fluid from the cuttings surface is difficult and operator dependent. The authors use a compact 20-MHz NMR spectrometer, with measurements under three minutes on about 2 g of rock. They propose automated analysis of the T2 distribution to remove the external fluid contribution, giving a first-order porosity correction. They also run repeated NMR measurements during sample preparation to estimate the fluid mass saturating the pore space. Tests used limestone blocks of varied texture, with cuttings produced on a drilling bench under in-situ conditions. On 25 cuttings samples with porosity of 8 to 24%, the porosity error was under 1 point against reference measurements, making the result independent of the preparation process. Automation with a custom NMR instrument is discussed.

## Reducing Sample Heating During NMR Measurements

- ID: SCA2019-061, year: 2019
- Authors: M. J. Dick, D. Veselinovic, D. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: CPMG pulse sequence, NMR sample heating, logarithmic echo spacing, NMR porosity, T2 distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-061.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-061)

Logarithmically spaced CPMG pulse trains reduce NMR sample heating, avoiding roughly 10% porosity errors while still reproducing the standard measurements accurately.

Standard linearly spaced CPMG pulse trains use many pulses at high duty cycle, which heats samples and lowers the NMR signal. This heating can change retrieved NMR porosity by about 10% in bulk fluids and core samples. The authors also examined the effects of fluid salinity and NMR field strength on heating. They propose and implement logarithmically spaced CPMG sequences that keep closely spaced early pulses for small pores but thin out later pulses, cutting the total pulse count. They report that these sequences reproduce the NMR measurements accurately for all samples while eliminating heating.

## Simulation and Experimental Measurements of Internal Magnetic Field Gradients and NMR Transverse Relaxation Times (T2) in Sandstone Rocks

- ID: SCA2019-082, year: 2019
- Authors: Paul R. J. Connolly, Weichao Yan, Daniel Zhang, Mohammed Mahmoud, Michael Verrall, Maxim Lebedev, Stefan Iglauer, Peter J. Metaxas, Eric F. May, Michael L. Johns
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: internal magnetic field gradients, NMR T2 relaxation, magnetic susceptibility, finite element simulation, random walk simulation, SQUID magnetometer
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-082.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-082)

FEM simulation of internal magnetic field gradients on CT images of five sandstones, with random-walk T2 simulation, is compared to measurements; gradients are underpredicted at the high end, while T2 agrees.

Internal magnetic field gradients caused by susceptibility contrast between rock matrix and pore fluid can distort NMR T2 measurements. The authors ran finite element simulations of these gradients on 3D digital CT images of five sandstones, coupled with a random walk simulation of T2 relaxation. Rock magnetic susceptibility was measured with a SQUID magnetometer over a range of field strengths. Simulated gradient probability distributions were generally in reasonable agreement with experiment but missed the larger gradients observed. The assumption of a single mean magnetic susceptibility was identified as the main source of the discrepancy. Simulated 2 MHz T2 relaxation agreed well with measurements across all five sandstones.

## Laboratory core analysis of potentially in-situ recovery amenable sandstone-hosted uranium deposits in the Morrison Formation/New Mexico/USA

- ID: SCA2019-084, year: 2019
- Authors: Micha Janosch Zauner, Matthias Halisch, Andreas Weller
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: in-situ recovery, sandstone-hosted uranium, spectral induced polarization, NMR bound water, permeability anisotropy, µCT flow simulation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-084.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-084)

Multi-method petrophysical and mineralogical analysis of Morrison Formation sandstone cores shows mineralized samples have more bound water and 3:1 permeability anisotropy, favoring horizontal flow for uranium in-situ recovery.

Core plugs from a ~1,000 m borehole through the Westwater Canyon Member (Morrison Formation, New Mexico) were evaluated for amenability to uranium in-situ recovery. Mineralogy and geochemistry were measured by ICP-MS, XRF, XRD, MLA and microscopy, and petrophysics by He pycnometry, SIP, N2 adsorption, NMR, µCT, permeametry and digital MICP simulation. Uranium occurs mainly as secondary coatings on grains of arkosic sandstone, with some pore fill. Mineralized samples show doubled clay- and capillary-bound water in NMR, lower free-fluid porosity, higher chargeability and resistivity, and greater specific surface area. They also show 3:1 horizontal-to-vertical permeability anisotropy and higher tortuosity, whereas barren samples are isotropic with higher porosity and permeability. µCT flow simulations and column leach tests indicate that lixiviant contact with the uranium coating is favored in horizontal flow.

## NMR Core Analysis On Whole Core Samples

- ID: SCA2021-003, year: 2021
- Authors: M. J. Dick, D. Veselinovic, R. Krumm, R. Antle, D. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: whole core NMR, field of view calibration, T1-T2 maps, out-of-volume suppression, saturation profile, NMR porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2021/SCA2021-003.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2021-003)

Presents a calibration correction for bulk NMR T2 and T1-T2 on whole core longer than the probe field of view, plus out-of-volume suppression for saturation profiles.

Whole-core NMR is hampered by signal folding in from outside the probe's field of view, which overestimates porosity and distorts profiles. The authors used an 8 MHz Oxford spectrometer with a 100 mm probe, mapping the field of view (~14 cm) with a CuSO4 pancake sample and deriving a correction factor of 1.034 from a 62 cm long fluid-filled tube. Applied to a 40 cm whole core, T2 porosity was 11.52 p.u. versus 11.94 p.u. for a 10 cm piece from the same core, and T1-T2 porosities were 12.07 and 12.53 p.u. with nearly identical maps. The calibration needs homogeneous samples and fails for imaging, so a slice-selective adiabatic inversion out-of-volume suppression sequence was used to obtain a valid saturation profile over an 11.2 cm region of interest. Extension to 2D/3D imaging and slice-selective bulk measurements is planned.

## Monitoring fluid migration using in-situ nuclear magnetic resonance core flooding system integrated with fiber optic sensors: A proof of concept

- ID: SCA2021-015, year: 2021
- Authors: Bruno da Silva Falcão, Lionel Esteban, Ausama Giwelli, Yevhen Kovalyshen, Stephen Banks, Ahmed Al-Yaseri, Alireza Keshavarz, Stefan Iglauer
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: fiber Bragg grating, NMR core flooding, strain measurement, fluid front tracking, Savonnières limestone, pore pressure
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2021/SCA2021-015.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2021-015)

Eight fiber Bragg grating strain sensors were integrated into an NMR core flooding cell, tracking brine fronts in Savonnières limestone faster than NMR and revealing structure-dependent strain.

Conventional strain sensors cannot be used inside NMR core flooding cells, so spatially resolved mechanical response during fluid migration has been poorly characterized. The authors modified an NMR cell to carry an optical fiber with 8 FBG sensors glued spirally onto a heterogeneous Savonnières limestone plug containing a tight bedding layer. Under 1000 psi confining pressure at 22 °C, doped brine was injected at 0.1 cc/min, first with open outlet and then with 200 psi backpressure. Strain decreased with confining pressure (about -250 to -350 µε at 1000 psi). The brine front increased strain by 2–3 µε at each sensor, in agreement with NMR saturation profiles, but FBG sampled at 1000 Hz versus about 2 min per NMR profile. Backpressure raised strain changes to 30–50 µε, with sensors facing high-porosity zones (4, 5) responding most and those near the bedding (2, 3) least.

## Study of Bakken and Three Forks Formations using NMR Core Analysis

- ID: SCA2021-030, year: 2021
- Authors: Mohamed M. Awad, Philip M. Singer, Z. Harry Xie, Maura Puerto, George Hirasaki
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR wettability index, T1-T2 maps, surface relaxation mechanism, internal gradients, bitumen, Bakken
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2021/SCA2021-030.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2021-030)

NMR T1-T2 at 2.3 and 22 MHz on uncleaned Bakken and Three Forks cores shows wettability trends, bitumen, relaxation mechanisms and internal gradients at 22 MHz.

The paper uses NMR T1-T2 measurements at 2.3 MHz and 22 MHz on as-received, octane-saturated and brine-saturated Bakken and Three Forks twin cores, without cleaning, to assess wettability and relaxation physics. Wettability indices from relaxivity ratios are about -0.94 for the Upper Bakken (strongly oil-wet; brine saturation only 0.24), +0.77 for the Middle Bakken and +0.97 for the Three Forks. Bitumen in the Upper Bakken appears at 22 MHz as signal at T2 < 0.1 ms with T1/T2 near 100. Temperature dependence of T2 (25 to 100 °C) indicates enhanced 1H-1H dipole-dipole relaxation in the Upper Bakken and octane-saturated Middle Bakken, and paramagnetic relaxation in the Three Forks and brine-saturated Middle Bakken. No internal gradients appear at 2.3 MHz, but echo-spacing and frequency tests show strong gradients at 22 MHz in octane-saturated Middle Bakken and Three Forks cores.

## A Bayesian optimization approach to the extraction of intrinsic physical parameters from $T_2$ relaxation responses

- ID: SCA2022-12, year: 2022
- Authors: Rupeng Li, Igor Shikhov, Christoph H. Arns
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: Bayesian optimization, T2 relaxation, surface relaxivity temperature dependence, random walk NMR simulation, Gaussian process, Bentheimer sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-12.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-12)

A concurrent Bayesian optimization fits simulated and measured T2 distributions of Bentheimer sandstone at 20 and 60°C, finding quartz surface relaxivity constant or decreasing, not increasing, with temperature.

Extracting intrinsic NMR parameters from T2 distributions is hard because multiple factors are entangled and simulations are expensive. The authors adapt a Bayesian optimization workflow (CO-OPT, vector-valued Gaussian process, expected improvement, multi-modal search) to jointly fit micro-CT random-walk T2 simulations to CPMG measurements on Bentheimer sandstone at 20°C and 60°C. Five parameters were sought: quartz surface relaxivity and clay effective T2 at each temperature, plus a shared clay tortuosity, within a budget of 200 simulations. Two physically valid solution groups emerged: quartz relaxivity decreasing about 15% (11.6 to 9.8 µm/s) or nearly constant (12.0 to 12.1 µm/s), while clay effective relaxation time fell 30–40%. Faster relaxation at higher temperature therefore does not require higher quartz relaxivity, though tortuosity remains the least certain parameter and fit quality cannot resolve the non-uniqueness.

## Shale Characterization Using Magnetic Resonance

- ID: SCA2022-13, year: 2022
- Authors: Mohammad Sadegh Zamiri, Jiangfeng Guo, Florea Marica, Laura Romero-Zerón, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: T1-T2* relaxation correlation, Look-Locker, SPRITE imaging, shale kerogen quantification, water uptake wettability, step pyrolysis
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-13)

T1-T2* and Look-Locker T1*-T2* MR correlations resolve and quantify kerogen, oil and water in shales at high and low field, and SPRITE imaging shows bedding-controlled fluid distribution.

Shale characterization is hampered by short-lived MR signals, overlapping kerogen, oil and water components, and low sensitivity at low field. The authors used T1-T2* correlation (FID-based, microsecond resolution), Look-Locker T1*-T2*, and centric-scan SPRITE imaging with magnetization preparation on outcrop Eagle Ford, Marcellus and Barnett shales at 0.2, 2.4 and 4.7 T. T1-T2* resolved all three species at both fields where T1-T2 (CPMG) peaks overlapped or missed kerogen. Calibration with adsorption/desorption and evaporation gave 0.24 g water and 0.98 g oil per 100 g rock for one sample, and step pyrolysis tied the short-T2* signal to kerogen (1.05 mol H/100 g rock). Look-Locker cut measurement time (e.g., 1 min vs 30 min at high field; 17 min vs 2.5 h at low field) with similar fluid quantities, and imaging plus water uptake showed alternating oil- and water-rich laminae with imbibition following bedding, indicating wettability control.

## Numerical study of NMR relaxation responses in synthetic clayey sandstone by dual-scale modeling

- ID: SCA2022-17, year: 2022
- Authors: Yingzhi Cui, Igor Shikhov, Christoph Arns
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: diffusional coupling, NMR T2 relaxation, random walk simulation, kaolinite clay morphology, SDR and Coates permeability, T2 cutoff
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-17.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-17)

Dual-scale random-walk NMR simulations on synthetic kaolinite-bearing Bentheimer models show diffusional coupling is strongest for coated and pore-lining clay and at high temperature, biasing SDR and Coates permeability prefactors.

Diffusional coupling between micro- and macro-pores can violate the fast-diffusion assumption behind NMR pore-size and permeability interpretation. The authors built synthetic clayey sandstones from a 2.2 µm Bentheimer micro-CT image, with 6, 11 and 16 vol% kaolinite in pore-filling, pore-lining, coated and laminated distributions. Clay pockets were replaced by resolved kaolinite models (22 nm voxels, porosity 0.3-0.6) and NMR was simulated with a dual-scale random walk at 2 MHz, tracking magnetization exchange via Euclidean distance maps. Coated and pore-lining clay gave the strongest coupling (coated about five times more exchange than other types), exchange peaked at 10-30 ms and decayed exponentially with distance, and exchange rose with temperature (about 1.3-1.4 times from 25 to 95 °C). SDR prefactors rose by up to about 1.6 times with temperature for 16 vol% pore-lining clay, and T2 cutoff fell (42 to 30 ms for pore-lining, 74 to 65 ms for pore-filling). Coates prefactors declined with temperature above 10 vol% clay, so fixed T2 cutoffs would overestimate permeability.

## A Combinational NMR and Dielectric Technique Using Spectral NMR Mapped Distributions of Dielectric Relaxation

- ID: SCA2022-26, year: 2022
- Authors: James Funk, Michael Myers, Lori Hathon
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: dielectric dispersion, Havriliak-Negami model, NMR T2 distribution, Maxwell-Wagner relaxation, formation factor, carbonate vugs
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-26)

Dielectric dispersion (1–300 MHz) of carbonates is split into matrix and vug components and spectrally mapped onto NMR T2 distributions, correlating with formation factor and diffusion.

The paper links dielectric relaxation and NMR relaxation in vuggy Michigan carbonates, where dielectric response is usually treated only as rock texture. Eleven samples were micro-CT scanned, brine saturated at various salinities and imbibition stages, and measured with 1–300 MHz impedance dielectric spectroscopy and 2 and 23 MHz NMR (T2, T1/T2, variable echo spacing). Dispersion curves were fit with the Havriliak-Negami model to a matrix and vug pore combination (HN tau 5.47e-9 s and 1.93e-10 s). The matrix fraction was mapped onto the T2 distribution with a Debye-shielding-type exponential weighting parameter, τPCM, which correlates with formation factor (from the 10 MHz conductivity) and holds for partially saturated imbibition states. Matrix fractions ranged about 0.37–0.70, and the mapping combined with variable-tau NMR gave internal gradients (about 68 G/cm for the sample and vugs, 120 G/cm for the matrix). T1/T2 maps at 23 MHz showed a T2-independent long-T1 vug peak.

## Modification of the SDR equation for permeability prediction

- ID: SCA2022-45, year: 2022
- Authors: Andreas Weller, Zeyu Zhang
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: SDR equation, NMR permeability prediction, surface relaxivity, effective hydraulic radius, harmonic mean pore radius, sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-45.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-45)

Replacing the characteristic T2 in the SDR equation with a harmonic-mean pore radius from sample-specific surface relaxivity improves NMR permeability prediction in three sandstone sets.

The study tests original and modified SDR equations for NMR-based permeability prediction on 58 sandstone plugs from the Shahejie (China), Araba and Nubia (Egypt) formations, with permeability spanning four orders of magnitude. Sample-specific surface relaxivity, derived from nitrogen-adsorption S_por and T2hm, converts relaxation times into pore radii. The original SDR equation with log-mean T2 and porosity^4 gives an average log deviation d of 0.82 with a uniform prefactor and 0.41 with formation-specific prefactors, which vary over two orders of magnitude with surface relaxivity. Using the weighted harmonic mean pore radius r_hm with porosity exponent 2 gives the lowest deviation (d = 0.211 for all samples, ≤0.25 per formation) with prefactors of 0.65–1.03. r_hm proves a reliable proxy for the effective hydraulic radius (R² > 0.79, factor about 0.53), though it needs S_por for each sample.

## A joint workflow towards a reliable quantification and understanding of NMR surface relaxivity

- ID: SCA2022-49, year: 2022
- Authors: Matthias Halisch, Raphael Dlugosch, Zeyu Zhang, Andreas Weller
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: NMR surface relaxivity, T2 relaxation, pore size distribution, nitrogen adsorption, sandstone mineralogy, 3-D imaging
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-49)

Presents a combined petrophysical, mineralogical and 2-D/3-D imaging workflow to obtain reliable sample-specific NMR surface relaxivity, tested on about a dozen sandstones and compared with conventional approaches.

NMR T2 spectra need a surface relaxivity value to be converted into pore size distributions. This value is often estimated from nitrogen adsorption, which tends to fail in mineralogically and texturally complex rocks, such as those rich in clay or Fe-oxides/hydroxides. The authors present a joint workflow combining petrophysical and mineralogical methods with 2-D and 3-D imaging to derive individual surface relaxivity values for core samples. It was tested on about a dozen sandstones spanning a wide range of mineralogical and petrophysical complexity. The abstract states the results are compared with conventional approaches, but gives no numerical results.

## NMR T2 Response versus Roughness: A Numerical and Analytical Study

- ID: SCA2022-53, year: 2022
- Authors: Yiteng Li, Xupeng He, Marwa AlSinan, Hyung Kwak, Hussein Hoteit
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, surface roughness, random walk simulation, upscaling factor, Latin hypercube sampling, machine learning
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-53.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-53)

Builds a machine-learning upscaling model that predicts NMR T2 responses of rough pores from smooth-pore responses, giving roughly thousand-fold speedup over direct random-walk simulation.

Surface roughness alters NMR T2 responses, and earlier approaches that fold roughness into surface relaxivity can violate the fast-diffusion assumption. The authors generate synthetic 3D rough pore structures using Latin hypercube sampling over relative roughness, roughness density and shape factor. Random-walk simulations and T2 inversion give the T2 responses of rough pores, and effective radii of smooth pores are found that reproduce them. A machine-learning "value-to-value" model maps the roughness parameters to an upscaling factor. Validation against direct simulation shows the model estimates rough-pore T2 from smooth-pore T2 with about three orders of magnitude speedup. The generated roughness must be both diverse and representative to keep the trained model reliable.

## NMR Spin-spin Relaxation in Unconventional Source Rocks

- ID: SCA2022-55, year: 2022
- Authors: Z. Harry Xie
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, CPMG, inter-echo spacing, shale, non-exponential decay, free induction decay
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-55.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-55)

Reviews CPMG validity conditions and shows with shale T2 distributions at varying TE that spin-spin relaxation in source rocks is non-exponential, so shortening TE alone does not fix it.

CPMG T2 measurement assumes the intrinsic T2 decay is much slower than the free induction decay T2*, which holds for less restricted liquids. In unconventional tight rocks, relaxation occurs at sub-millisecond rates, challenging this assumption. The paper reviews the general conditions for using CPMG and presents T2 distributions from shale samples measured at various inter-echo spacings (TE). These show the non-exponential nature of spin-spin relaxation in source rocks, and simply shortening TE does not resolve the problem. Early-time signals from rigid solids in the source rocks further support the non-exponential behavior, which should improve NMR interpretation for logging and core analysis.

## Development and testing of a new 10000 PSI NMR overburden probe

- ID: SCA2022-61, year: 2022
- Authors: Michael Dick, Dragan Veselinovic, Taylor Kenney, Derrick Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR overburden probe, confining pressure, magnetic susceptibility, eddy currents, signal-to-noise ratio, NMR background signal
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-61.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-61)

A redesigned NMR overburden probe removes background signal, reaches 10,000 psi confining pressure, and improves signal-to-noise about tenfold by moving from 2 to 12 MHz.

Existing NMR-compatible overburden cells are limited in pressure, show background signal from the vessel, or lack adequate safety. The authors extend their earlier Oxford Instruments P5 cell design (limited to under 5000 psi, with significant cell background). They changed internal components to NMR-invisible materials and replaced the secondary containment with metal. Metal grades were screened for magnetic susceptibility and eddy currents from magnetic field switching. Pressure seals were upgraded to allow 10,000 psi confining pressure, and the operating frequency was raised from 2 to 12 MHz, improving SNR by roughly 10X.

## Bulk saturation measurement of water and oil in porous media using $^{13}\text{C}$ and $^1\text{H}$ magnetic resonance

- ID: SCA2023-005, year: 2023
- Authors: Naser Ansaribaranghar, Mohammad Sadegh Zamiri, Laura Romero-Zerón, Florea Marica, Andrés Ramírez Aguilera, Derrick Green, Benjamin Nicot, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 13C magnetic resonance, water saturation, Dean-Stark extraction, CPMG, variable field MR, Bentheimer sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-005.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-005)

Natural-abundance 13C MR quantifies oil volume in core plugs; combined with 1H MR or gravimetric pore volume it gives water saturation agreeing with Dean-Stark.

Dean-Stark water saturation measurement is slow, hazardous and error prone. The authors use 13C CPMG MR, which detects only hydrocarbon, to get oil volume, and combine it with gravimetric pore volume or 1H CPMG (at 33.7 and 8.5 MHz) to derive water volume and saturation. Tests used Bentheimer and Berea plugs saturated with brine and decane or viscosity standard oils, calibrated against reference fluid samples. MR water saturations agreed with Dean-Stark within about ±3% saturation, with Dean-Stark generally slightly lower, attributed to water loss, while 13C short-lifetime components may bias oil volume low. MR takes 1-3 hours, is non-destructive, and a variable field magnet allows both nuclei with one probe.

## Direct Hydrocarbon Saturation Imaging in Porous Media with $^{13}\text{C}$

- ID: SCA2023-018, year: 2023
- Authors: Naser Ansaribaranghar, Mohammad Sadegh Zamiri, Laura Romero-Zerón, Florea Marica, Andrés Ramírez Aguilera, Derrick Green, Benjamin Nicot, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 13C MRI, Hybrid SE-SPI, oil saturation profiles, capillary end effect, wettability alteration, coreflooding
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-018.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-018)

Natural-abundance 13C 1D MRI directly images oil saturation in sandstone cores without dopants, revealing capillary end effects in oil-wet samples and matching Dean-Stark saturations.

Conventional 1H relaxation-based MRI and doped X-ray CT struggle to separate oil and brine, so the authors use natural-abundance 13C Hybrid SE-SPI to image only the hydrocarbon phase. Experiments used water-wet Bentheimer and Berea sandstones and AquaSil-treated oil-wet Bentheimer, with S6 and S20 viscosity-standard oils and 2.1 wt% NaCl brine, in static end-point and continuous drainage floods at a 3.1 T variable-field magnet. In water-wet Bentheimer after 10 PV brine, residual oil was uniform at 27% (CPMG 26.1%) with no end effect, whereas the treated oil-wet sample showed 39% oil at the inlet half and 62% at the outlet, a clear capillary end effect. 1H T2 distributions and profiles could not distinguish the phases in these samples. A validation gave 81.6% average oil saturation by 13C MRI, 77.7% by CPMG, and 81.9±0.7% by Dean-Stark.

## Delineating Light Hydrocarbon Configuration in Source Rocks from NMR Relaxation Mechanism of Nano-Confined Fluids

- ID: SCA2023-022, year: 2023
- Authors: Jin-Hong Chen, Stacey Althaus, Chao Liu, Mohammed Boudjatit
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 correlation, kerogen dipolar coupling, cross-relaxation, nanoconfined fluids, source rock shale, MCM-41
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-022.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-022)

Nanoconfinement raises fluid-solid dipolar cross-relaxation about 100-fold, yet far too little to explain large T1/T2 peaks in source rocks, which are instead attributed to hydrocarbons absorbed in kerogen.

Source-rock 2D T1-T2 spectra show peaks with large T1/T2 ratios, previously attributed to nanoconfined fluids coupled to kerogen by dipolar cross-relaxation, which would need about 10^6 s-1. The authors measured 1H NMR of isolated kerogen at 500 MHz, finding a Gaussian component of 13.8 kHz linewidth (T2 about 23 µs), so kerogen is invisible to low-field CPMG with first echoes near 100 µs. Cross-relaxation between methanol-OD and methyl-functionalized MCM-41 (2.57 nm pores) was measured by selective NOE, giving R_BA of -0.10 ± 0.04 s-1, which matched a theoretical 2D confined-diffusion model. Confinement increases the rate from about 10^-3 s-1 in bulk to about 10^-1 s-1, still roughly seven orders of magnitude too small. The authors therefore propose that the large T1/T2 peaks arise from light hydrocarbons absorbed in the kerogen matrix with restricted mobility, which could allow their quantification.

## Pore Size Measurement in Core Plugs with Magnetic Resonance based on Brownstein-Tarr Relaxation Theory

- ID: SCA2023-028, year: 2023
- Authors: Peiyuan Yan, Florin Marica, Benjamin Nicot, Derrick Green, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: Brownstein-Tarr theory, surface relaxivity, NMR T2 relaxation, intermediate diffusion regime, non-ground modes, pore size distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-028.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-028)

Using temperature-dependent T2 shifts and Brownstein-Tarr intermediate-regime theory, the authors estimate dominant pore size and surface relaxivity of sandstones, validated with glass microcapillaries and imaging.

The fast-diffusion assumption commonly used to convert NMR T2 to pore size fails when relaxation is in the Brownstein-Tarr intermediate regime. Glass microslides and a microcapillary of known size (30-100 µm) showed non-ground modes and surface relaxivities of about 68-76 µm/s, confirming the theory. For Berea, Buff Berea and Nugget sandstones, T2 was measured at 5-45 °C and the ground-mode peak shift was fitted with planar, cylindrical and spherical models. Dominant pore sizes (e.g., Berea 20-26 µm) agreed with SEM (26 µm) and µCT (22 µm), with BT numbers as low as 0.17 showing intermediate regime. Nugget had the highest relaxivity (about 101-176 µm/s depending on geometry), and subtracting the first non-ground mode of large pores allowed conversion of T2 to a pore size distribution.

## Investigation of temperature characteristics of secondary organic matter and hydrocarbons in the early Jurassic Marrat formation using laboratory NMR techniques

- ID: SCA2023-033, year: 2023
- Authors: Phil Hawley, Abdul Mohsen Al Mershed, Mihira Narayan Acharya, Nilotpaul Neog, Matthias Appel, Seán Dolan
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T2 relaxation, pyrobitumen, Arrhenius activation energy, T1-T2 map, variable temperature NMR, carbonate
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-033.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-033)

Variable-temperature 2 MHz and 23 MHz NMR on Marrat dolomite with pyrobitumen shows bitumen mobilizes with heating, and T2 of bitumen and oil follows Arrhenius behavior.

Pyrobitumen partly occludes porosity in Early Jurassic Marrat dolomite from Kuwait and complicates core analysis and NMR log interpretation. Chunk and crushed samples were measured by NMR at 23 MHz (TE 0.07 ms, T1-T2 maps) and 2 MHz (TE 0.2 ms, T2) from ambient to about 135°C. At 23 MHz a solid-like component with T2 < 0.1 ms was seen at ambient temperature, with porosities of 3.9 PU (SA) and 9.6 PU (RA). At 2 MHz this component was missed, but the short-T2 signal grew with temperature as the organic matter mobilized. Three-Gaussian fits of the LF T2 spectra (shortest peak fixed at 0.54 ms) gave temperature-dependent bitumen and pore-oil peaks following Arrhenius behavior, with activation energies of 6.412 and 3.881 kcal/mol.

## Pushing the boundary of traditional 2 MHz NMR T1-T2 maps for source rock characterization

- ID: SCA2023-041, year: 2023
- Authors: Esteban Domené, Gabriela Vila, Paula Bedini, Emilia Silletta, Manuel Velasco, Yamila Garro Linck, Belén Franzoni, Gustavo Monti, Rodolfo Acosta
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: NMR T1-T2 maps, SR-FID-ECHO-CPMG, source rock, total organic carbon, clay-bound water, Vaca Muerta
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-041.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-041)

A SR-FID-ECHO-CPMG sequence on a 2 MHz benchtop NMR yields T1-T2 maps of Vaca Muerta shales, with zones correlating to TOC and clay content.

Standard 2 MHz CPMG measurements miss solid-like organic matter and clay-associated protons in source rocks. The authors applied a combined saturation-recovery FID-echo-CPMG sequence on an Oxford Geospec2 (2.27 MHz) to seven as-received Vaca Muerta outcrop samples (50-80 g) with TOC of 2-10 wt% and clay content of 14-32%. The resulting T1-T2 maps were divided into eight zones and integrated. The short-T2 zone B correlates linearly with Rock-Eval TOC (R2 = 0.877). Zone E correlates with XRD clay content (R2 = 0.953), whereas zone C does not (R2 = 0.130), and zone A may reflect hydroxyls in clay and kerogen. Clay typing was not attempted because all samples contained over 84% illite/smectite.

## A complex Carbonate Characterization by Digital Rock Physics and NMR Methods during Centrifuge Desaturation

- ID: SCA2023-049, year: 2023
- Authors: Jun Gao, Hyung Kwak
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, NMR T2 relaxation, centrifuge desaturation, micro-CT deep learning, carbonate microporosity, saturation profile
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-049.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-049)

Four complex carbonates were monitored with microCT and NMR during stepwise centrifuge desaturation; saturation profiles agree, but T2 agreement is worse in micro-porous, low-permeability samples.

The study applies deep-learning-accelerated micro-CT (digital rock physics) and NMR to monitor saturation evolution in complex carbonates during centrifuge desaturation. Four core samples with different porosity, permeability and micro/macro pore systems were brine saturated and desaturated stepwise to irreducible water saturation.
