Micro-CT scans, at two quality levels with shorter scans between steps, were combined with NMR T2 distributions, saturation profiles and spatial T2 at each step. Saturation profiles from both methods agreed very well. T2 distributions agreed better for high-permeability, macro-pore-dominated samples than for low-permeability, micro-pore-dominated ones, likely because of unresolved microporosity. Both methods showed core-scale heterogeneity.

## Comparing Two Different NMR Based Porosity Measurements of Drill Cuttings

- ID: SCA2023-059, year: 2023
- Authors: M.J. Dick, D. Veselinovic, S. M. Althaus, J-H. Chen, D. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: drill cuttings, NMR T2 distribution, porosity, surface fluid, D2O rinsing, T2 cutoff
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-059.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-059)

Compares two NMR methods, a T2 cutoff approach and a centrifuge/D2O rinse approach, for measuring drill cuttings porosity while excluding surface fluid.

NMR porosity of drill cuttings is biased by surface fluid, which can be mistaken for pore volume. Two methods were developed to separate pore fluid from surface fluid. The Aramco method applies a T2 cutoff and combines NMR with mass measurements. The GIT method removes surface fluid by centrifugation and D2O rinsing to measure pore volume directly, then uses bulk volume from two further T2 measurements. Both methods are applied independently to the same set of cuttings and the resulting porosities are compared. The text provided gives no quantitative results.

## Modeling Surface Roughness Effor Porous Media: From Pore to Core Scale

- ID: SCA2024_1019, year: 2024
- Authors: Yiteng Li, Xupeng He, Hyung Kwak, Hussein Hoteit
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: surface roughness, NMR T2 relaxation, random walk simulation, spherical harmonics, evolutionary clustering, digital rock
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1019.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1019)

Pore-scale 3D surface roughness from segmented micro-CT pores is upscaled by evolutionary clustering and used to correct random-walk NMR T2 simulations, matching pore network modeling pore sizes.

Surface roughness is usually ignored in NMR T2 interpretation, which leads to underestimated pore sizes. The authors segment the pore space of a digital Berea sandstone with the SNOW algorithm and a skeleton breakup step. They reconstruct each pore surface with spherical harmonics (degree 40) and build a reference surface at degree 6, and compute a roughness coefficient per pore. Upscaling is posed as a clustering problem and four algorithms are compared: k-means, DBSCAN, local gravitation and evolutionary clustering. DBSCAN over-partitions the data, while evolutionary clustering works slightly better than the others and needs no case-specific prior knowledge; its two cluster centroids give roughness coefficients of 0.3694 and 0.4536. Using the inverse exponential of these coefficients as a correction factor in random walk simulation gives pore size distributions consistent with pore network modeling, whereas ignoring roughness underestimates pore size.

## Discrete Inversion NMR (DI-NMR): An NMR data processing method for Accurate Fluid Typing and Saturation Determination

- ID: SCA2024_1024, year: 2024
- Authors: Jun Gao, Mohammed Boudjatit, Gabor Hursan, Geir Humborstad Sorland, Hyung Tae Kwak
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: discrete inversion NMR, Anahess, inverse Laplace transform, T1-T2 fluid typing, shale saturation, 2D NMR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1024.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1024)

Applies Anahess-based discrete inversion of NMR decay data to synthetic, bulk-fluid and shale samples, giving accurate component amplitudes, relaxation times and direct fluid typing without ILT partitioning.

ILT-based NMR processing is ill-posed and yields overlapping continuous distributions that need subjective partitioning for fluid typing. The authors apply a discrete inversion (Anahess, DI-NMR) that fits the raw decay with a small number of discrete exponential components, and compare it with two ILT versions on 10%-noise synthetic data, a brine/OBM filtrate mixture (T2, T1-T2, T2-D at 2 MHz) and a preserved shale (T1-T2 at 12 MHz). For the synthetic three-peak sample, DI-NMR recovered volumes of 1.9, 1.4 and 2.9 ml (total 6.2 ml, equal to input) and relaxation times within about 2%, whereas ILT gave total volumes of 6.6-6.9 ml with spurious peaks. ILT could not separate brine and filtrate except at very low regularization, while DI-NMR found the same three main components in 1D and 2D data. For the shale, discrete components were assigned to bitumen/heavy oil, absorbed oil, free oil, structural/absorbed water and free water (e.g. 0.77 ml free oil, 0.245 ml free water) in the NFDM workflow.

## Determination of clay-bound water in non-conventional oil reservoirs via T1 NMR measurements. A case study for the Vaca Muerta formation

- ID: SCA2024_1035, year: 2024
- Authors: Esteban A. Domené, Jorge Juarez, Carolina Bernhardt, Lourdes Vera López, Mariela Silka, María Belén Franzoni, Rodolfo H. Acosta, Diana Masiero
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: clay-bound water, T1 NMR, T1-T2 map, Vaca Muerta, shale, log-normal fitting
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1035.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1035)

Clay-bound water in Vaca Muerta shales is quantified from 1D T1 distributions fitted with two log-normal components, agreeing well with 2D T1-T2 maps, XRD and logs at half the time.

Quantifying clay-bound water (CBW) in unconventional shales normally needs time-consuming 2D T1-T2 maps, which is problematic for preserved samples that lose fluid. The authors fit 1D T1 distributions with two log-normal components to isolate the short-T1 CBW signal, using a 2.27 MHz Geospec2 on sidewall cores from three Vaca Muerta wells (15, 48 and 27 samples). In Well A, T1 CBW correlated with T1-T2 CBW (R²=0.9577) but overestimated it by 13%, possibly from short-T1 solid-like components not decayed before the first FID point. Against NMR logs in Wells B and C, differences had a standard deviation of 1.5 pu and maxima of 2.6 and 3 pu. Correlation with XRD clay content showed considerable scatter, attributed to sample heterogeneity and clay type (illite-smectite, chlorite).

## 13C Magnetic Resonance Signal Density and Relaxation Times of Crude Oils

- ID: SCA2024_1065, year: 2024
- Authors: Naser Ansaribaranghar, Mohammad Sadegh Zamiri, Laura Romero-Zerón, Florea Marica, Andres Ramirez Aguilera, Michael J. Dick, Derrick Green, Benjamin Nicot, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: 13C NMR, oil saturation, signal per volume, T1-T2 correlation, crude oil density, reference-free calibration
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1065.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1065)

13C and 1H MR signal per volume varies only ~5% across eight crude oils, so a generic reference oil can give oil saturation within 5% relative error.

Oil saturation from 13C MR normally needs a reference sample of the saturating oil. The authors measured 13C and 1H signal per volume and per mass for decane and eight crude oils (API 25-50) in vials, plus T2 and T1-T2 correlations. Signal per volume had a coefficient of variation of about 5.5% (13C) and 4.7% (1H), and both signal per volume and per mass varied linearly with density (R2 80-98%). This implies a maximum 5% relative error in saturation when a different oil is used for calibration. Five Bentheimer plugs with brine and crude oil, calibrated with dodecane signal per volume, gave 13C saturations consistent with SCAL values. 1H and 13C T1-T2 trends were similar for several oils, but decane behaved differently, and the conclusions are limited to the nine oils tested, not heavy oils.

## Fluid Quantification and Kerogen Assessment in Shales Using $^{13}\text{C}$ and $^1\text{H}$ Magnetic Resonance Measurements

- ID: SCA2024_1067, year: 2024
- Authors: Mohammad Sadegh Zamiri, Naser Ansaribaranghar, Andrés Ramírez Aguilera, Florea Marica, Bruce J. Balcom
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 13C NMR, shale kerogen, T1-T2 correlation, Van Krevelen H/C ratio, oil quantification, CPMG
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1067.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1067)

Natural-abundance 13C MR resolves and quantifies kerogen and oil in shales without water interference; combined with 1H it gives kerogen H/C ratios of 1.1 (E10) and 0.4 (H2).

Conventional fluid quantification and kerogen assessment in shales are destructive and slow, and 1H MR signals from water, oil and kerogen overlap. The authors measured natural-abundance 13C, which is detected only in oil and kerogen, on an outcrop Eagle Ford sample (E10) and a mature water-saturated sample (H2) at 33.7 MHz using CPMG and 2D T1-T2 measurements. Decane-calibrated 13C T2 distributions separated kerogen (T2 ~0.4 ms) from oil, giving kerogen of 0.24 and 0.6 mol C and oil of 1.65 and 0.77 ml for E10 and H2. Kerogen showed high T1/T2 (e.g. 711 for 13C in E10), and the T1 increase from 1H to 13C was far below the factor of 256 predicted by dipole theory, suggesting paramagnetic relaxation. Combining 1H and 13C kerogen contents gave H/C of 1.1 for E10 (partially mature class I/II) and 0.4 for H2 (mature); O/C is lacking and no comparison with conventional measurements was made.

## A Rock Catalogue Data Study for Extending NMR Fluid Substitution to Sandstone

- ID: SCA_2025_1024, year: 2025
- Authors: Holger Thern
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: NMR fluid substitution, T2 spectral cutoff, T2-saturation ratio, sandstone rock catalogue, static and changeable water, irreducible water saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2025/SCA_2025_1024.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA_2025_1024)

Extends NMR fluid substitution from chalk to sandstone using a static/changeable water spectral cutoff at one third of T2cut, validated on 352 rock catalogue sandstone plugs.

NMR fluid substitution estimates the fully water-saturated T2 distribution from a partially saturated one, but had only been developed for mono-modal water-wet chalk. Using 352 sandstone plugs from the ART rock catalogue with 2 MHz NMR T2 at Sw=1 and Swir (centrifuged), the water signal is split by a spectral cutoff into static and changeable fractions. Only the changeable fraction is shifted and scaled by 1/Sw,ch following the T2-saturation-ratio relationship, with optional peak widening. With T2cut,stat set to one third of the bound-water cutoff, T2gm after substitution correlates excellently with measured T2gm at Sw=1, and scatter is much lower than in the unmodified TSR plot. Catalogue T2cut values (P5 10 ms, P95 110 ms) imply T2cut,stat of 10–20 ms, so a 15 ms default is proposed. An automated workflow with default T2 thresholds (10 and 200 ms) is described; well log validation is future work.

## Investigating NMR T2-Cutoffs: Towards better reservoir Characterization

- ID: SCA_2025_1061, year: 2025
- Authors: Farhan Ali, Chiara Cavalleri, Mohammed Al-Hamad
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: T2 cutoff, T1/T2 ratio, NMR log calibration, centrifuge desaturation, irreducible water saturation, bound fluid volume
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2025/SCA_2025_1061.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA_2025_1061)

NMR T2 and T1-T2 measurements on 7 carbonate and 4 sandstone plugs yield T2 cutoff correlations with Sw and T1/T2, applied to logs.

T2 cutoff is normally measured on cores by centrifuge, which is slow and costly, and standard values may not hold along a reservoir column. The authors measured T2 and T1-T2 on 7 carbonate and 4 sandstone outcrop plugs at full saturation and three centrifuge desaturation steps (about 2, 15 and 100 psi). Average T2 cutoffs at the lowest saturation were 119 ms for carbonates and 26 ms for sandstones, differing from the standard 100/200 ms and 33 ms. Linear correlations of T2 cutoff vs Sw, T2 cutoff vs T1/T2 log mean, and T1/T2 vs Sw gave R2 of 0.78–0.97 for carbonates and 0.80–0.96 for sandstones. Applied to wireline NMR logs in a carbonate and a gas-bearing sandstone field example, the variable cutoffs gave bound fluid and permeability estimates consistent with core and testing data, and factor analysis independently picked 26 ms in the sandstone.

## Practical Limitations Of Low Field NMR Measurements of $^{13}\text{C}$ and $^{23}\text{Na}$

- ID: SCA_2025_1068, year: 2025
- Authors: Michael J. Dick, Dragan Veselinovic, Taylor Kenney, Derrick P. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: 13C NMR, 23Na NMR, low field NMR probe, acoustic ringing, oil-water saturation, T2 distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2025/SCA_2025_1068.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA_2025_1068)

A dual-tuned 13C/23Na NMR probe for a 0.3 T magnet was built and commissioned; acoustic ringing and Teflon background were fixed, but 13C SNR in cores remains low.

Multinuclear NMR (13C for oil, 23Na for brine) could give non-destructive oil/water saturation, but sensitivity is low at low field. The authors built a dual-tune 13C (3.25 MHz) / 23Na (3.41 MHz) probe for an existing 0.3 T magnet, accepting 1.5" x 2" cores. Acoustic ringing was reduced with copper shielding, and a persistent background was traced to 13C in a Teflon coil holder, which was replaced with ceramic. The 23Na probe was calibrated with 25% NaCl; brines of 2–25% gave observed Na masses close to expected, e.g. 10% brine reached SNR 152 in 82 s. Extrapolation suggests about 6.4 h for a 10 p.u. core half-saturated with 10% brine at SNR 80. The 13C probe was calibrated with glycerol (SNR 12.6 in 82 s), and glycerol/water bulk tests showed 13C sees only the oil phase. A decane-saturated sandstone gave a 13C T2 matching 1H but only SNR 7.3 in 30 h, attributed to an untuned circuit, so a second probe version is planned.

## HSR-NMR Scanner for High-Throughput Measurement of Fluids Distribution in Whole Cores

- ID: SCA_2025_1079, year: 2025
- Authors: Jin-Hong Chen, Mohammed Boudjatit, Ivan Deshenenkov, Arcady Reiderman
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: whole core NMR, spatial resolution deconvolution, CPMG T2 spectra, source rock fluid distribution, iterative reweighted least squares, core scanning
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2025/SCA_2025_1079.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA_2025_1079)

A 2 MHz whole-core NMR scanner steps cores through the sensor and deconvolves signals to give inch-scale fluid distribution, scanning 3 ft in about 70 minutes.

Conventional plug-based core analysis gives sparse, often destructive fluid data, and a finite-length NMR coil averages signal over about 6 inches. The authors built a 2 MHz whole-core scanner (110 mm bore, 4-inch cores) that moves the core stepwise by the target resolution and acquires CPMG data. Iterative reweighted least squares deconvolution using a measured axial response map gives inch-scale echo trains, which are then inverted for T2 spectra. Four scans were sufficient for accurate porosity even near 3 pu (range 2.8–6.1 pu). A plug cut from a whole core gave 7.5 pu versus 7.4 pu from the scan. A 36-inch composite of six shale rocks was scanned in 72 minutes, showing inch-scale heterogeneity and minimal fluid in the outcrops; the scanner would cover 200 ft of core in about three days.

## Using Artificial Intelligence to Assist in NMR Scanning

- ID: SCA2026-029, year: 2026
- Authors: Michael J. Dick, Dragan Veselinovic, Derrick P. Green
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: NMR prescans, CPMG T2 distribution, automated parameter selection, T2* free induction decay, recycle delay, SPRITE FSE imaging
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2026/SCA2026-029.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2026-029)

Fast T1, T2 and T2* prescans automatically set NMR scan parameters; resulting T2 distributions match expert-set ones within 5%, at the cost of longer acquisition time.

Setting NMR parameters (tau, recycle delay, T2 max, imaging sequence) requires many user inputs, and poor choices distort T2 distributions. The authors implement automated parameter selection using low-SNR (25) prescans: saturation recovery for T1, CPMG for T2, and FID for T2*, with rules of thumb such as recycle delay of 5×T1max and tau at the instrument minimum unless more points per echo are allowed. Tested on a doped H2O/D2O calibration sample, bulk water, a sandstone and a shale, prescan-derived T2 distributions and NMR volumes agreed with experienced-user scans within 5%, and SNR was often higher (e.g., 22328 vs 7462 for the calibration sample) due to multiple points per echo. Prescans added acquisition time (about 4 min vs 0.5 min for the calibration sample; 22 vs 8 min for bulk water). Minor discrepancies arose from a longer tau in the sandstone and a T2 max truncated at 30 ms in the shale, and the user-set parameters for bulk water were too short.
